They joined the group that surrounded him, expostulating, sober or fuddled:
“Don’t be an ass, Tom.”
“The cops are coming.”
“I say, come on home.”
“How many more has he got?”
“Get him home, you fellows.”
“Stop him.”
Meanwhile, abetted by the admiring, delighted McNab, Tom Kelly, taking the most solicitous aim, was continuing his serious efforts to hit the moon with the pool-balls which he had procured no one knew how.
“I say, McNab,” said Stover, drawing him aside, “better get him to stop now. Too many cops around. Use your influence﻿—he’ll listen to you.”
McNab’s sense of responsibility having thus become violently agitated, he wabbled up to the laboring Kelly, and the following historic dialogue took place:
“I say, Tom, old fellow, you know me, don’t you? You know I’m a good sort, don’t you﻿—one of the finest?”
“I know you, Dopey McNab; I’m proud to know you.”
“I want to speak a word with you seriously.”
“What?”
“Seriously.”
“Say on.”
“Now, seriously, Tom, do you think you can hit it?”
“Don’t know; going to try’s much as in me. Biff!”
“Hold up,” said McNab, staying his hand. “Tom, I’m going to appeal to you as man to man.”
“Appeal.”
“You understand﻿—as man to man.”
“Sure.”
“You’re a man; I’m a man.”
“The finest.”
“Now as man to man, I’m going to tell you the truth.”
“The whole truth?”
“Solemn truth.”
“Tell on.”
“You can’t hit it.”
“Why not?”
“Tom, it’s too﻿—too far away!”
The two shook hands solemnly and impressively.
“Can’t hit it﻿—too far away,” said Kelly, with the pool-ball clutched tight. “Too far away, eh?”
“My dear Tom,” said McNab, tearfully breaking the news, “it’s too far﻿—entirely too far away. You can’t reach it, Tom; believe me, as man to man﻿—you can’t, you can never, never hit it.”
“I know I can’t, Dopey,” said Kelly, in an equally mournful tone, “I know all that. All that you say is true. But, Dopey, suppose I *should* hit it, suppose I *should*, just think﻿—think﻿—how my name would go reeling and rocking down to fushure generations! Biff!”
They left McNab overcome by the impressiveness of this argument, busily gathering up the pool-balls, resolved that every opportunity should be given Kelly to rank among the immortals.
Stover would have liked to stay. For the moment, almost a rebellion swept over him at the drudgery to which he had condemned himself in his ambition.

===Chinese===

他们围拢过去，劝的劝，清醒的、晕乎的：

「别犯傻，汤姆。」

「条子来了。」

「我说，回家吧。」

「他还有多少颗？」

「你们几个送他回去。」

「拦住他。」

与此同时，在那钦佩不已、乐开了花的麦克纳布的怂恿下，汤姆·凯利瞄得无比精心，继续他那桩庄严的事业——用谁也不知他从哪儿弄来的台球击中月亮。

「我说，麦克纳布，」斯托弗把他拉到一边，「现在最好让他收手。这一带条子太多。用用你的面子——他听你的。」

麦克纳布的责任感就此被猛烈搅起，他晃晃悠悠蹭到正在苦干的凯利跟前，下面这场历史性的对话发生了：

「我说，汤姆，老伙计，你认得我，对不对？你知道我是个好样儿的，对不对——顶好那一类？」

「我认得你，多皮·麦克纳布；认得你是我的骄傲。」

「我要同你说句正经话。」

「什么？」

「正经话。」

「讲。」

「那好，正经说，汤姆，你觉得你打得中它吗？」

「不知道；有多少力气使多少力气。嘿！」

「打住，」麦克纳布按住他的手。「汤姆，我要以男人对男人的身份，向你发出呼吁。」

「呼吁吧。」

「你明白——男人对男人。」

「明白。」

「你是男人；我也是男人。」

「顶好的男人。」

「现在，男人对男人，我要告诉你真相。」

「全部真相？」

「庄严的真相。」

「讲吧。」

「你打不中它。」

「为什么？」

「汤姆，它太——太远了！」

两人庄严而郑重地握了握手。

「打不中——太远了，」凯利说，台球攋得紧紧的。「太远了，嗯？」

「亲爱的汤姆，」麦克纳布眼泪汪汪地宣布这个噩耗，「太远了——完完全全太远了。你够不着它，汤姆；信我，男人对男人——你够不着，你永远、永远打不中它。」

「我知道我打不中，多皮，」凯利用同样悲恸的声调说，「这些我全知道。你说的句句是真。可是，多皮，万一我*偏*打中了呢，万一我*偏*打中了，你想——想想——我的名字将怎样摇摇晃晃、轰轰隆隆地传到子子孙孙手里！嘿！」

他们撤下麦克纳布，他已被这论断的分量彻底折服，正忙着把台球一颗颗拾起来，铁了心要给凯利一切机会，好教他跻身不朽者的行列。

斯托弗真想留下。一时间，一近乎造反的情绪漫过他——为他那份野心给自己判下的苦役。

===Original===

He saw again the low table, through the smoke, and Buck Waters’s jovial pagan face leading the crowd in lazy, carefree abandon. He felt that liberty, that zest of life, that wild spirit of youth for which he yearned and of which he had been defrauded by Le Baron’s hand, that hand which had ruthlessly torn away the veil. Something leaped up within him﻿—a longing to break the harness, to jump the gate and go heels in the air, cavorting across unfenced meadows. He rebelled against the way that had been marked out for him. He rebelled against the self-imposed discipline, and, most of all, he rebelled against the hundred eyes under whose inspection he must now inevitably walk.
Ahead of them to the left, across by Osborne, came the gay, defiant singing of a group of upper classmen returning to the campus:

> “For it’s always fair weather

When good fellows stand together,

With a stein on the table

And a good song ringing clear.”

===Chinese===

烟雾里，他又看见那张低矮的桌案，巴克·沃特斯那张快活的异教徒的脸，正领着满屋人懒洋洋、无牵无挂地放浪。

他感到那种自由，那种生之酣畅，那种他渴慕的少年的野性——而这野性，已被勒巴龙那只手窃走了，那只无情揭开帷幕的手。

有什么东西在他体内蹿了起来——一种挣断挽具的渴望，跳过栅栏，四蹄朝天，撒着欢儿奔过没有围栏的草场。

他反抗那条替他划好的路。他反抗自己套上的纪律，尤其反抗那一百只眼睛——从今往后，他注定要在那一百只眼睛的注视底下走路。

他们左前方，奥斯本馆那一带，传来一伙返校高年级生快活而无忌的歌声：

> 「只因天公总作美，

当好汉们站成一堆，

桌上啤酒杯满，

一支好歌清亮地飞。」

===Original===

The echo came to him with a certain grim mockery. There would be very little of that for him. It was to be four years, not of pleasure and inclination, but of seriousness and restraint, if he continued in his decision. For a moment the pagan in him prevailed, and he doubted. Then they passed across High Street, and at their sides the dead shadow of the society tomb suddenly intruded upon them. Which of the group at the end of the long three years would be of the chosen? Which would lead?
“Well, fellows, we go this way,” said Bain’s methodical voice. “Drop around at the rooms soon. Good night.”
Stover, Hunter, and Bain for the moment found themselves together, each striving for the same social honor, each conscious that, whatever an established system might bring to them, with its enforced comradeship, among them would always be the underlying contending spirit of variant ambitions.
Stover felt it keenly, almost with a sharp antagonism that drove from him finally the slumbering rebellion he had felt all that night﻿—the tugging at the bridle of consciousness which had been imposed upon him. This was a bigger thing, a thing that wakened in him the great instincts of combat. He would be a leader among leaders. He would succeed as success was reckoned.
He gave a little laugh and held out his hand to Hunter.
“Good night, Jim,” he said.
“Why﻿—good night,” said Hunter, surprised at the laugh and the unnecessary handshake.
But the hand had been offered in challenge, and the laugh marked the final deliberate acceptance of all that Le Baron had logically exposed to him.
“I’ll play the game, and I’ll play it better than they will,” he said, setting his lips. “I’ve got my eyes open, and I’m not going to throw away a single chance. We’ll see who’ll lead!”

===Chinese===

这回声传到他耳朵里，带着某种冷酷的嘲弄。这样的日子，轮不到他几天。他若不改主意，这四年便不是快活随心，而是严正与克制。有一瞬，他体内的异教徒占了上风，他动摇了。随后他们穿过高街，会社墓穴那死沉沉的影子募地逼到他们身边。漫长三年之后，这一伙人里谁将位列入选者？谁将领头？

「那么，弟兄们，我们走这边，」贝恩有条不紊的嗓音说。「改日到宿舍来坐。晚安。」

斯托弗、亨特和贝恩一时同路，各自争的是同一样体面，各自心里明白：不论这套既成的制度连同它强加的伙伴情谊会给他们带来什么，他们中间永远潜伏着一股志向各异的争竞之气。

斯托弗敏锐地感到了它，几乎带着一种尖利的敌意——这一下，终于把他整晚潜伏在身的造反情绪驱走了，那股拽着他新套上的那副缰绳的劲头。这是桩更大的事，一桩唤醒他体内伟大战斗本能的事。他要当领袖中的领袖。他要按世人算成功的那份成功。

他轻轻一笑，向亨特伸出手。

「晚安，吉姆。」他说。

「呃——晚安。」亨特说，为这声笑和这多余的一握吃了一惊。

可那只手是作为挑战递出去的，那声笑，则标志着他最终从容收下了勒巴龙有条不紊摊给他的那一切。

「这场游戏我来玩，而且我要玩得比他们都漂亮，」他抿紧嘴唇说。「我睁着眼睛呢，一个机会也不会丢。走着瞧，看谁来领头！」

## VIII / 八
===Original===

The intensity and seriousness of the football season abetted Stover in his new attitude of Napoleonic seclusion by leaving him little time for the lighter side of college pleasures. Every hour was taken up with the effort of mastering his lessons, which he then regarded, in common with the majority of his class, as a laborious task, a sort of necessary evil, the price to be paid for the privilege of passing four years in pleasant places with congenial companions.
After supper he returned immediately to his rooms, where presently a succession of visiting sophomores, members of the society campaign committees, took up the first hours. These inquisitorial delegations, formal, stiff, and conducted on a basis of superior investigation, embarrassed him at first. But this feeling soon wore off with the consciousness that he was a subject of dispute; and, secure in the opportunity that would come to him with the opening of the winter-term period of elections, his interest was directed only to the probable selection among his classmates.
By the middle of October the situation at Yale field had become critical. The earlier games had demonstrated what had been foreseen﻿—the weakness and inexperience of the raw material in hand. Serious errors in policy were committed by Captain Dana, who, in the effort to find some combination which would bolster up the weak backfield, began a constant shifting of the positions in order to experiment with heavier men behind the line. A succession of minor injuries arrived to further the disorganization. The nervousness of the captain communicated itself to the team, harassed and driven in the effort for accomplishment. That there was serious opposition among the coaches to these new groping policies every man saw plainly; yet, to Stover’s amazement, the knowledge remained within the team, impregnated with the spirit of loyalty and discipline.
After three weeks of brilliancy at his natural position of end, buoyed up by the zest of confidence and success, he was abruptly called to one side.
“Stover, you’ve played behind the line, haven’t you?” said Dana.
“A couple of games at school, sir,” he answered hastily, “just as a makeshift.”
“I’m going to try you at fullback.”
“At fullback?”
“Get into it and see if you can make good.”
“Yes, sir.”

===Chinese===

橄榄球赛季的紧张与严肃，帮着斯托弗把他那套拿破仑式的离群索居坐实了——它几乎不给他留一点工夫，去沾学院乐子里轻松的那一面。每个钟头都用来啃功课。当时他同班里大多数人一样，把功课看作一桩苦差，一种少不了的坏事，是换取特权的价钱——换取在愉快的地方同投契的伙伴共度四年的特权。
晚饭后他立刻回屋。不一会儿，学生会社竞选委员会的二年级生们便一批接一批登门，占去头几个钟头。这一队队问案的使团，一板一眼、僵里僵气，摆着居高临下盘查的架势，起初叫他发窘。可这感觉很快就消了——他明白自己已是各家的争夺对象；冬学期选举期一到，机会稳稳是他的，他的兴味便只落在同学里可能被挑中的是哪几位。
到十月中旬，耶鲁球场的局势已经吃紧。前几场球坐实了大家早预见到的——手头这批生料子又弱又嫩。达纳队长在方略上犯了严重的错误：为寻一个组合来撑住薄弱的后场，他开始不停地挪换位置，试用更重的人到锋线背后。一连串轻伤接踵而至，乱上添乱。队长的焦躁传给了全队，人人被驱赶着、苦苦熬着求成。教练们对这些摸着石头过河的新法子有严重异议，谁都看得明白；然而叫斯托弗吃惊的是，这知情始终留在队里，浸在忠诚与纪律的精神里，不出门槛。
在天生位置边锋上打了三周的好球，被信心和顺利的兴头托着，他忽然被叫到一边。
「斯托弗，你在锋线背后打过球，是不是？」达纳说。
「在学校打过两场，学长，」他急忙答，「纯粹应急。」
「我要试试你打全卫。」
「全卫？」
「上吧，看你顶不顶得下来。」
「是，学长。」

===Original===

He went without spirit, sure of the impossibility of the thing, feeling only the humiliation and failure that all at once flung itself like a storm-cloud across his ambition. A coach took charge of him, running over with him the elementary principles of blocking and plunging.
When he lined up, it was with half of the coaching force at his back.
“Come on, Stover; get into it!”
“Wake up!”
“Get your head down!”
“Keep a-going!”
“Ram into it!”
“Knock that man over!”
“Knock him *over*!”
He went into the line blindly, frantically, feeling for the first time that last exhausting, lunging expenditure of strength that is called forth with the effort to fall forward when tackled. Nothing he did satisfied. It was a constant storm of criticism, behind his back, in his ears, shrieked to his face:
“Keep your feet﻿—oh, keep your feet!”
“Smash open that line!”
“Rip open that line!”
“Hit it﻿—hit it!”
“Hard﻿—harder!”
“Go on﻿—don’t stop!”
A dozen times he flung his meager weight against the ponderous bodies of the center men, crushed by the impact in front, smothered by the surging support of his own line behind, helpless in the grinding contention, turned and twisted, going down in a heap amid the shock of bodies, thinking always:
“Well, the darn fools will find out just about how much use I am here!”
When the practise ended, at last, Dana called on Tompkins.
“Joe, take Stover and give him a line on the punting, will you?”
“I say, he’s been worked pretty hard,” said the coach with a glance.
“How about it?” said Dana quickly.
“All right,” said Stover, lying gloriously. At that moment, aching in every joint, he would have given everything to have spoken his mind. Instead he brought forth a smile distinguished for its eagerness, and said, “I’d like to get right at it, sir.”
“Fullback’s the big problem,” said Tompkins, as they started across the field. “Bangs can fill in at end, but we’ve got to get a fullback that can catch punts, and with nerve enough to get off his kicks in the face of that Princeton line.”
“I’ll do my best, sir,” said Stover, with a sinking feeling.
For twenty minutes, against the rebellion of his body, he went through a rigorous lesson, improving a little in the length of his punts, and succeeding fairly well in holding the ball, which came spinning end over end to him from the region of the clouds.
“That’ll do,” said Tompkins, at last.
“That’s all?”

===Chinese===

他无精打采地去了，认定这事没有指望，只觉得屈辱和挫败，像一团乌云一下子罩过他的野心。一位教练接管了他，把阻挡和猛冲的基本功给他过了一遍。
列队的时候，半个教练班底都压在他背后。
「来，斯托弗；上！」
「醒醒！」
「把头压低！」
「别停！」
「往里凿！」
「把那个人撞翻！」
「把他撞*翻*！」
他盲目地、发疯般撞进锋线，头一回尝到那种把力气掏到见底的、前扑式的耗竭——被擒抱住时还要拼死向前栽的那种力气。他干什么都不成。批评像一场不停的风暴，背后、耳边、当面尖叫：
「站住脚——哦，站住脚！」
「把锋线砸开！」
「把锋线撕开！」
「撞——撞上去！」
「狠——再狠！」
「上——别停！」
十几次，他把自己那点单薄的分量抡向中路那几具沉重的身躯：正面被撞得粉碎，背后又被自家锋线涌上来的推挤闷住，在磨盘似的绞斗里毫无办法，翻过来拧过去，在一堆身子的冲撞里栽倒，心里永远想着：
「好啊，这帮该死的傻瓜，马上就会知道我在这儿顶个什么用！」
训练终于收了场，达纳叫汤普金斯。
「乔，把斯托弗带走，给他练练踢球，行不行？」
「我说，他今天练得够苦了，」教练瞟了一眼说。
「怎么样？」达纳立刻问。
「行，」斯托弗说，撒了个光荣的谎。那一刻他周身的骨节都在酸，本愿意拿一切换一句实话出口。可他挤出一脸急切万分的笑，说：「我正想接着练呢，学长。」
「全卫是头号难题，」两人往球场那头走时，汤普金斯说。「邦斯可以顶边锋，可我们得找一个接得住悬空球的全卫，还得有胆量对着普林斯顿那条锋线把球踢出去。」
「我尽力，学长，」斯托弗说，心往下一沉。
二十分钟里，他顶着身体的抗议，挨了一堂严课：悬空球的远近略有长进，接球也接得像样了——球从云头那一边翻着跟头旋下来，他抱得还算牢。
「行了，」汤普金斯终于说。
「就这些？」

===Original===

said Stover stoically, picking up his sweater.
“That’s all.” Tompkins, watching him for a moment, said suddenly: “Stover, I don’t know whether Dana’ll keep you at full or not, but I guess you’ll have to get ready to fill in. Come over to the gym lot every morning for about half an hour, and we’ll see if we can’t work up those punts.”
“Yes, sir.”
They walked out together.
“Stover, look here,” said Tompkins abruptly, “I’m going to speak straight to you, because I think you’ll keep your mouth shut. We’re in a desperate condition here, and you know it. There’s only one man in charge at Yale, now and always, and that’s the captain. That’s our system, and we stand or fall by it; and in order that we can follow him four times out of five to victory, we’ve got sometimes to shut our eyes and follow him down to defeat. Do you get me?”
“I think I do.”
“No matter what happens, no criticism of the captain﻿—no talking outside. You may think he’s wrong, you may know he’s wrong, but you’ve got to grin and bear it. That’s all. Remember it﻿—a close mouth!”
But it required all Stover’s newly learned stoicism to maintain this attitude in the weeks that arrived. After a week he was suddenly returned to his old position, and as suddenly redrafted to fullback when another game had displayed the inadequacy of the regular. From a position where he was familiar with all the craft of the game, Stover suddenly found himself a novice whom a handful of coaches sought desperately to develop by dint of hammering and driving. His name no longer figured in the newspaper accounts as the find of the season, but as Stover the weak spot on the eleven. It was a rude discipline, and more than once he was on the point of crying out at what seemed to him the useless sacrifice. But he held his tongue as he saw others, seniors, put to the same test and giving obedience without a word of criticism for the captain, who, as everyone realized, face to face with a hopeless outcome, was gradually going to pieces.
Meanwhile Dopey McNab was just as zealously concerned in the pursuit of his classic ideal, which, however, was imagined more along the lines of such historic scholars as Verdant Green, Harry Foker, and certain heroes of his favorite author, Charles Lever.

===Chinese===

斯托弗咬着牙问，拾起毛衫。
「就这些。」汤普金斯端详了他一会儿，忽然说：「斯托弗，我说不准达纳会不会把你钉在全卫上，可我料你得准备顶上去。每天早上到体育馆场子来半个钟头，看看咱们能不能把那脚球练出来。」
「是，学长。」
两人一同走出去。
「斯托弗，听着，」汤普金斯突兀地说，「我要同你说掏心的话，因为我看你嘴严。我们眼下的处境凶险，这你知道。耶鲁这地方，管事的永远只有一个人——就是队长。这是我们的制度，成也它，败也它；为着五次里能跟他打四次胜仗，我们就得有时闭上眼，跟着他打进败仗里去。听懂了没有？」
「我想我懂。」
「不管出什么事，不许议论队长——不许到外头说。你可以觉得他错了，你可以明知他错了，你也得咬着牙受着。就这样。记住——把嘴闭严！」
可接下来几个星期，要守住这个姿态，得用上斯托弗新学来的全部坚忍。一周后他突然被挪回老位置，又随着另一场球把正牌全卫的底细瞭了出来，突然再被征调回全卫。原本在一个熟门熟路、样样技艺在握的位置上，斯托弗一变而为生手，一小撮教练死命想靠敲打驱赶把他赶出来。报纸的战报里，他的名字不再是本季的发现，而成了十一人队里的薄弱一环。这是粗粝的磨炼，不止一回，他几乎要为这种在他看来无谓的牺牲喊出声来。可他终究闭住嘴——他看见别的同学、四年级生，受着同样的考验，对队长没半个字的怨言；而谁都明白，队长面对一场无望的结局，正在一分一分垮下去。
与此同时，多皮·麦克纳布正同样热切地追求他的古典理想——只是这理想多半是照着维尔丹特·格林（Verdant Green）、哈利·福克（Harry Foker），以及他心爱的作家查尔斯·利弗（Charles Lever）笔下几位英雄的路子想象出来的。

===Original===

The annoyance of recitations by an economical imagination he converted into periods of repose and refreshing slumber behind the broad back of McMasters, who, for a certain fixed portion of tobacco a week, agreed to act as a wall in moments of calm and to awake him with a kick on the shins when the summons to refuse to recite arrived.
Having discovered Buck Waters as a companionable soul, congenially inclined to the pagan view of life, it was not long before the two discovered the third completing genius in the person of Tom Kelly, who, though a member of the Sheff freshman class, immediately agreed not to let either time, place, or conflicting recitations stand in the way of that superior mental education which must result from the friction of three such active imaginations.
The triumvirate was established on a firm foundation on the day after Kelly’s ambitious but unsuccessful attempt to hit the moon with a pool-ball, and immediately began a series of practical jokes and larks which threatened to terminate abruptly the partnership or remove it bodily to an unimaginative outer world.
McNab, like most gentlemen of determined leisure, worked indefatigably every minute of the day. Having slept through chapel and first recitation, with an occasional interruption to rise and say with great dignity “Not prepared,” he would suddenly, about ten o’clock in the morning, awake with a start, and drifting into Stover’s room plaster his nose to the window and restlessly ask himself what mischief he could invent for the day.
After a moment of dissatisfied introspection, he would say fretfully:
“I say, Dink?”
“Hello!”
“Studying?”
“Yes.”
“Almost finished?”
“No.”
“What are you doing, McCarthy?”
“Boning out an infernal problem in spherical geometry.”
“I gave that up.”
“Oh, you did!”
“Sure, it’s too hard﻿—what’s the use of wasting time over it, then? What do you say to a game of pool?”
“Get out!”
“Let’s go for a row up on Lake Whitney.”
“Shut up!”
“Come over to Sheffield and get up a game of poker with Tom Kelly.”
At this juncture, Stover and McCarthy rising in wrath, McNab would beat a hurried retreat, dodging whatever came sailing after him. Much aggrieved, he would go down the hall, trying the different doors, which had been locked against his approach.

===Chinese===

背诵课的烦难，他凭一分节俭的想象力，化成了休憩和提神的酣睡——躲在麦克马斯特斯（McMasters）宽阔的后背后面。后者每周领一份定量的烟丝，答应太平时候充当一堵墙，等到「某某，起来背诵」的传唤落到头上，就朝他小腿上踢一脚，把他踢醒。
他又发现巴克·沃特斯是个气味相投的灵魂，同他一样倾向异教徒的人生观；不久，两人又发现了补足三巨头的第三位天才——汤姆·凯利（Tom Kelly）。此人虽是谢菲尔德理学院的一年级生，却立刻表示：时间、地点、撞车的背诵课，一概不许妨碍那场高级的精神教育——三个如此活跃的想象力相摩擦，必然结出的教育。
三巨头在这一天奠定了坚实的根基——凯利用台球击月、壮志未酬的翌日——并立即开始了一系列实际的恶作剧和胡闹，险些一下子断了合伙的情分，或叫它整个被放逐到一个没有想象力的外部世界去。
麦克纳布同大多数抱定游手好闲的绅士一样，一天里每一分钟都在不倦地忙。睡过礼拜堂和第一堂背诵课——偶尔被打断，站起来极有尊严地说一声「没准备」——上午十点来钟，他会猛地惊醒，飘进斯托弗的屋子，把鼻子贴在窗玻璃上，焦躁地盘算这一天能发明点什么祸。
先作一阵不满意的内省，然后烦躁地说：
「我说，丁克？」
「喂！」
「用功呢？」
「嗯。」
「快完了吧？」
「没。」
「麦卡锡，你在干什么？」
「啃一道要命的球面几何。」
「我放弃了。」
「哦，你放弃了！」
「当然，太难了——把工夫耗在上面干什么？打一局台球去？」
「滚！」
「上惠特尼湖划船去。」
「闭嘴！」
「到谢菲尔德找汤姆·凯利凑一局扑克去。」
每到这个节骨眼，斯托弗和麦卡锡便震怒而起，麦克纳布抱头便走，一路躲开朝他飞来的任何东西。他委委屈屈地顺着楼道走，挨个推门——各家的门早防着他锁上了。

===Original===

About this time Buck Waters, moved by similar impulses, would appear and the two would camp down on the top step and practise duets, until a furious uprising in the house would drive them ignominiously on to the street.
Left to their own resources, they would wander aimlessly about the city, inventing a hundred methods to accomplish the most difficult of all feats, killing time.
On one particular morning in early November, McNab and Buck Waters, being refused admission to three houses on York Street, and the affront being aggravated by jeers and epithets of the coarsest kind, went arm in arm on mischief bent.
“I say, what let’s do?” said McNab disconsolately.
“We must do something new,” said Buck Waters.
“We certainly must.”
“Well, let’s try the old clothes gag,” said McNab; “that always amuses a little.”
Reaching the thoroughfare of Chapel Street, McNab stationed himself at the corner while Waters proceeded to a point about halfway down the block.
Assuming a lounging position against a lamppost, McNab waited until chance delivered up to him a superhumanly dignified citizen in top hat and boutonnière, moving through the crowd with an air of solid importance.
Darting out, he approached with the sweep of an eagle, saying in a hoarse whisper:
“Old clothes, any old clothes, sir?”
His victim, frowning, accelerated his pace.
“Buy your old clothes, sir, buy ’em now.”
Several onlookers stopped and looked. The gentleman, who had not turned to see who was addressing him, said hurriedly in an undertone:
“No, no, nothing today.”
“Buy ’em tomorrow﻿—pay good price,” said McNab peevishly.
“No, no, nothing to sell.”
“Call around at the house﻿—give good prices.”
“Nothing to sell, nothing, I tell you!”
“Buy what you got on,” said McNab at the psychological moment, “give you five dollars or toss you ten or nothinks!”
“Be off!” said the now thoroughly infuriated victim, turning and brandishing his cane. “I’ll have you arrested.”
McNab, having accomplished his preliminary role, retreated to a safe distance, exclaiming:
“Toss you ten dollars or nothinks!”
The now supremely self-conscious and furious gentleman, having rid himself of McNab, immediately found himself in the hands of Buck Waters, who pursued him for the remainder of the block, with a mild obsequious persistency that would not be shaken off.

===Chinese===

约莫这个时候，巴克·沃特斯也被同一股冲动驱使着出现了，两人便在顶级的台阶上安营，练习二重唱，直唱到楼里一场总暴动，把他们灰溜溜轰到街上去。
没了去处，两人便在城里漫无目的地荡，发明出一百种法子，去完成天下最难的一桩功业——消磨时光。
十一月初一个特定的上午，麦克纳布和巴克·沃特斯连吃约克街三家的闭门羹，又被最粗俗的嘲笑和臭骂加码，于是手挽着手，专程寻祸去了。
「我说，干什么好呢？」麦克纳布恹恹地说。
「得来点新花样，」巴克·沃特斯说。
「那是当然。」
「那就来老一套的『旧衣裳』吧，」麦克纳布说；「这总能乐一小阵。」
到了教堂街这条大街，麦克纳布在街角站定，沃特斯往街那头走到半路的地方。
麦克纳布往灯柱上一倚，摆出闲人的架势，等运气送来一位超凡入圣的体面市民——大礼帽、领口别着花，一脸沉甸甸的要人气度穿过人群。
他一个箭步蹿出去，带着鹰隼的俯冲逼近，用沙哑的耳语说：
「旧衣裳，收旧衣裳，先生？」
受害人皱起眉，加快脚步。
「收购您的旧衣裳，先生，现在就收。」
几个路人停下来看。那位先生没回头看清是谁在搭话，急急地低声说：
「不，不，今天什么都不卖。」
「明天来收——出好价钱，」麦克纳布气鼓鼓地说。
「不，不，没什么可卖的。」
「登门来收——给好价钱。」
「没什么可卖，跟你说，没有！」
「您身上这套我买了，」麦克纳布瞅准心理时机说，「给您五块，要么掷个十块，或是一文不给！」
「滚开！」这会儿彻底暴怒的受害人说，回身挥舞手杖。「我报警抓你。」
麦克纳布头一炮打完，退到安全距离，嚷：
「掷个十块，或是一文不给！」
这位此刻自我意识膨胀到顶、怒火冲天的先生刚甩脱麦克纳布，立刻落进巴克·沃特斯手里——沃特斯黏着他走完了剩下的半条街，那副温良谄媚的死缠，怎么甩也甩不脱。

===Original===

By this time the occupants of the shop windows and the loiterers, perceiving the game, were in roars of laughter, which made the passage of the second and third victims a procession of hilarious triumph for McNab and Waters.
Tiring of this, they locked arms again and, taking by hazard a side street, continued their quest for adventure.
“Mornings are a dreadful bore,” said McNab, pulling down his hat.
“They certainly are.”
“Who was the old duck we tackled first?”
“Don’t know﻿—familiar whiskers.”
“Seemed to me I’ve seen him somewhere.”
“Say, look at the ki-yi.”
“It’s a Shetland poodle.”
“It’s a pen-wiper.”
Directly in front of them a shaggy French poodle, bearing indeed a certain resemblance to both a Shetland pony and a discarded pen-wiper, was gleefully engaged in the process of shaking to pieces a rubber which it had stolen.
“If it sees itself in a mirror it will die of mortification,” said Buck Waters.
“And yet, Buck, he’s happier than we are,” said McNab, who had been unjustifiably forced to flunk twice in one morning’s recitation.
“I say, Dopey,” said Waters in alarm, “quit that!”
“I will.”
“Look at the fireworks,” said Waters, stopping suddenly at a window, “pinwheels, rockets, Roman candles.”
“What are they doing there this time of the year?” said McNab angrily.
“Election parade, perhaps.”
“That’s an idea to work on, Buck.”
“It certainly is.”
“We must tell Tom Kelly about that.”
“We will.”
“Why, there’s that ridiculous ki-yi again!”
“He seems to like us.”
“I’m not complimented.”
At this moment, with the poodle sporting the rubber about fifteen feet ahead of them, they beheld an Italian barber lolling in the doorway of his shop, as profoundly bored by himself as they affected to be in conjunction.
“Fine dog,” said the barber with a critical glance.
“Sure,” said McNab, halting at once.
The poodle, for whatever reason, likewise halted and looked around.
“Looka better, cutta da hair.”
“You’re right there, Columbus,” assented Buck Waters. “His fur coat looks as though it came from a fire sale.”
“He ought to be trim up nice, good style.”
“Right, very, very right!”
“Give him nice collar, nice tuft on da tail, nice tuft on da feet.”
“Right the second time!”
“I clip him up, eh?” said the barber hopefully.
“Why not?” said McNab, looking into the depth of Buck Waters’s eyes.
“Why not, Beecher?” said Waters, giving him the name of the President of the College Y.

===Chinese===

到这时，铺子窗口里的人和闲汉们都看出了门道，哄笑成一片，害得第二、第三位受害人过街，成了麦克纳布和沃特斯一场凯旋的游行。
玩腻了这个，两人重新挽起胳膊，随手拈进一条小街，继续寻他们的奇遇。
「上午真闷得死人，」麦克纳布拉低了帽檐说。
「可不是。」
「咱们头一个纠缠的老鸭子是谁？」
「不认得——胡子眼熟。」
「我好像在哪儿见过他。」
「喂，瞧那条杂毛狗。」
「是只设得兰贵宾犬。」
「是个掸笔擦。」
正前方，一只毛茸茸的法国贵宾犬——确实同设得兰小马和废弃的掸笔擦都有几分像——正欢天喜地，把它偷来的一块橡皮筋甩着玩，一心抖个稀烂。
「它要是照照镜子，非臊死不可，」巴克·沃特斯说。
「可话说回来，巴克，它比咱们快活，」麦克纳布说——他这天上午一堂背诵课莫名其妙地挨了两回不及格。
「我说，多皮，」沃特斯警觉地说，「打住！」
「我打住。」
「看烟花，」沃特斯在一扇橱窗前忽然站住说，「转花、火箭、罗马烛。」
「一年里的这个时节摆这些干什么？」麦克纳布生气地说。
「竞选游行吧，多半是。」
「这话有得挖，巴克。」
「大有得挖。」
「得告诉汤姆·凯利。」
「一定。」
「咦，那条可笑的杂毛狗又来了！」
「它好像喜欢咱们。」
「我可不觉着荣幸。」
正在这时，那条贵宾犬叼着橡皮，在他们前头十五英尺的地方撒欢，他们望见一个意大利理发师在店门口懒洋洋地倚着，百无聊赖得同他们装出来的那副一样深。
「好狗，」理发师挑剔地瞥了一眼说。
「可不是，」麦克纳布应声站住。
那条贵宾犬不知为什么也站住，回头看了看。
「剪剪毛，好看多多。」
「这话在理，哥伦布，」巴克·沃特斯附和。「它这身皮大衣，瞧着像火烧大甩卖上来的货。」
「该给它修剪整齐，像像样样。」
「对，太对太对了！」
「给它戴好项圈，尾巴剪好绒球，脚上剪好绒球。」
「又对了一回！」
「我给它剪剪，嗯？」理发师满怀指望地说。
「有何不可？」麦克纳布说，直望进巴克·沃特斯的眼睛深处。
「有何不可，比彻？」沃特斯说，把学院基督教青年会（Y. M. C. A.）会长的名号奉送给他。

===Original===

M. C. A.
“I think it an excellent suggestion, Jonathan Edwards,” said McNab instantly.
With considerable strategic coaxing, the dog was enticed into the shop, where to their surprise he became immediately docile.
“You see he lika da clip,” said the barber enthusiastically, preparing a table.
“He’s a very intelligent dog,” said McNab.
“You’ve done much of this, Columbus?” said Waters with a businesslike air.
“Sure. Ten, twenta dog a day, down in da city.”
“Edwards, we shall learn something.”
The dog was induced to come on the table, and Waters delegated to hold him in position.
“Something pretty slick now, Christopher,” said McNab, taking the attitude a connoisseur should take. “Explain the fine points to us, as you go along.”
“Sure.”
“I like the way he handles the scissors, Beecher﻿—strong, powerful stroke.”
“He’s got a good batting eye, too, Edwards.”
“My, what a nice clean boulevard!”
“Just see the hair fly.”
“It’ll certainly improve the tail.”
“Clip a little anchor in the middle of the back.”
“Did you see that?”
“I did.”
“He’s a wonder.”
“He is.”
“Columbus, a little more off here﻿—oh, just a trifle!”
“First rate; shave up the nose and part the whiskers!”
“Look at the legs, with the dinky pantalets﻿—aren’t they dreams?”
“I love the tail best.”
“Why, Columbus is an artist. Never saw anyone like him.”
“Would you know the dog?”
“Why, mother wouldn’t know him,” said McNab solemnly.
“All in forty-three minutes, too.”
“It’s beautifully done, beautifully.”
“Exquisite!”
The barber, perspiring with his ambitious efforts, withdrew for a final inspection, clipped a little on the top and to the side, and signified by a nod that art could go no further.
“Pretta fine, eh?”
“Mr. Columbus, permit me,” said Waters, shaking hands.
McNab gravely followed suit. The dog, released, gave a howl and began circling madly about the room.
“Open the door,” shouted McNab. “See how happy he is!”
The three stationed themselves thoughtfully on the doorstep, watching the liberated poodle disappear down the street in frantic spirals, loops and figure-eights.
“He lika da feel,” said the barber, pleased.
“Oh, he’s much improved,” said Waters, edging a little away.
“He fine lookin’ a dog!”
“He’ll certainly surprise the girls and mother,” said McNab, shifting his feet. “Well, Garibaldi, ta-ta!”
“Hold up,” said the barber, “one plunk.”
“One dollar, Raphael?”

===Chinese===

「我看这条建议好极了，乔纳森·爱德华兹（Jonathan Edwards），」麦克纳布立刻接上。
两人连哄带骗施展战略，把狗诱进店堂；不料它一进门就服服帖帖。
「你看，它喜欢剪毛，」理发师兴冲冲地说，张罗起台子。
「它是条非常聪明的狗，」麦克纳布说。
「干这行很久了吧，哥伦布？」沃特斯摆出办正事的腔调。
「当然。城里一天十头、二十头狗。」
「爱德华兹，咱们要长学问了。」
狗被劝上了台，沃特斯受命按住它。
「现在来点漂亮的，克里斯托弗，」麦克纳布摆出鉴赏家的架势。「一路剪，一路把妙处讲给我们听。」
「成。」
「我喜欢他使剪子的手法，比彻——有力，下剪千钧。」
「他眼力也棒，爱德华兹。」
「乖乖，好一条清爽的大道！」
「瞧毛飞得多欢。」
「尾巴这一剪肯定精神多了。」
「背脊当中剪个小锚。」
「瞧见没有？」
「瞧见了。」
「他是位奇才。」
「是啊。」
「哥伦布，这儿再剪掉一点——哦，就一丁点！」
「顶好；鼻梁剃光，胡子分缝！」
「瞧这四条腿，套着小灯笼裤——不是画的梦吗？」
「我最爱这条尾巴。」
「嗬，哥伦布是位艺术家。从没见过这样的。」
「还认得出这条狗吗？」
「嗐，它亲妈都认不得了，」麦克纳布郑重地说。
「前后才四十三分钟。」
「剪得漂亮，漂亮。」
「精美绝伦！」
理发师为一番雄心大干，汗水涔涔，退后作最后一巡检视，头顶剪一点，两侧剪一点，一点头示意：艺术至此，登峰造极。
「漂亮吧，嗯？」
「哥伦布先生，请受一握，」沃特斯说着，伸出手去。
麦克纳布郑重其事，跟着照办。狗一松开，嚎了一声，绕着屋子疯转起来。
「开门，」麦克纳布喊。「看它多快活！」
三人若有所思地在门槛上站定，看那条解放了的贵宾犬，一路狂乱的螺旋、圈套和八字形，消失在街尾。
「它喜欢这手感，」理发师心满意足。
「哦，它是精神多了，」沃特斯说，悄悄挪开半步。
「好一条俊狗！」
「它准会把姑娘们和老妈子吓一跳，」麦克纳布说，挪了挪脚。「好啦，加里波第（Garibaldi），回头见！」
「站住，」理发师说，「一块钱。」
「一块钱，拉斐尔（Raphael）？」

===Original===

said Buck Waters in innocent surprise. “What for, oh, what for?”
“One plunk, clippa da dog.”
“Yes, but Garibaldi,” said McNab gently, “that wasn’t our dog.”
“Shall we run for it?” said Waters, as they went hurriedly up the block.
“Wait until Garibaldi gives chase﻿—we must be dignified,” said McNab, with an eye to the rear.
“Dagos have no sense of humor. Here he comes with a razor﻿—scud for it!”
They dashed madly for the corner, doubled a couple of times, joined by the rejuvenated friendly poodle, and suddenly, wheeling around a corner, ran straight into the dean, who as fate would have it, was accompanied by the very dignified citizen who had been the first victim of their old clothes act and upon whom the frantic poodle, with canine expressions of relief and delight, immediately cast himself.
---
“Buck,” said McNab, half an hour later, as they went limply back, “Napoleon would have whipped the British to an omelet at Waterloo if he’d known about that sunken road.”
“We are but mortals.”
“How the deuce were we to know the pup belonged to Professor Borgle, the eminent rootitologist?”
“Well, we paid the dago, didn’t we?”
“That was outrageous.”
“I say, Dopey, what’ll you do if they fire us?”
“Don’t joke on such subjects.”
“Dopey,” said Waters solemnly, “while the dean has the case under consideration, just to aid his deliberations, I think we had better﻿—well, study a little.”
“I suppose we must flirt with the textbooks,” said McNab, “but let’s do it together, so no one’ll suspect.”

===Chinese===

巴克·沃特斯天真地吃惊说。「凭什么，哦，凭什么？」
「一块，剪狗。」
「可是，加里波第，」麦克纳布温和地说，「那不是我们的狗。」
「跑不跑？」沃特斯说，两人急急往街那头走。
「等加里波第追上来再跑——咱们得体面，」麦克纳布说，一只眼留着后路。
「意大利佬没一点幽默感。他拎着剃刀追来了——快溜！」
两人疯也似的冲向街角，连拐了几个弯，那条返老还童的亲热贵宾犬也加入了进来；猛然一个转弯，迎头撞上了教务长——偏赶上他身边正走着那位超凡体面的市民，旧衣裳把戏的头一位受害人；那条发狂的贵宾犬带着一脸狗式的如释重负和欢喜，立刻扑了过去。
---
「巴克，」半小时后，两人一瘸一拐往回走，麦克纳布说，「拿破仑当年要是知道那条洼路，滑铁卢早把英国佬煎成鸡蛋饼了。」
「我们到底是凡人。」
「谁想得到那小狗是博格利教授的——那位大名鼎鼎的根学泰斗？」
「反正意大利佬的钱咱们付了，不是吗？」
「付得肉疼。」
「我说，多皮，要是学校开除咱们，你怎么办？」
「这种题目不许开玩笑。」
「多皮，」沃特斯郑重地说，「趁教务长还在斟酌这个案子，为帮他参详参详，我想咱们最好——嗯，稍稍用点功。」
「课本嘛，想必只好同它们调调情，」麦克纳布说，「不过得咱们俩一块儿，免得招人疑心。」

## X / 十
===Original===

Almost before he knew it Stover was in the car and the wheels were moving at last irresistibly toward the field. There was no longer any pretense in those last awful moments that had in them all the concentrated hopes and fears of the weeks that had rushed away. The faces of his own teammates were only gray faces without identity. He saw someone’s lips moving incessantly, but he did not remember whose they were. Opposite him, another man was bending over, his head hidden in his hands. Someone else at his side was nervously locking and unlocking his fingers, breathing short, hard breaths. He remembered only the stillness of it all, the forgetfulness of others, the set stares, and Charlie de Soto fidgeting on the seat and nervously humming something irrelevant.
Caught up in this unreasoning intensity of a young nation, filled, too, with this exaggerated passion of combat, Stover leaned back limply. Outside, the street was choked with hilarious parties packed in rushing carriages, blue or orange-and-black. Horns and rattles sounded like tiny sounds in his ears, and his eyes saw only grotesque blurred shapes that swept across them.
“I’ll get ’em off﻿—they won’t block any on me﻿—they mustn’t,” he said to himself, closing his eyes.
Then, on top of the draining weakness that had him in its grip, came a sudden feeling of nausea, and he knew suddenly what the man opposite him with his head in his hands was fighting. He put his arms over the ledge of the door, and rested his head on them, too weak to care that everyone saw him, gulping in the stinging air in desperation.
All at once there came a grinding jerk and the car stopped. From the inside came Tompkins’ angry, rasping voice:
“Everyone up! Get out there! Quick! On the jump!”
Instinctively obedient, the vertigo left him, his mind cleared. He was out in the midst of the bobbing mass of blue sweaters, moving as in a nightmare through the black spectators, seeing ahead the mammoth stands, hearing the dull, engulfing roars as one hears at night the approaching surf.
Then they were struggling through the human barriers, and he saw something green at the bottom of a stormy pit, and a great growing roar of welcome smote him as of a descending gale, the hysterical cry of the American multitude, a roar acclaiming Yale.
“All ready!” said Dana’s unrecognizable voice somewhere ahead. “On the trot, now!”

===Chinese===

还没等他明白过来，斯托弗已经坐进了车，车轮终于无可抗拒地朝球场滚去。最后这可怕的片刻里再没有半点做作——里面浓缩着飞逝而去的那些星期里全部的希望与恐惧。队友们的脸只是些没有名姓的灰脸。他看见有谁的嘴唇不停翕动，却想不起那是谁的嘴。对面一个人弓着身，头埋在手里。身边另一个人紧张地十指交扣又松开，呼吸又短又硬。他只记得那一路的寂静，人人忘了旁人，目光发直，还有查利·德索托（Charlie De Soto）在座位上坐立不安，神经质地哼着不相干的小调。

被这个年轻民族的这股不假思索的炽烈裹挟着，又灌满了这股夸张的战斗激情，斯托弗瘫软地靠在椅背上。车窗外，街道塞满了一车车疾驰的欢腾人群，一片蓝，一片橙黑。喇叭和响板在他耳朵里只像细碎的声响，眼睛看到的只是掠过车窗的奇形怪状的模糊影子。

「我会把球踢出去——他们封不死我一个——绝不能，」他闭着眼睛对自己说。

随后，在那股攋着他不放的、抽空力气般的虚弱之上，又涌起一阵突兀的恶心，他忽然明白了对面那个抱头的家伙在对抗什么。他把胳膊架在车窗沿上，头搁上去，虚弱得顾不上人人都看得见他，绝望地大口吞着刺人的空气。

募地，车子咯噔一声刹住。车厢里传来汤普金斯暴躁刺耳的嗓音：

「都起来！出去！快！跳着走！」

本能地服从，眩晕退了，脑子清了。他已置身于一片起伏攒动的蓝色毛衫的人海，像在梦魇里穿过黑压压的观众，望见前方庞大的看台，听见那闷闷的、吞没一切的吼声，像夜里听见逼近的浪头。

接着他们挤过一道道人的屏障，他看见风暴般的深坑底部有一片绿色，一阵越来越响的欢迎怒吼劈头盖来，像一阵俯冲的狂风——美国大众歇斯底里的呐喊，为耶鲁喝彩的吼声。

「都齐了！」前面什么地方传来达纳变了嗓的喊声。「跑步走！」

===Original===

Instantly he was sweeping on to the field and up along the frantic stands of suddenly released blue. All indecision, all weakness, went with the first hoarse cry from his own. Something hot and alive seemed to flow back into his veins, and with every stride the spongy turf underneath seemed to send its strength and vitality into his legs.
From the other end of the field, through the somber crowd, an orange-and-black group was trickling, flowing into a band and sweeping out on the field, while the Princeton stands were surging to their feet, adding the mounting fury of their welcome to the deafening uproar that suddenly bound the arena in the gripping hollow of a whirlwind.
“Line up, you blue devils,” came Charlie de Soto’s raucous cry. “On your toes. Get your teeth into it. Hard, now. Ha‑a‑ard!”
He was in action immediately, thinking only of the signals, sweeping down the field, now to the right, now to the left, stumbling in his eagerness.
“Enough,” said the captain’s voice, at last. “Get under your sweaters, fellows. Brown and Stover, start up some punts.”
Dana and Dudley went back to practise catching. Brown, the center, pigskin under hand, set himself for the pass, while Stover, blowing on his hands, measured his distance. Opposite, Bannerman, the Princeton fullback, was setting himself for a similar attempt.
In the stands was a sudden craning hush as the great audience waited to see with its own eyes the disparity between the rival fullbacks.
Stover, standing out, felt it all instinctively, with a little nervous tremor﻿—the quick stir in the stands, the muttered comments, the tense turning of even the cheer leaders.
Then the ball came shooting back to him. He caught it, turned it in his hands, and drove forward his leg with all his might. At the same moment, as if maliciously calculated, the great booming punt of Bannerman brought the Princeton stands, rollicking and gleeful, to their feet in a burst of triumph.
In his own stands there was no answering shout Stover felt on his cheeks, under his eyes, two hot spots of anger. What did they know, who condemned him, of the sacrifice he had made, of the far more difficult thing he was doing? He remembered Tompkins’ advice; he could not compete with Bannerman in the air.

===Chinese===

转眼间他已涌进场内，沿着突然泄洪般狂腾起来的蓝色看台前行。所有的迟疑、所有的虚弱，都随着自家看台头一声嘶喊烟消云散。某种滚烫的活物仿佛流回了血管，每一步踏下去，脚下松软的草皮都像把力气和生气灌进他的双腿。

球场另一头，一支橙黑的小队正穿过暗色的人流渗出来，汇成一带，涌进场内；普林斯顿的看台同时轰然起立，把越来越凶的欢迎加进那震耳欲聋的喧嚣——整个竞技场霉时被卷进旋风的轰心。

「列队，你们这群蓝魔鬼，」查利·德索托的粗嗓门喊。「踮起脚尖。咬住劲。狠，现在。狠——！」

他立刻投入动作，满脑子只有战术口令，沿球场扫下去，忽而向右，忽而向左，急得直打趔趄。

「够了，」队长终于开口。「把毛衫穿上，弟兄们。布朗、斯托弗，练几脚踢球。」

达纳和达德利（Dudley）退后练接球。中锋布朗（Brown）把橄榄球垫在手边，摆好传球姿势；斯托弗往手上呵着气，量着步点。对面，普林斯顿的后卫班纳曼（Bannerman）也摆开了同样的架势。

看台上忽然一片伸长脖子的肃静，满场观众等着亲眼看看两位对位后卫的高下。

站在场上的斯托弗本能地把这一切都感到了，带着一点紧张的颤——看台上迅疾的骚动，压低的议论，连啦啦队长们都绷紧了转身望来。

这时球嗖地传回给他。他接住，在手里一转，使出全身力气踢腿。同一时刻，仿佛恶毒地掐好了算好，班纳曼那记轰轰烈烈的悬空球，让普林斯顿看台笑闹欢腾地全体起立，爆出一片凯旋。

自家看台上没有回应的喊声。斯托弗感到脸颊上、眼睛底下，烧起两团火。那些判他死刑的人，哪里知道他做出的牺牲，哪里知道他干的这件难上加大难的事？他记起汤普金斯的叮嘱；比空中，他拼不过班纳曼。

===Original===

Deliberately he sent his next punt low, swift, striking the ground about thirty yards away and rolling treacherously another fifteen feet before Dudley, who had swerved out, could stop it. This time from the mass almost a groan went up.
A sudden cold contempt for them, for everything, seized possession of Stover. He hated them all. He stooped, plucked a blade of grass, and stuck it defiantly between his teeth.
“Shoot that back a little lower, Brown,” he said with a sudden quick authority, and again and again he sent off his fast, low-rolling punts.
“That’s the stuff, Dink,” said Tompkins, with a pat on the shoulder, “but you’ve got to get ’em off on the instant﻿—remember that. Here, throw this sweater over you.”
“All right.”
He did not sit down, but walked back and forth with short steps, waiting for the interminable conference of the captains to be over. And again that same sinking, hollow feeling came over him in the suspense before the question that would be answered in the first shock of bodies.
The feeling he felt ran through the thousands gathered only to a spectacle. The cheers grew faint, lacking vitality, and the stir of feet was a nerve-racked stir. Dink gazed up at the high benches, trying to forget the interval of seconds that must be endured. It did not seem possible that he was to go out before them all. It seemed rather that in a far-off consciousness he was the same loyal little shaver who had squirmed so often on the top line of the benches, clinging to his knees, biting his lips, and looking weakly on the ground.
“All ready﻿—get out, boys!”
Dana came running back. Yale had won the toss and had chosen to kick off.
Someone pulled his sweater from him, struck him a stinging slap between the shoulders, and propelled him on the field.
“Yale this way!”
They formed in a circle, heads down, arms locked over one another’s shoulders, disputing the same air; and Dana, the captain, who believed in a victory, spoke:
“Now, fellows, one word. It’s up to us. Do you understand what that means? It’s up to us to win, the way Yale has won in the past﻿—and win we’re going to, no matter how long it takes or what’s against us. Now, get mad, every one of you. Run ’em right off their feet. That’s all.”
The shoulders under Stover’s left him.

===Chinese===

他故意把下一脚踢得又低又快，球在三十来码外砸地，又刁滑地滚出十五英尺，才被斜插出来的达德利截住。这一回，人堆里几乎升起一声呻吟。

一股冷冷的轻蔑，对他们、对一切，攋住了斯托弗。他恨他们全体。他弯腰拔下一根草，挑衅地叼在牙间。

「传球再低一点，布朗，」他忽然用上一种干脆的威严，一脚又一脚，把球踢得又快又低贴地乱滚。

「就是这个路子，丁克，」汤普金斯拍拍他的肩，「可你得说踢就踢，一瞬不耽搁——记住。来，把这件毛衫披上。」

「好。」

他不坐，来回踱着碎步，等两位队长那场没完没了的会商结束。那种下坠的、空落落的感觉又漫上来——在肉身相撞的头一响之前，那道即将见分晓的悬题底下。

这股感觉，也穿过那几千个专为看一场热闹而来的人。欢呼稀了，没了活气，脚下的骚动成了一种神经备受折磨的骚动。丁克仰头望着高高的看台排，努力忘掉那段必须熬过去的分秒。他就要当着这所有人的面上场——这简直不像真的。倒不如说，在某个遥远的意识里，他还是当年那个忠心耿耿的小毛头，多少回在看台顶排的座位上扭来扭去，抱着膝盖，咬着嘴唇，虚弱地望着地面。

「都齐了——上场，弟兄们！」

达纳跑回来。耶鲁掷币赢了，选择开球。

有人扯掉他的毛衫，在他两肩胛之间啪地拍了一记脆的，把他推上场。

「耶鲁的这边来！」

他们围成一圈，头低着，胳膊互相锁在肩上，同争一口气；笃信胜利的队长达纳开口：

「听着，弟兄们，一句话。看我们的了。你们明白这是什么意思吗？赢，要看我们的——像耶鲁从前赢的那样去赢——而且我们赢定了，不管打多久，不管对面是什么。现在，一个个都给我恨起来。把他们跑断腿。就这些。」

斯托弗手底下的肩膀散了。

===Original===

He went hazily to the place, a little behind the rest, where he knew he should go, waiting while Brown poised the football, waiting while the orange-and-black jerseys indistinctly scattered before him to their formation, waiting for the whistle for which he had waited all his life to release him.
And for a third time his legs seemed to crumble, and the whole blurred scheme of stands and field to reel away from him, and his heart to be lying before him on the ground where he could lean over and pick it up.
Then like a pistol shot the whistle went throbbing through his brain. He sprang forward as if out of the shell of himself, keen, alert, filled with a savage longing.
Down the field a Princeton halfback had caught the ball and was squirming back. Then a sudden upheaval, and a mass was spread on the ground.
“Guess he gained about fifteen on that,” he said to himself. “They’ll kick right off.”
Dana came running back to support him. Out of the sky like a monstrous bird something round, yellow, and squirming came floating toward him. He was forced to run back, misjudged it a little, reached out, half fumbled it, and recovered it with a plunging dive just as Cockerell landed upon him.
“Get you next time, Dink,” said the voice of his old school captain in his ear.
Stover, struggling to his feet, looked him coolly in the eye.
“No, you won’t, Garry, and you know it. The next time I’m going back ten yards.”
“Well, boy, we’ll see.”
They shook hands with a grim smile, while the field straggled up. He was lined up, flanked by Dana and Dudley, bending over, waiting for the signal. Three times De Soto, trying out the Princeton line, sent Dana plunging against the right tackle, barely gaining the distance. A fourth attempt being stopped for a loss, Stover dropped back for a kick on the second down.
The ball came a little low, and with it the whole line seemed torn asunder and the field filled with the rush of converging bodies. To have kicked would have been fatal. He dropped quickly on the ball, covering it, under the shock of his opponents.
Again he was back, waiting for the trial that was coming. He forgot that he was a freshman﻿—forgot everything but his own utter responsibility.
“You center men, hold that line!” he cried. “You give me a chance! Give me time!”

===Chinese===

他恍惚地走向那个位置，比旁人慢半拍，那他知道该去的地方，等着布朗摆好球，等着眼前橙黑的球衣影影绰绰散开成阵，等着那声他等了一辈子的哨音来释放他。

第三次，他的双腿像要瘫了，看台和球场那整幅模糊的图景像要脱开他旋走，他的心像躺在了眼前的地上，一俯身就能拾起来。

哨声像一声枪响，穿过他的脑子嗡嗡震颤。他向前跃出，仿佛从自己的躯壳里挣脱出来，锐利，警醒，满腔凶悍的渴望。

球场那头，普林斯顿的半卫接住球，正往回挣。随后一阵突起的人浪，一堆人摊在了地上。

「这一下他大概推进了十五码，」他对自己说。「他们马上要踢了。」

达纳跑回来支援他。天上像一只怪鸟，一团圆圆的、黄的、扭动着的东西朝他飘来。他被迫回跑，略微判断错了落点，伸手去接，险些脱手，一个俯冲才把球捞回怀——恰在此时科克雷尔（Cockerell）压到了他身上。

「下回逮住你，丁克，」他老校队队长的嗓音贴着他耳朵说。

斯托弗挣扎起身，冷冷地直视他的眼睛。

「不，你逮不住，加里，你自己知道。下回我要往回退十码接。」

「好啊小子，走着瞧。」

两人狩笑着握手，队员们陆陆续续归位。他列进阵里，达纳和达德利一左一右，弓着身，等口令。德索托试探普林斯顿的防线，三次把达纳朝对方右截锋里砸，勉强够着距离。第四次尝试被堵回倒丢码数，第二次进攻，斯托弗退后准备踢球。

球传得低了些，随之整条防线像被撕开，满场都是向一点汇聚的人潮。踢，必死。他猛地扑倒在球上，把它压在身下，硬生生扛着对手的冲撞。

他又退了回来，等着下一场考验。他忘了自己是新生——忘了一切，只剩自己那份天大的责任。

「中锋的，给我顶住那条线！」他喊。「给我个机会！给我点时间！」

===Original===

Then the ball was in his hands, and, still a little hurried, he sent it too high over the frantic leaping rush, hurled to the ground the instant after.
The exchange had netted Princeton twenty yards. A second time Bannerman lifted his punt, high, long, twisting and turning over itself in tricky spirals. It was a perfect kick, giving the ends exact time to cover it.
Stover, with arms outstretched, straining upward, cool as a Yankee, knew, from the rushing bodies he did not dare to look at, what was coming. The ball landed in his convulsive arms, and almost exactly with it Garry Cockerell’s body shot into him and tumbled him clear off the ground, crashing down; but the ball was locked in his arms in one of those catches of which the marvel of the game is, not that they are not made oftener, but that they are made at all.
“Come on now, Yale,” shouted Charlie De Soto’s inflaming voice. “We’ve got to rip this line. Signal!”
Two masses on center, two futile straining, crushing attempts, and again he was called on to kick. The tackles he had received had steadied him, driving from his too imaginative mind all consideration but the direct present need.
He began to enjoy with a fierce delight this kicking in the very teeth of the frantic Princeton rushes, as he had stood on the beach waiting for great breakers to form above his head before diving through.
On the fourth exchange of kicks he stood on his own goal-line. The test had come at last. Dana, furious at being driven back without a Princeton rush, came to him wildly.
“Dink, you’ve got to make it good!”
“Take that long-legged Princeton tackle when he comes through,” he said quietly. “Don’t worry about me.”
Luckily, they were over to the left side of the field. He chose his opening, and, kicking low, as Tompkins had coached him, had the joy of seeing the ball go flying over the ground and out of bounds at the forty-yard line.
The Princeton team, springing into position, at last opened its attack.
“Now we’ll see,” said Stover, chafing in the backfield.
Using apparently but one formation, a circular mass, which, when directly checked, began to revolve out toward end, always pushing ahead, always concealing the runner, the Princeton attack surely, deliberately, and confidently rolled down the field like a juggernaut.

===Chinese===

球进了他手里，他还有点仓促，一脚踢得偏高，掠过疯狂跃起封堵的人潮，随即自己被掼倒在地。

这一换，普林斯顿净赚二十码。班纳曼第二次起脚，又高又长，带着刁钻的螺旋自转。一记完美的踢球，给两翼边锋留足了封堵的时间。

斯托弗双臂张开，向上极力伸展，冷静得像个扬基佬，凭那些他不敢正眼看的人影，知道要来什么了。球砸进他痉挛的双臂，几乎同一瞬间，加里·科克雷尔的身子撞进他，把他整个掀离地面砸下去；可球锁在他怀里——那种接球，此中奇迹不在于是不是常常接住，而在于竟然接得住。

「上啊，耶鲁，」查利·德索托那把点火的嗓子喊。「这条防线得撕开。口令！」

两次砸中锋，两次挤死人的苦攻都白费，又轮到他踢球。挨的那几记擒抱反倒稳住了他，把他那颗过于多想的脑子里的杂念全数赶走，只留下眼前的急需。

他开始凶猛地享受这种迎着普林斯顿疯狂冲锋的踢球，像当年站在海滩上，等大浪在头顶上聚成形，再一头扎过去。

第四个踢球回合，他站上了自家的球门线。考验终于来了。达纳对被人家不费一冲就逼退火冒三丈，狂躁地奔过来。

「丁克，这一脚你必须踢好！」

「那个长腿普林斯顿截锋冲进来时你顶着，」他静静地说。「别操心我。」

幸好，球在场地左侧。他选好空当，按汤普金斯教的踢低的，欣然看着球贴地飞窜，四十码线外出了界。

普林斯顿各就各位，终于发起了进攻。

「这回看吧，」斯托弗在后场摩拳擦掌。

只用一招似的——一团圆形的人堆，正面一被挡住，便朝边锋外侧旋转展开，永远向前拱，永远把持球者藏在腹心——普林斯顿的进攻沉着、笃定、自信，像一辆神车碾下球场。

===Original===

From the forty-yard line to the thirty it came in two rushes, from the thirty to the twenty in three; and then suddenly someone was tricked, drawn in from the vital attack, and the runner, guarded by one interferer, swept past the unprotected end and set out for a touchdown.
Stover went forward to meet them like a shot, frantic to save the precious yards. How he did it he never quite knew, but somehow he managed to fling himself just in front of the interferer and go down with a death grip on one leg of the runner.
A cold sponge was being spattered over him, he was on his back fighting hard for his breath, when he again realized where he was. He tried to rise, remembering all at once.
“Did I stop him?”
“You bet you did.”
Regan and Dudley had their arms about him, lifting him and walking him up and down.
“Get your breath back, old boy.”
“I’m all right.”
“Take your time; that Princeton duck hasn’t come to yet!”
He perceived in the opposite group something prone on the ground, and the sight was like a tonic.
The ball lay inside the ten-yard line, within the sacred zone. In a moment, no longer eliminated, but close to the breathing mass, he was at the back of his own men, shrieking and imploring:
“Get the jump, Yale!”
“Throw them back, Yale!”
“Fight ’em back!”
“You’ve got to, Yale﻿—you’ve got to!”
Then, again and again, the same perfected grinding surge of the complete machine: three yards, two yards, two yards, and he was underneath the last mass, desperately blocking off someone who held the vital ball, hoping against hope, blind with the struggle, saying to himself:
“It isn’t a touchdown! It can’t be! We’ve stopped them! It’s Yale’s ball!”
Someone was squirming down through the gradually lightening mass. A great weight went from his back, and suddenly he saw the face of the referee seeking the exact location of the ball.
“What is it?” he asked wildly.
“Touchdown.”
Someone dragged him to his feet, and, unnoticing, he leaned against him, gazing at the ball that lay just over the goal-line, seeing with almost a bull-like rage the Princeton substitutes frantically capering up and down the line, hugging one another, agitating their blankets, turning somersaults.
“Line up, Yale,” said the captain’s unyielding voice, “this is only the beginning. We’ll get ’em.”
But Stover knew better. The burst of anger past, his head cleared.

===Chinese===

四十码线到三十码线，两冲；三十到二十，三冲；随后忽然有人中了计，被从要害的攻势里调开，持球者由一名掩护护着，从失了保护的边锋外侧一掠而过，直奔达阵去了。

斯托弗像出膛的子弹迎上去，疯了似的要保住那金贵的码数。他到底怎么做到的，他自己从没完全弄清，反正他抢到了那名掩护的正前方扑倒下去，死死抓住持球者的一条腿。

冷水淋淋漓漓浇在他身上，他仰面躺着拼命喘气，这才重新想起自己在哪儿。他猛地全想起来了，挣扎着要起身。

「我拦住他了吗？」

「那还用说。」

里根和达德利架着他，扶他起来来回走。

「把气喘匀了，老伙计。」

「我没事。」

「慢慢来；那普林斯顿鸭子还没缓过来呢！」

他看见对面人堆里有个东西趴在地上，这景象如同补药。

球躺在十码线之内，进了圣地。转眼间，不再是被除名的外人，而是贴着那团喘息的肉山，他挤到自家队员身后，尖声哀告：

「抢启动，耶鲁！」

「把他们顶回去，耶鲁！」

「打回去！」

「你们必须顶住，耶鲁——必须！」

一遍又一遍，那台整机同样磨盘般的碾压推进：三码，两码，两码，他压在最底下那堆人身下，拼死堵着某个抱着要命之球的家伙，怀着不抱希望的希望，挣扎得两眼发盲，心里念着：

「没达阵！不可能！我们拦住了！球是耶鲁的！」

有人正从渐渐松动的人堆里往下钻。背上一座大山挪走了，他忽然看见裁判的脸，正在找球的准确落点。

「怎么样？」他狂乱地问。

「达阵。」

有人把他拽起来，他浑然不觉地靠在那人身上，望着刚过球门线的那只球，几乎公牛般狂怒地看着普林斯顿的替补们沿边线疯狂蹦跳，互相捾抱，抖着毯子，翻跟头。

「列队，耶鲁，」队长那不肯屈服的声音说，「这才开头。我们扳得回来。」

可斯托弗心里有数。一阵怒气过去，脑子清了。

===Original===

That Princeton team was going to score again, by the same process, playing on his weakness, exchanging punts, hoping to block one of his until within striking distance, and the size of the score would depend on how long he could stand it off.
“Goal,” came the referee’s verdict, and with it another roar from somewhere. He went up the field looking straight ahead, hearing, like a sound in a memory, a song of jubilation and the brassy accompaniment of a band.
Again the same story: ten, fifteen yards gained on every exchange of kicks, and a slow retrogression toward their own goal. Time and again they flung themselves against a stronger line, in a vain effort to win back the last yards. Once, in a plunge through center, he found an opening, and went plunging along for ten yards; but at the last the ball was Princeton’s on the thirty-five-yard line, and a second irresistible march bore Yale back, fighting and frantic over the line for the second score.
Playing became an instinct with him. He no longer feared the soaring punts that came tumbling to him from the clouds. His arms closed around them like tentacles, and he was off for the meager yards he could gain before he went down with a crash. He no longer felt the shock of the desperate tackles he was called on to make, nor the stifling pressure above him when he flung himself under the serried legs of the mass.
He had but one duty﻿—to be true to what he had promised Tompkins: not to fumble, not to miss a tackle, to get each punt off clean.
All at once, as he was setting in position, a body rushed in, seizing the ball.
“Time!”
The first half was over, and the score was: Princeton, 18; Yale, 0.
Then all at once he felt his weariness. He went slowly, grimly with the rest back to the dressing-room. A group of urchins clustering to a tree shrieked at them:
“O you Yaleses!”
He heard that, and that was all he heard. A sort of rebellion was in him. He had done all that he could do, and now they would haul him over the coals, thinking that was what he needed.
“Oh, I know what’ll be said,” he thought grimly. “We’ll be told we can win out in the second, and all that rot.”
Then he was in the hands of the rubbers, having his wet, clinging suit stripped from him, being rubbed and massaged. He did not want to look at his comrades, least of all Dana. He only wanted to get back, to have it over with.

===Chinese===

那支普林斯顿队还会再得分，还是老一套——吃准他的软肋，换踢，指望封下他一脚，好推进到够得着达阵的地方；最终比分会是多少，就看他能顶多久。

「加分命中，」裁判的判词传来，随着不知何处又一声吼。他朝前直望，向球场上头走去，听见一首凯旋歌和乐队铜管器的伴奏，像记忆里的一段声响。

还是老一套：每换一轮踢球就丢十码、十五码，慢慢朝自家球门退。一次又一次，他们扑向一条更硬的防线，妄想夺回那几码，全是徒劳。有一次从中锋的空当里钻出缺口，他一路撞出十码；可最后球还是落在三十五码线上归了普林斯顿，第二次不可抗拒的进军把苦战疯挣的耶鲁压回线内，第二次得分。

打球成了他的本能。他不再怕那些从云端翻滚砸下的高球。双臂像触手一样把它们圈住，随即狂奔，去挣那可怜的几码，直到轰然被放倒。他不再感到那些该他出手的亡命擒抱的冲撞，也不再感到钻进人丛密林般的腿底下时，压在上面的窒息。

他只剩一桩职责——兑现对汤普金斯的承诺：不掉球，不漏一记擒抱，每一脚都干干净净踢出去。

募地，他正要就位，一条身影冲进来，抢走了球。

「时间到！」

上半场结束，比分：普林斯顿十八，耶鲁零。

这时疲惫忽然压了下来。他同众人一道，一步一步咬着牙走回更衣室。一伙顽童攀在树上冲他们怪叫：

「哦你们这些耶鲁们！」

他听见了这个，也只听见了这个。一股造反的劲儿在他心里。他能做的全做了，如今他们要拿他架在火上烤，还当那就是他需要的。

「哦，等着听什么我都知道，」他狠狠地想。「他们会说下半场我们能翻盘，还有那一套屁话。」

随后他交到了按摩师手里，湿漉漉粘在身上的球衣被剥下，又是搓又是揉。他不想看队友们，最不想看达纳。他只想回到场上去，把它了结。

===Original===

“Yale, I want you to listen to me.”
He looked up. In the center stood Tompkins, preternaturally grave, trembling a little with nervous, uncontrollable twitches of his body.
“You’re up against a great Princeton team﻿—the greatest I remember. You can’t win. You never had a chance to win. But, Yale, you’re going to do something to make us proud of you. You’re going to hold that score where it is! Do you hear me? All you’ve got left is your nerve and the chance to show that you can die game. That’s all you’re going to do; but, by heaven, you’re going to do that! You’re going to die game, Yale! Every mother’s son of you! And when the game’s over we’re going to be prouder of your second half than the whole blooming Princeton bunch over their first. There’s your chance. Make us rise up and yell for you. Will you, Yale?”
He passed from man to man, advising, exhorting, or storming, until he came to Stover.
“Dink,” he said, putting out his hand and changing his tone suddenly, “I haven’t a word to say to you. Play the game as you’ve been doing﻿—only play it out.”
Stover felt a sudden rush of shame; all the fatigue left him as if by magic.
“If Charlie’ll only give me a few chances at the center. I know I could gain there,” he said eagerly.
“You’ll get a chance later on, perhaps, but you’ve quite enough to do now.”
The second view of the arena was clear to him, even to insignificant details. He thought the cheer leaders, laboring muscularly with their long megaphones, strangely out of place﻿—especially a short, fat little fellow in a white voluminous sweater. He saw in the crowd a face or two that he recognized﻿—Bob Story in a group of pretty girls, all superhumanly glum and cast down. Then he had shed his sweater and was out on the field, back under the goalposts, ready for the bruising second half to begin.
“All ready, Yale!”
“All ready.”
Again the whistle and the rush of bodies. Dana caught the ball, and, shifting and dodging, shaking off the first tacklers, carried it back twenty yards. Two short, jamming plunges by Dudley, through Regan, who alone was outplaying his man, yielded first down. Then an attempt at Cockerell’s end brought a loss and the inevitable kick.
Instead of a return punt, the Princeton eleven prepared to rush the ball.
“Why the deuce do they do that?” he thought, biting his fingers nervously.

===Chinese===

「耶鲁，我要你们听我说。」

他抬头。场子中央站着汤普金斯，异样地凝重，身子带着神经质的、压不住的抽动，微微发颤。

「你们面前是一支伟大的普林斯顿队——我这辈子记得的最伟大的一支。你们赢不了。你们从来就没有赢的机会。可是，耶鲁，你们要做出点事来，让我们为你们骄傲。你们要把这个比分钉死在原地！听见没有？你们剩下的只有胆气，和一个证明你们能壮烈倒下的机会。你们要做的就这一件；可是老天在上，这一件你们必须做到！你们要壮烈地倒下，耶鲁！一个不落，每一个！等比赛打完，你们这下半场要让我们骄傲——比那帮开花的普林斯顿人为他们的上半场还骄傲。机会就在这儿。让我们为你们站起来吼。干不干，耶鲁？」

他挨个走过去，出主意，打气，或者咆哮，走到斯托弗跟前。

「丁克，」他伸出手，声调陡然一变，「我对你没一句话要说。照你这样打下去——只是打完它。」

斯托弗一阵羞歇涌上来；浑身的疲惫像被魔法收走了。

「只要查理肯给我几次中锋的机会。我知道那兒我能推进。」他急切地说。

「待会儿会有你的机会，也许，可你眼下的事已经够多了。」

再进竞技场，他看得分明，连细枝末节都清楚。他觉得那些啦啦队长抡着长话筒卖力呼喊，显得说不出的格格不入——尤其是一个矮胖的小个子，裹在一件白色的肥大毛衫里。他在人群里认出一两张脸——鲍勃·斯托里（Bob Story）在一群漂亮姑娘中间，个个超乎凡人地阴郁沮丧。随后他脱下毛衫出了场，退到门柱底下，等着那皮开肉绽的下半场开打。

「都齐了，耶鲁！」

「齐了。」

又是哨响，又是肉身相撞。达纳接住球，变向，闪躲，甩开头一拨擒抱，带回二十码。达德利两次短促硬塞的冲撞，打里根身后——全场只有里根压着他对位的人打——换来首次进攻成功。随后一次攻科克雷尔那侧边锋的尝试丢了码数，该来的踢球来了。

普林斯顿没有回踢，全队摆开了地面推进的架势。

「他们干吗这么干？」他想着，紧张地咬手指。

===Original===

Opening up their play, Princeton swept out toward Bangs’s end, forcing it back for four yards, and immediately made first down with a long, sweeping lunge at the other end.
Suddenly Stover, in the backfield, watching like a cat, started forward with a cry. Far off to one side, a Princeton back, unperceived, was bending down, pretending to be fastening one of his shoelaces.
“Look out﻿—look out to the left!”
His cry came too late. The Princeton quarter made a long toss straight across, twenty yards, to the loitering half, who caught it and started down field clear of the line of scrimmage.
A Princeton forward tried to intercept him, but Stover flung him aside, and, without waiting, went forward at top speed to meet the man who came without flinching to his tackle. It was almost head on, and the shock, which left Stover stunned, instinctively clinging to his man, sent the ball free, where Dana pounced upon it.
“Holy Mike, what a tackle!” said Regan’s voice. “Any bones broken?”
“Of course not,” he said gruffly.
Someone insisted on sponging his face, much to his disgust.
“How’s the other fellow?” he said grimly.
“He’s a tough nut; he’s up, too!”
“He must be.”
The recovery of the ball gave them a short respite, but it served also to enrage the other line, which rose up and absolutely smothered the next plays. Again his kick seemed to graze the outstretched fingers of the Princeton forwards, and he laughed a strange laugh which he remembered long after.
This time the punting duel was resumed until, well within Yale territory, Cockerell looked around and gave the signal for attack.
“Now, Yale, stop it, stop it!” Dink said, talking to himself.
But there was no stopping that attack. Powerless, not daring to approach, he saw the blue line bend back again and again, and the steady, machine-like rolling up of the orange and black. Over the twenty-five-yard line it came, and on past the twenty.
“Oh, Yale, will you let ’em score again?” De Soto was shrieking.
“You’re on your ten-yard line, Yale.”
“Hold them!”
“Hold them!”
Two yards at a time, they were rolled back with a mathematical, unfeeling precision.
“Third down; two yards to go!”
“Yale, stop it!”
“Yale!”
And stop it they did, by a bare six inches. Behind the goal-line, Charlie De Soto came up, as he stood measuring his distance for a kick.
“How are you, Dink? Want a bit of a rest﻿—sponge-off?”
“Rest be hanged!”

===Chinese===

普林斯顿展开阵型，朝班格斯那侧边锋卷过去，压回四码，随即又朝另一侧长扫一记，拿下首攻。

募地，后场里猫一样盯着的斯托弗叫着抢前一步。远远的一侧，一名普林斯顿后卫不声不响正弯着腰，装作系鞋带。

「小心——左路小心！」

他的喊声来得太迟。普林斯顿四分卫横着一记长传，二十码，落到那个装闲的半卫手里，此人接球便向阵地深处奔去，早越过了争球线。

一名普林斯顿锋线队员想拦截，斯托弗把他甩到一边，不停步，全速迎上那人——那家伙毫不退缩，直冲他的擒抱而来。几乎是正面对撞，这一震把斯托弗撞懵了，本能地抱死对手，球却脱了手，达纳扑上去按住。

「我的老天，这记擒抱！」里根的嗓门。「骨头断没断？」

「当然没有。」他粗声说。

有人非要用海绵给他擦脸，叫他好不厌烦。

「那家伙怎么样？」他狠狠地问。

「那是块硬骨头；他也起来了！」

「他得起得来。」

夺回球给了他们片刻喘息，却也激怒了对面那条防线，它轰然而起，把接下来的几波进攻彻底闷死。他那一脚又像擦着了普林斯顿锋线伸长的指尖，他发出一声怪笑——这笑声他记了很久。

这一轮踢球对轰重新开始，直到深入耶鲁腹地，科克雷尔环顾一声，打出进攻的信号。

「上啊耶鲁，拦住它，拦住它！」丁克自言自语。

可那波进攻拦不住。他无能为力，不敢近前，眼看着蓝线一次又一次弯折，橙黑大军机器般稳步碾上来。越过二十五码线，又过了二十码线。

「哦，耶鲁，你们还让他们再得分吗？」德索托在尖叫。

「你们已经在本方十码线上了，耶鲁。」

「顶住！」

「顶住！」

一次两码，他们被一种数学般精确、毫无怜悯的节奏碾退。

「第三次进攻；还差两码！」

「耶鲁，拦住！」

「耶鲁！」

他们真拦住了，只差着可怜的六英寸。球门线后，他正量着踢球的步点，查利·德索托走过来。

「怎么样，丁克？要不要歇口气——擦把脸？」

「歇个鬼！」

he said fiercely. “Come on with that ball.”
Suddenly, instead of kicking low and off to the right, he sent the ball straight down the field with every ounce of strength he could put in it. A great roar went up from the Yale stands, fired by the spirit of resistance.
Thereafter it had all a grim sameness, except, in a strange way, it seemed to him that nothing that had gone before counted﻿—that everything they were fighting for was to keep their goal-line inviolate. Nothing new seemed to happen. When he went fiercely into a melee, finding his man somehow, or felt the rush of bodies about him as he managed each time to get clear his punt, he had the same feeling:
“Why, I’ve done this before.”
A dozen times they stopped the Princeton advance, sometimes far away and sometimes near, once within the five-yard line. Every moment, now, someone cried wearily:
“What’s the time?”
The gray of November twilights, the haze that settles over the struggles of the gridiron like the smoke of a battlefield, began to close in. And then a sudden fumble, a blocked kick, and by a swift turn of luck it was Yale’s ball for the first time in Princeton’s territory. One or two subs came rushing in eagerly from the side lines. Everyone was talking at once:
“What’s the time?”
“Five minutes more.”
“Get together, Yale!”
“Show ’em how!”
“Ram it through them!”
“Here’s our chance!”
Stover, beside himself, ran up to De Soto and flung his arms about his neck, whispering in his ear:
“Give me a chance﻿—you must give me a chance! Send me through Regan!”
He got his signal, and went into the breach with every nerve set, fighting his way behind the great bulk of Regan for a good eight yards. A second time he was called on, and broke the line for another first down.
Regan was transformed. All his calm had gone. He loomed in the line like a Colossus, flinging out his arms, shouting:
“We’re rotten, are we? Carry it right down the field, boys!”
Everyone caught the infection. De Soto, with his hand to his mouth, was shouting hoarsely, through the bedlam of cheers, his gleeful slogan:
“We don’t want to live forever, boys! What do we care? We’ve got to face Yale after this. Never mind your necks. We’ve got the doctors! A little more murder, now!

此后的一切都是同一种惨烈的重复，只有一点古怪的不同：在他感觉里，先前的一切都不作数了——他们拼死争的只剩一件事，守住球门线不失。再没有新鲜事发生。每当他凶狠地扎进乱阵，总能不知怎么逮住自己该防的人；每当他被人潮围着冲撞、又回回把球干干净净踢出去，心里都是同一句话：

十几次，他们掐灭了普林斯顿的推进，有时远，有时近，有一次就在五码线内。这时候，时时刻刻有人有气无力地喊：

「还有多久？」

十一月暮色的灰，那像战场硝烟一样罩在橄榄球场搏杀上的薄霭，开始四合。随后一次突兀的掉球，一记被封的踢球，运气猛地一转，球头一回落进普林斯顿腹地，归了耶鲁。一两名替补急切地从边线冲进场。人人抢着说话：

「还有多久？」

「还有五分钟。」

「给他们看看！」

「机会来了！」

斯托弗忘乎所以，跑上去捾住德索托的脖子，贴着他耳朵说：

「给我个机会——你必须给我个机会！让我从里根身后冲！」

口令来了，他绷紧每根神经扎进缺口，跟在里根那座肉山背后杀出足足八码。第二次点到他，他又凿穿防线，再下一城。

里根脱胎换骨。他那份镇定全没了。他像一尊巨像耸立在线上，张开双臂吼：

「我们是脓包，是吗？把球一路端到底，弟兄们！」

人人都染上了这股热。德索托手拢着嘴，穿过欢呼的鼎沸嘶声喊着他那句得意的口号：

「咱们又不想活一万年，弟兄们！怕什么！这一仗之后咱们还得见耶鲁呢。别心疼脖子。大夫都备好了！再杀狠一点！

===Original===

Shove that ball down that field, Yale! Send them back on stretchers! Nineteen﻿—eight﻿—six﻿—four﻿—Ha‑a‑ard!”
Again and again Stover was called on, and again and again, with his whole team behind him or Regan’s great arm about him, struggling to keep his feet, crawling on his knees, fighting for every last inch, he carried the ball down the field twenty, thirty yards on.
He forgot where he was, standing there with blazing eyes and colorless face. He forgot that he was only the freshman, as he had that night in the wrestling bout. He gave orders, shouted advice, spurred them on. He felt no weariness; nothing could tire him. His chance had come at last. He went into the line each time blubbering, laughing with the fierce joy of it, shouting to himself:
“I’m the weak spot, am I? I’ll show them!”
And the certainty of it all overwhelmed him. Nothing could stop him now. He knew it. He was going to score. He was going to cross that line only fifteen yards away.
“Give me that ball again!” he cried to De Soto.
Then something seemed to go wrong. De Soto and Dudley were shrieking out something, protesting wildly.
“What’s wrong?” he cried.
“They’re calling time on us!”
“No, no, it’s not possible! It’s not time!”
He turned hysterically, beseechingly, catching hold of the referee’s arm, not knowing what he did.
“Mr. Referee, it isn’t time. Mr. Referee﻿—”
“Game’s over,” said Captain Dana’s still voice. “Get together, Yale. Cheer for Princeton now. Make it a good one!”
But no one heard them in the uproar that suddenly went up. Nature could not hold out; the disappointment had been too severe. Stover stood with his arms on Regan’s shoulders, and together they bowed their heads and went choking through the crowd. Others rushed around him﻿—he thought he heard Tompkins saying something. He seemed lost in the crowd that stared at him, struggling to hold back his grief. Only one figure stood out distinctly﻿—the figure of a white-haired man, who took off his hat to him as he went through the barrier, and shouted something unintelligible﻿—a strangely excited white-haired man.
All the way back to the gymnasium, through the jubilant street, Dink sat staring out unseeing, his eyes blurred, a great lump in his throat, possessed by a fatigue such as he had never known before. No one spoke.

他忘了自己在哪儿，站在那里，两眼喷火，脸上没了血色。他忘了自己只是个新生，就像那晚摔跤场上一样。他发号令，喊着主意，催他们向前。他不觉得累；什么都累不倒他。他的机会终于来了。每一回冲进人墙，他都在嘶喊，都在为这凶猛的快乐大笑，心里喊着：

「我是软肋，是吗？我让他们看看！」

一股笃定把他整个人淹没了。现在什么都拦不住他。他知道。他要得分了。他要冲过那条线了，只隔着十五码。

「再给我球！」他朝德索托喊。

随后有什么不对了。德索托和达德利在尖声嚷着什么，狂乱地抗议。

「怎么了？」他喊。

「他们叫停我们了！」

「不，不，不可能！还没到时间！」

他歇斯底里地转过身，哀哀地一把抓住裁判的胳膊，不知道自己在干什么。

「裁判先生，还没到时间。裁判先生——」

「比赛结束了，」达纳队长静静的声音说。「集合，耶鲁。现在为普林斯顿欢呼。拿出像样的一声！」

可在那骤然炸开的喧嚣里，谁也没听见。血肉之躯撑不住了；这失望太重。斯托弗把胳膊搭在里根肩上，两人一齐垂着头，哽咽着挤出人群。别人围着他跑——他恍惚听见汤普金斯在说什么。他像迷失在一圈盯着他看的人里，拼命忍着那股悲。只有一个人影清清楚楚——一个白头发的男人，在他穿过围栏时朝他脱帽，喊着一句听不清的话——一个激动得出奇的白发人。

一路回健身房，穿过欢腾的街道，丁克坐着向外望，视而不见，两眼模糊，喉咙里堵着一大团，浑身是一种他从未领教过的疲惫。没有人说话。

Through his own brain ceaselessly the score, strangely jumbled, went its tiring way:
“Eighteen to nothing﻿—to nothing! Eighteen to six﻿—it should have been eighteen to six. Eighteen to nothing. It’s awful﻿—awful! If I only could punt!”
His ideal, his dream of a Yale team, had always been of victory, not like this, to go down powerless, swept aside, routed﻿—to such a defeat!
Then he shut his eyes, fighting over again those last desperate rushes against defeat, against hope, against time, unable to believe it was over.
“How many times did I take that ball?” he thought wearily. “Was it seven or eight? If I’d only got free that last time﻿—kept my feet!”
He remembered flashes of that last frenzy﻿—the face of a Princeton rusher who reached for him and missed, the teeth savage as a wolf’s and the strained mouth. He saw again Regan turning around to pull him through, Regan, the brute, raging like a fury. He remembered the quick, strange white looks that Charlie De Soto had given him, wondering each time if he had the strength to go on. Why had they stopped them? They had a right to that last rally!
“Eighteen to nothing. Poor Dana﻿—I wonder what he’ll do?”
He remembered, in a far-off way, tales he had heard of other captains, disgraced by defeat, breaking down, leaving college, disappearing. He dreaded the moment when they should break silence, when the awful thing must be talked over, there in the gymnasium, feeling acutely all the misery and ache Dana must be feeling.
“All right there, Stover? Let yourself go, if you want to.”
The voice was Tompkins’, who was looking up at him anxiously, the gymnasium at his back.
“All right,” he said gruffly, raising himself with an effort and half slipping to the ground.
“Sure? How’s Dudley?”
He realized in a curious way that others, too, had gone through the game. Then Regan’s arm was around him. He did not put it from him, grateful for any support in his weakness. Together they went through the crowd of ragamuffins staring open-mouthed at a defeated team.
“What’s the matter with Dudley?”
“Played through all the last with a couple of broken ribs.”
“Dudley?”
“Yes. Go as slow as you want, old bantam.”
“If we only could have had another minute, Tom﻿—” He stopped, unable to go on, shaking his head.
“I know, I know.”
“It was tough.”
“Darned tough.”
“I thought we were going to do it.”
“Now, you shut up, young rooster.

那个比分，乱七八糟地，在他脑子里无休无止地打着疲惫的转：

「十八比零——零！十八比六——本该是十八比六的。十八比零。太惨了——太惨了！要是我会踢球该多好！」

他的理想，他的耶鲁之梦，从来都是胜利，绝不是这样——毫无还手之力地倒下，被扫开，被击溃——败成这样！

随后他闭上眼，把最后那几次绝望的冲锋重新打了一遍——对抗败局，对抗希望，对抗时间——不能相信一切已经完了。

「我抱了多少次球？」他疲惫地想。「七次还是八次？最后一回我要是挣脱了——站住了——该多好！」

他记得最后那阵疯狂里的几个闪影——一张普林斯顿冲锋者的脸，伸手抓他落了空，牙齿狼一般凶，嘴绷得变形。他又看见里根回身把他从人堆里拽出来，里根那头蛮牛，怒得像尊怒神。他记得查利·德索托投给他的那些急促的、异样的白眼，一回一回地在掂量：他还有没有力气再冲。他们凭什么叫停？最后那一搏是他们的权利！

「十八比零。可怜的达纳——他会怎么样呢？」

他恍恍惚惚记起听来的那些往事：别的队长，被败局辱没了，垮掉，退学，销声匿迹。他怕那一刻——他们打破沉默，在健身房里把那桩惨事摊开来讲——他尖锐地感到达纳此刻必在受着的全部苦与痛。

「没事吧，斯托弗？想哭就哭出来。」

是汤普金斯的嗓音，他正仰头忧心地看着他，身后就是健身房。

===Original===

Don’t think of it any more. You played like a fiend. We’re proud of you.”
“Poor Dana!”
Upstairs a couple of rubbers took charge of him, stripping him and rubbing him rigorously. Two or three coaches came up to him, gripping him with silent grips, patting him on the back. The cold bite of the shower brought back some of his vitality, and he dressed mechanically with the squad, who had nothing to say to one another.
“Yale, I want to talk to you boys a moment.”
He looked up. In the center of the room was Rivers, coach of coaches, around whom the traditions of football had been formed. Stover looked at him dully, wondering how he could stand there filled with such energy.
“Now, boys, the game’s over. We’ve lost. It’s our turn; we’ve got to stand it. One thing I want you to remember when you go out of here. *Yale teams take their medicine!*”
His voice rose to a nervous staccato, and the sharp, cold eye seemed to look into every man, just as at school the Doctor used to awe them.
“Do you understand? Yale teams take their medicine! No talking, no reasoning, no explanations, no excuses, and no criticism! The thing’s over and done. We’ll have a dinner tonight, and we’ll start in on next year; and next year nothing under the sun’s going to stop us! Go out; take off your hats! A great Princeton team licked you﻿—licked you well! That’s all. You deserved to score. You didn’t. Hard luck. But those who saw you try for it won’t forget it! We’re proud of that second half! No talk, now, about what might have happened; no talk about what you’re going to do. Shut up! Remember﻿—grin and take your medicine.”
“Mr. Rivers, I’d like to say a few words.”
Stover, with almost a feeling of horror, saw Dana step forward quietly, purse his lips, look about openly, and say:
“Mr. Rivers, I understand what you mean, and what’s underneath it all, and I thank you for it. At the same time, it’s up to me to take the blame, and I’m not going to dodge it. I’ve been a poor captain. I thought I knew more than you did, and I didn’t. I’ve made one fool blunder after another. But I did it honestly. Well, that doesn’t matter﻿—let that go. I say this because it’s right, too, I should take my medicine, and because I don’t want next year’s captain to botch the job the way I’ve done. And now, just a word to you men.

别再想了。你打得像头恶鬼。我们为你骄傲。」

「可怜的达纳！」

楼上，两名按摩师接手，剥了他的衣服，狠狠地给他搓揉。两三位教练走过来，无言地攋攋他，拍拍他的背。冷水淋浴那记冰凉的咬，唤回他几分生气，他同全队一样机械地穿衣，人人相对无言。

「耶鲁，我要跟你们说几句话。」

他抬头。屋子中央站着里弗斯（Rivers），教练中的教练，橄榄球的传统就是围着他立起来的。斯托弗呆呆望着他，奇怪他怎么能站在那里，浑身是那样的劲。

「好了，弟兄们，球打完了。我们输了。轮到我们；就得受着。出门的时候，我要你们记住一件事。*耶鲁的球队认下这剂苦药！*」

嗓音升成神经质的断奏，那又利又冷的眼睛仿佛看进每个人心里，正如当年学校里「博士」震住他们的样子。

「明白吗？耶鲁的球队认下这剂苦药！不议论，不论理，不解释，不找借口，不互相指责！事情完了就完了。今晚我们吃顿饭，明天就开始为来年练；来年，天底下没有什么拦得住我们！出去；脱帽！一支伟大的普林斯顿队打败了你们——打得漂亮！就这些。你们本该得分。没有得分。运气不好。可看见你们拼过的人，忘不了！我们为那下半场骄傲！现在，不许谈什么假如；也不许谈你们将来要怎样。闭嘴！记住——咧开嘴，把药咽下去。」

「里弗斯先生，我想说几句。」

斯托弗近乎惊骇地看着达纳静静上前，抿了抿嘴，坦然环视四周，说：

「里弗斯先生，我明白您的意思，也明白这底下的分量，我谢谢您。同时，这罪责该我来担，我也不打算躲。我当了个差劲的队长。我自以为比你们懂，其实不懂。我犯了一个又一个蠢错。但我都是诚心犯的。好，这不要紧——不提了。我说这些，也因为这是对的，我也该咽下我的药，还因为我不愿明年的队长把这份差事办砸成我这样。最后，对你们大家说一句。

===Original===

You’ve done everything I asked you to do, and kept your mouths shut, no matter what you thought of it. You’ve been loyal, and you’ll be loyal, and there’ll be no excuses outside. But I want you men to know that I’ll remember it, and I want to thank you. That’s all.”
Instantly there was a buzz of voices, and one clear note dominating it﻿—Regan’s voice, stirred beyond thought of self:
“Boys, we’re going to give that captain a cheer. Are you ready? Hip﻿—hip!”
Somehow the cry that went up took from Dink all the sting of defeat. He went out, head erect, back to meet his college, no longer shrinking from the ordeal, proud of his captain, proud of his coach, and proud of a lesson he had learned bigger than a victory.

屋里顿时嗡的一声，一个声音压过一切——里根的嗓音，激动得忘了自己：

「弟兄们，咱们给这位队长来一嗓子。预备好了吗？嘿——嘿！」

不知怎么，这一声冲天而起的呐喊，把败绩的刺整个从丁克心里拔走了。他昂着头走出去，回去见他的学校，不再畏那一场过堂——为他的队长骄傲，为他的教练骄傲，也为一堂比胜利更大的课骄傲。

## XI / 十一
===Original===

After the drudgery of the football season he had a few short weeks of gorgeous idleness, during which he browsed through a novel a day, curled up on his window-seat, rolling tobacco clouds through the fog of smokers in the room. He had won his spurs and the right to lounge, and he looked forward eagerly to the rest of the year as a time for reading and the opening up of the friendships of which he had dreamed.
Old age settled down rapidly upon him, and at eighteen that malady appears in its most virulent form. Perhaps there was a little justification. The test he had gone through had educated him to self-control in its most difficult form. He was not simply the big man of the class, the first to emerge to fame, but the prospective captain of a future Yale eleven. A certain gravity was requisite﻿—moreover, it was due the University. To have seen the burning letters S-T-O-V-E-R actually vibrating on the front pages of metropolitan papers, to have gazed on his distinguished (though slightly smudged) features, ruined by an unfeeling photographer, but disputing nevertheless the public attention with statesmen and champions of the pugilistic ring﻿—to have felt these heavenly sensations at the age of eighteen could not be lightly disguised.
So he lay back among welcome cushions, book in hand, and listened with a tolerant ear to the rapid-fire comedy of McNab and Buck Waters. He stayed much in his own room, which became a sort of lounging spot where the air was always blue with smoke and a mandolin or guitar was strumming a low refrain or a group near the fireplace was noisy with the hazards of the national game.
Pretty much everyone of importance in the class dropped in on him. The preliminary visiting period of the sophomore societies was nearly over. With the opening of the winter term the hold-offs and elections would begin. He understood that those who were uncertain wished the advantage of being seen in his company﻿—that his, in fact, was now the “right” crowd.
He intended to call on several men who interested him: Brockhurst, who had made his appearance with a story in the *Lit* which announced him as a possible future chairman; Gimbel, about whose opinions and sincerity he was in doubt; and, above all, Regan, who genuinely attracted him.

===Chinese===

熬过橄榄球赛季的苦役，他得了短短几个星期的、金光灿烂的清闲：一天一本小说，蜷在窗座上啉，在满屋烟客的雾里，一口口滚着烟云。他挣下了马刺，也挣下了瘫坐的权利；他热切地盼着这一年剩下的日子，当作读书的时辰、当作把他梦寐中的友谊一扇扇打开的时辰。
老境迅速罩上他——十八岁上，这毛病发得最烈。也许有一点道理。他捱过的那场考验，把他教到最难的一种自制。他不单是班里的头号人物、头一个出名的，还是未来耶鲁十一人队队长的候补。几分持重是必须的——再说，这也是对大学应尽的礼数。亲眼见过 S-T-O-V-E-R 几个火辣辣的字母当真在各大报头版上颤动，亲眼端详过自己那张名流相（虽然有点花）——被一位没心没肺的摄影师糟蹋了，却仍在同政治家和拳击场冠军争夺公众的注目——十八岁上尝过这等登天的滋味，是装不出淡泊的。
于是他往迎人的软垫里一靠，手里一本书，带着宽容的耳朵，听麦克纳布和巴克·沃特斯连珠炮的滑稽戏。他多半待在自己屋里，屋子成了一处闲窝：空气永远叫烟熏得发蓝，一把曼陀林或吉他低低拨着叠句，壁炉边一伙人正为国民游戏的输赢闹得欢。
班里要紧的人物几乎都来看过他。二年级会社的初访期近尾声。冬学期一开，观望与选举就要登场。他明白，那些拿不定的人，巴望着让人看见同他在一起的好处——事实上，他这一伙如今就是「对」的一伙。
他打算去回访几个他有兴趣的：布罗克赫斯特（Brockhurst），在《文学》上用一篇小说亮了相，俨然是未来主席的苗子；金贝尔，他的意见和真心，斯托弗还拿不准；还有顶要紧的里甘，那人当真吸引他。

===Original===

But, somehow, having now nothing to do, his afternoons and evenings seemed always filled, and he continually postponed until the morrow what suggested itself during the day. Besides, there was a complacent delight in being his own master again and of looking forward to such a period of independent languor.
The first discordant note to intrude itself upon this ideal was a remark of Le Baron’s during one of the evening visits. These embassies were always conducted with punctiliousness and gravity. The inquisitorial sophomores arrived about eight o’clock in groups of three and four. As McCarthy was the object of attention from a different society, Stover, when the former’s inspectors arrived, shook hands gravely, and shortly discovered that he had a letter to post at the corner. When the committee on Stover appeared trimly at the door, McCarthy rose at once to return a hypothetical book, after which the conversation began with about as much spontaneity and zest as would be permitted to a board of alienists sitting in judgment on a victim. The sophomores were embarrassed with their own impromptu dignity, and the freshmen at the constraint of their superiors.
On one such occasion, after the committee of four had spent fifteen minutes in the grave discussion of a kindergarten topic, and had filed out with funereal solemnity, Le Baron returned for a more intimate conversation.
Since the night of his introduction to college, Stover had had only occasional glimpses of Le Baron. True, he was generally of the visiting committee that called every other night for perfunctory inspection, but through it all the sophomore had adopted an attitude of almost defensive aloofness and impartiality.
“I want to talk over some of the men in the class,” said Le Baron, falling into an armchair and picking up a pipe, while his manner changed to naturalness and equality. Stover understood at once that the attitude was a notice served on him of the security of his own position.
“Dink, I want to know your opinion. What do you think of Brockhurst, for instance?”
“Brockhurst? Why, I hardly know him.”
“Is he liked?”
“Why, yes.”
“Who are his friends?”
Stover thought a moment.
“Why, I think he rather keeps to himself. He strikes me as being﻿—well, a little undeveloped﻿—rather shy.”
“Do you like him?”
“I do.”
“And Schley?”

===Chinese===

可是不知怎么，如今没事可干，下午和晚上倒总排得满满的，白天想起的拜访，总叫他推到明天。再说，重新当上自己的主人，指望这样一段自在的闲散，里头自有一种惬意的甜。
头一个撞进这幅理想图景的不谐和音，是勒巴龙某晚来访时的一句话。这类使馆式的拜访，永远办得一板一眼、庄重其事。问案的二年级生七八点钟三三两两到来。麦卡锡是另一家会社的注意对象，所以他那边的视察官一到，斯托弗便郑重握手，不多时就发现自己有一封信要送到街角去寄。轮到斯托弗的委员会衣冠齐整地出现在门口，麦卡锡立刻起身去还一本假设中借来的书；随后谈话开场，那份自然和兴致，同一班精神鉴定专家围坐评审一个病人所被容许的差不多。二年级生被自己临时的威严弄得发窘，新生被上头的拘束弄得发窘。
这样的一回，四位委员就一个幼儿园题目郑重讨论了十五分钟，出殡似的鱼贯而出之后，勒巴龙折返回来，作一番更贴己的谈话。
自开学头一夜被引见之后，斯托弗只在偶尔几眼里见到勒巴龙。不错，隔晚到访、例行视察的委员会里常有他，可从头到尾，这位二年级生摆着一副近乎设防的不偏不倚。
「我想谈谈班里几个人，」勒巴龙说着坐进扶手椅，拿起一只烟斗，神态也换成自然和平等。斯托弗立刻明白：这一姿态是给他递话——他自己的位置稳了。
「丁克，我想听听你的意见。比如，你看布罗克赫斯特怎么样？」
「布罗克赫斯特？哦，我跟他不大熟。」
「他人缘怎么样？」
「哦，不错。」
「他有哪些朋友？」
斯托弗想了一想。
「哦，我看他挺独来独往的。给我的印象是——嗯，有点没长开——相当腼腆。」
「你喜欢他吗？」
「喜欢。」
「那么施莱呢？」

===Original===

The question was put abruptly, Le Baron raising his eyes to get his answer from Stover’s face.
“Schley?” said Dink, considering a little. “Why, Schley seems to﻿—”
“Regan?” said Le Baron, satisfied.
“One of the best in the class!”
“He seems a rather rough diamond.”
“He’s proud as Lucifer﻿—but he has more to him than anyone I know.”
“It’s a question what he’ll do.”
“I’d back him every time.”
“You are quite enthusiastic about him,” said Le Baron, looking at him with a little quizzical surprise.
“He’s a man,” said Stover stoutly.
“Of course, the football captaincy will probably be between you two.”
“Regan?” said Stover, amazed.
“Either you or Regan.”
Stover had never thought of him as a rival for his dearest ambition. He remained silent, digesting the possibility, aware of Le Baron’s searching inquiry.
“Of course, you have nine chances out of ten, but the race is a long one.”
“He would make a good captain,” Stover said slowly.
“You think so?”
“I hadn’t thought of it before,” Stover said, with a sudden falling inside, “but he has the stuff in him of a leader all right.”
“I wish he weren’t quite so set,” said Le Baron. “He hasn’t made a particularly favorable impression on some of the fellows.”
An involuntary smile came to Stover at the thought of Regan’s probable reception of a committee of inspection.
“He doesn’t perhaps realize the importance of some things,” he said carefully.
“He doesn’t,” said Le Baron, who was not without a sense of humor. “It’s a pity, though, for his sake. I wish you’d talk to him a little.”
“I will.”
Le Baron rose.
“By the way, what are you going out for this spring?”
“This spring?” said Stover, surprised.
“Ever rowed any?”
“Never.”
“That doesn’t make any difference. You learn the stroke quicker﻿—no bad habits.”
“I’m light as mischief.”
“Oh, I don’t know﻿—not for the freshman. We want to stimulate the interest in rowing up here. It’s a good example for a man like you to come out. Ever done anything in baseball or the track?”
“No.”
“Rowing’s the stunt for you.” He went toward the door, and turned. “Have a little chat with Regan. I admire the fellow, but he needs to rub up a bit with you fellows and get the sharp edges off him. By the way, when you start rowing I’ll get hold of you and give you a little extra coaching.”

===Chinese===

问题来得突兀，勒巴龙抬起眼，要从斯托弗脸上取他的答案。
「施莱？」丁克说，斟酌了一下。「哦，施莱好像——」
「里甘呢？」勒巴龙说，满意了。
「班里最好的人之一！」
「看着是块没打磨的糙钻石。」
「他骄傲得像卢西弗——可他肚子里的东西，比我认得的谁都多。」
「他将来自处如何，是个问题。」
「我每回都押他赢。」
「你对他倒热乎得很，」勒巴龙说，带一点打趣的惊异望着他。
「他是条汉子，」斯托弗理直气壮。
「当然，橄榄球队长，多半就在你们两个中间出。」
「里甘？」斯托弗说，愣了。
「不是你，就是里甘。」
斯托弗从没把他当作自己最心头那个志向的对手。他沉默着，消化这个可能，觉出勒巴龙那道刨根的审视。
「当然，你十成里占着九成，可这条路长着呢。」
「他会是个好队长，」斯托弗慢慢地说。
「你这么想？」
「我先前没想过，」斯托弗说，心里忽然一沉，「可他身上确实有当领袖的料。」
「但愿他别那么犟，」勒巴龙说。「他在几个人那儿没落下什么特别好的印象。」
