I haven’t thought on a single subject, and as for politics, and what you men talk about, I don’t know the slightest thing. I say, Tom, I’d like to come around and talk with you.”
“Come,” said Regan; “I’ve had the door on the latch for a long while, old rooster.”

===Chinese===

我没有就任何一件东西思想过；至于政治，还有你们谈的那些，我一星半点也不懂。我说，汤姆，我想常来，同你们谈谈。」
「来，」里甘说；「门闩我早替你拔了好久了，老公鸡。」

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

The next afternoon Stover passed Brockhurst going to dinner.
“Hello,” he said, with a cordial wave of the hand.
“Hello,” said Brockhurst, with a little avoidance, for he had a certain physical timidity, which always shrank at the consequences of his mental insurgency.
“I was a chump and a fool last night,” said Stover directly, “and here’s my apology.”
“Oh, all right.”
“Drop in on me. Talk things over. You’ve started me thinking. Drop in﻿—I mean it.”
“Thanks, awfully.”
Brockhurst, ill at ease, moved away, pursued always by a shackling self-consciousness in the presence of those to whom he consciously felt he was mentally superior.
One direct result came to Stover from the visit to Swazey’s rooms. Despite the protests and arguments, he did not report for the competition for the crew.
“Stay in for a couple of months,” said Le Baron. “We want the moral effect of everyone’s coming out.”
“Sorry; I’ve made up my mind,” said Dink.
“Why?”
“Want time to myself. I’ve never had it, and now I’m going to get it.”
Le Baron of the machine did not understand him, and he did not explain. Stover was essentially a man of action and not a thinker. He did not reason things out for himself, but when he became convinced he acted. So, when he had thought over Brockhurst’s theories and admitted that he was not independent, he determined at once to be so. He began zealously, turning his back on his own society crowd, to seek out the members of his class whom he did not know, resolved that his horizon should be of the freest. For the first time he began to reason on what others said to him. He went often to Swazey’s rooms, and Regan’s, which were centers of discussion. Some of the types that drifted in were incongruous, bizarre, flotsam and jetsam of the class; but in each, patiently resolved, he found something to stir the imagination; and when, under Regan’s quickening influence, he stopped to consider what life in the future would mean to them, he began to understand what his friend, the invincible democrat, meant by the inspiring opportunity of college﻿—the vision of a great country that lay on the lips of the men he had only to seek out.
Dink was of too direct a nature and also too confident in the strength of his position to consider the effect of his sudden pilgrimage to what was called the “outsiders.”

===Chinese===

第二天下午，斯托弗在去吃饭的路上碰见布罗克赫斯特。

「你好。」他热络地一挥手。

「你好。」布罗克赫斯特说，带着几分躲闪——他身上有一种体格上的怯，每逢自己精神上的造反要承担后果时，这份怯总要缩一缩。

「昨晚我又蠢又傻，」斯托弗直截了当地说，「这儿补上我的道歉。」

「哦，没事。」

「来我这儿坐坐。聊聊事。是你让我开始想事了。来——说真的。」

「太感谢了。」

布罗克赫斯特不自在地走开了——每逢站在那些他自觉智力高其一等的人面前，一副捆人手脚的自我意识总要缠着他。

造访斯韦齐的屋子，给斯托弗带来一个直接后果：任凭人家又劝又争，他没有去赛艇选拔报到。

「先留两三个月，」勒巴龙说。「我们要的是人人都到场的那股声势。」

「抱歉；我主意定了。」丁克说。

「为什么？」

「想要属于自己的时间。我从没有过，现在我要拿到它。」

身为机器一员的勒巴龙不懂他，他也不解释。斯托弗骨子里是行动的人，不是思想的人。他不惯自己把事情推演明白；可一旦信了，他就干。于是，等他把布罗克赫斯特的理论想了一遍，承认自己并不独立，他当即拿定主意要独立。他热切地干起来——背对自己会社的圈子，去寻访班里那些他不认识的人，铁了心要把眼界放到最宽。头一回，他开始琢磨别人对他说的话。他常去斯韦齐的屋，也常去里根的——两处都是议论的中心。有些飘进来的角色不伦不类、稀奇古怪，是班里的浮货和漂木；可每一个，只要耐着性子看进去，他都能找到一点搅动想象的东西；而当他在里根那股催人奋发的感染下，停下来想想往后的生活对这些人意味着什么，他开始懂得他那位朋友——那位不可战胜的民主派——说大学的激励人的机会是什么意思了：一个伟大国家的图景，就悬在那些人的唇边，他只消去寻访。

丁克性子太直，对自己地位的稳固又太自信，压根没去想这趟突兀的「圈外人」朝圣会招来什么效应。

===Original===

Swazey and Pike, at his invitation, took to dropping into his room and working out their lessons with him. Quite unconsciously, he found himself constantly in public companionship with them and other newly discovered types who interested him.
About two weeks after this new life had begun, Le Baron stopped him one day, with a little solicitous frown, saying:
“Look here, Dink, aren’t you cutting loose from your own crowd a good deal?”
“Why, yes, I guess I am,” Dink announced, quite unconsciously.
“I wouldn’t get identified too much with﻿—well, with some of the fellows you’ve taken up.”
Stover smiled, and went his way undisturbed. For the first time he felt his superiority over Le Baron. Le Baron could not know what he knew﻿—that it was just these new acquaintances who had waked him up out of his torpor and made a thinking being of him. Others in his class, mistaking his motives, began to twit him:
“I say, Dink, what are you out for?”
“Running for something?”
“Getting into politics?”
“Junior Prom, eh?”
He turned the jests aside with jests as ready, quite unaware that in his own crowd he was arousing a little antagonism; for he was developing in such deep lines that he did not perceive vexing details.
All at once he remembered that it had been over a fortnight since he had called at the Storys’ and he ran over one afternoon about four o’clock, expecting to stay for dinner; for the Judge kept open house to the friends of his son, and Stover had readily availed himself of the privilege to become intimate.
Although Bob Story was bound to him by the closest social ties, Dink felt, nor was he altogether at fault in the feeling, that the brother was still on the defensive with him, due to a natural resentment perhaps at Dink’s too evident interest in his sister.
When he arrived at the old colonial house set back among the elms, Eliza, the maid, informed him that no one was at home. Miss Jean was out riding. But immediately she corrected herself, and, going upstairs to make sure, returned with the welcome information that Miss Story had just returned and begged him to wait.
He took the request as a meager evidence of her interest, and entered the drawing-room.

===Chinese===

应他的邀请，斯韦齐和派克开始常到他屋里来，同他一起喬功课。全不自觉间，他发现自己总同他们俩、同其他新发现的、让他来劲的角色公开走在一起。

这新生活开了头约莫两星期，一天勒巴龙拦住他，眉头微蹙，一脸操心：

「我说，丁克，你是不是甩开自己那拨人甩得有点过了？」

「呢，是，大概吧。」丁克答，浑然不觉。

「我不愿你同——嗯，同你近来搭上的某些人绑得太紧。」

斯托弗一笑，安然走自己的路。头一回，他感到了自己对勒巴龙的高出一头。勒巴龙不可能知道他所知道的——恰恰是这些新交，把他从昏沉里叫醒，把他做成了一个会思考的人。班里旁的人错会了他的动机，开始打趣他：

「我说，丁克，你要拼什么去？」

「竞选什么？」

「搞政治了？」

「三年级舞会，嗯？」

他用同样现成的玩笑把这些玩笑挡回去，全不知自己那个圈子里正被惹起一点敌意；他长得太深了，那些硌牙的细节一概看不见。

募地他想起，已有两个多星期没上斯托里家了，便在一个下午四点钟光景跑了去，打算蹭顿晚饭——老法官对儿子的朋友们向来敞门迎客，斯托弗也乐得借着这份体面登堂入室。

虽说鲍勃·斯托里同他被最紧的社交纽带拴在一处，丁克却感到——这感觉也不算全错——当哥哥的对他仍存着戒备，兴许是天性里的一点抵触，恼他对妹妹的心思太过露骨。

他到了那栋退在榆树丛后的殖民地老宅，女仆伊莉莎告诉他，家里没人。琼小姐骑马出去了。可话音未落她又改了口，上楼去确认，回来时带着一个可喜的消息：斯托里小姐刚好回来了，请他稍等。

他把这句「请等」当成她心意的一星半点证据，进了客厅。

===Original===

Waiting there for her to come tripping down the stairs, he began to think of the new horizon that had opened to him, and the new feeling of maturity; and, feeling this with an acute realization, he was impatient for her to come, that he might tell her.
It was a good ten minutes before he turned suddenly at a rustling on the stairs, and saw her, fresh and flushed from the ride.
“It’s awfully good of you to wait,” she called to him. “I did my best to rush.”
Arrived on the landing, she gave him her hand, looking at him a little earnestly.
“How are you? You’re a terrible stranger.”
“Have I been very bad?” he said, holding her hand.
“Indeed you have. Even Bob said he hardly saw you. What have you been doing?”
She withdrew her hand gently, but stood before him, looking into his face with her frank, inquiring eyes. Stover wondered if she thought he’d been a trifle wild; and, as there was no justification, he was immensely flattered, and a little tempted dramatically to assume an attitude that would call for reform. He smiled and said:
“I’ve been on a voyage of discovery, that’s all. You’ll be interested.”
They sat down, and he began directly to talk, halting in broken phrases at first, gradually finding his words as he entered his subject.
“By George! I’ve had a wonderful two weeks﻿—a revelation﻿—just as though﻿—just as though I’d begun my college course; that’s really what it means. All I’ve done before doesn’t count. And to think, if it hadn’t been for an accident, I might have gone on without ever waking up.”
He recounted his visit to Swazey’s rooms, drawing a picture of his self-satisfied self descending *en prince* to bestow a favor; and, warming out of his stiffness, drew a word picture of Swazey’s telling his story before the fire, and the rough sentiment with which he brought forth the odd, common little tintypes.
“By George! the fellow had told a great story and he didn’t know it; but I knew it, and it settled me,” he added with earnestness, always aware of her heightened attention. “It was a regular knockout blow to the conceited, top-heavy, prancing little ass who had gone there. By Jove, it gave me a jar. I went out ashamed.”
“It is a very wonderful life﻿—simple, wonderful,” she said slowly, thinking more of the relator than of the story. “I understand all you felt.”
“You know life’s real to those fellows,” he continued, with more animation.

===Chinese===

等她下楼的那会儿，他开始想那道向他敞开的新地平线，想那股新生的成熟感；这份感觉体味得越尖，就越等不及她来，好讲给她听。

足足十分钟后，楼梯上一阵窜窣，他霍然回头，看见她——骑马归来，鲜润，红扑扑。

「你肯等，真是太好了。」她朝他喊。「我是拼命赶回来的。」

到了楼梯口，她把手伸给他，带着一点认真看他。

「你好吗？你可真成了稀客了。」

「我很不像话吗？」他握着她的手说。

「可不是。连鲍勃都说难得见你。你干什么去了？」

她轻轻抽回手，却站在他面前，用那双坦率的、探究的眼睛望着他的脸。斯托弗琢磨她是不是当他有些野了；既然并无实据，他大大地受用，还有点心痒，想戏剧性地摆出一副需要人规劝的架势来。他一笑，说：

「我出了一趟发现之旅，就这么回事。你会感兴趣的。」

两人坐下，他开门见山讲起来，起先句子断断续续，入了题，话就渐渐找着了路。

「天哪！这两个星期了不起——一场启示——就好像——就好像我这才开始上我的大学；真就是这个意思。从前干的一切都不作数了。想想看，要不是一场偶然，我可能就这么一路睡下去，永远不醒。」

他讲了那趟去斯韦齐屋里的经过，给自己画了幅像：一个自我陶醉的家伙，摆出*王孙驾临*的派头下去赏脸；讲着讲着，僵劲儿化开了，又用工笔描出斯韦齐在炉火前讲故事的样子，和他掏出那几张古里古怪、平平无奇的小铁版相时那份粗粝的深情。

「天哪！那家伙讲了一个了不得的故事，自己却不知道；可我知道，它把我了结了。」他添上一句，一片恳切，始终觉出她那份提起来了的专注。「对那个上门去的、自大的、头重脚轻、蹄子乱刨的小蠢驴，这是结结实实一记勾拳。老天，它把我打醒了。我是红着脸走出来的。」

「那是一种非常了不起的生活——简单，了不起。」她慢慢地说，想的与其说是故事，不如说是讲故事的人。「你感到的一切，我都懂。」

「你知道，对那些家伙，生活是真的。」他接着说，更加活泛。

===Original===

“They’re after something in this world; they believe in something; they’re fighting for something. There’s nothing real in me﻿—that is, there wasn’t. By George, these two weeks that I’ve gone about, looking for the men in the class, have opened up everything to me. I never knew my own country before. It’s a wonderful country! It’s the simple lives that are so wonderful.”
She had in her hand a piece of embroidery, but she did not embroider. Her eyes never left his face. For the first time, the roles were reversed: it was he who talked and she who listened. From time to time she nodded, satisfied at the decision and direction in his character, which had answered the first awakening suggestion.
“Who is Pike?” she asked.
“Pike is a little fellow from a little life in some country town in Indiana; the only one in a family of eight children that’s amounted to anything﻿—father’s a pretty even sort, I guess; so are the rest of them. But this fellow has a dogged persistence﻿—not so quick at thinking things out, but, Lord! how he listens; nothing gets away from him. I can see him growing right under my eyes. He’s interested in politics, same as Regan; wants to go back and get a newspaper some day. He’ll do it, too. Why, that fellow has been racing ahead ever since he came here, and I’ve been standing still. Ricketts is an odd character, a sort of Yankee genius, shrewd, and some of his observations are as sharp as a knife. Brockhurst has the brains of us all; he can out-think us every one. But he’s a spectator; he’s outside looking on. I can’t quite get used to him. Regan’s the fellow I want for a friend. He’s like an old Roman. When he makes up his mind﻿—it takes him a long while﻿—when he does, he’s right.”
He recounted Regan’s ideas on politics﻿—his enthusiasm, and his ideal of a college life that would reflect the thought of the nation.
Then, talking to himself, he began to walk up and down, flinging out quick, stiff gestures:
“Brockhurst states a thing in such a slap-bang way﻿—no compromise﻿—that it hits you at first like a blow. But when you think it over he has generally got to the point. Where he’s wrong is, he thinks the society system here keeps a man wrapped in cotton, smothering him and separating him from the class. Now, I’m an example to the contrary. It’s all a question of the individual. I thought it wasn’t at one moment, but now I know that it is.

===Chinese===

「他们在这世上有所追求；他们信着什么；他们为着什么在战斗。我身上没有一样真的——就是说，从前没有。天哪，这两个星期，我在班里到处找人，一切都朝我打开了。我从前不认识我自己的国家。这是个了不起的国家！了不起的正是那些简单的人生。」

她手里拿着一件刺绣活计，却没有绣。她的眼睛没离开过他的脸。头一回，角色倒了过来：说话的是他，听话的是她。她不时点头，为他性情里那份决断和方向感到满意——那份决断，应了当初头一句唤醒的话。

「派克是谁？」她问。

「派克是个小个子，从印第安纳哪个乡间小镇的一桩小人生里出来；一家八个孩子，成器的就他一个——父亲是个挺平常的人，我估摸；其余几个也是。可这家伙有一股死倔的韧劲——想事情不算快，可是天哪！他那个听法；什么都漏不出去。我眼看着他就在我眼皮底下长。他对政治有兴趣，同里根一样；想哪天回去办一份报纸。他办得成的。你想，这家伙打来了这儿就一路往前赶，我却一直原地站着。里基茨是个怪人，一种扬基天才，精得很，有些话锋利得像刀。布罗克赫斯特脑子比我们所有人都强；论想事，他一个顶我们一群。可他是看客；他站在外头看。我一时还习惯不了他。里根才是我要交的朋友。他像古罗马人。他拿主意——得花他好长时间——可一旦拿了，他就对。」

他讲了里根的政治见解——他的热忱，和他那个理想：大学生活应当映照整个国家的思想。

随后，像是说给自己听，他开始来回踱步，胳膊一甩一甩，动作又快又硬：

「布罗克赫斯特讲一件事，那种直不笼统的讲法——绝不妥协——乍一听像挨了一拳。可你细一琢磨，他多半正扎在点子上。他错的地方在于，他认为这里的会社制度把人裹在棉花里，闷着他，把他同班级隔开。而我，正是个反例。这全看各人。我一度以为不看各人，现在我知道了，就看各人。

===Original===

You can do just what you want﻿—find what you want.
“But we do get so interested in outside things that we forget the real; that’s true. Brockhurst says we ought to bring the college back to the campus, and the more I think of it the more I see what he means. The best weeks, the biggest in my life, are those when I’ve realized I had an imagination and could use it.” Suddenly he halted, gave a quick glance at her, and said:
“Here I’m talking like a runaway horse. I got started.”
“Thank you for talking to me so,” she said eagerly.
He had never seen in her eyes so much of genuine impulse toward him, and, suddenly recalled, in this moment of exhilaration, to the personal self, he was thrilled with a strange thrill at what he saw.
“You remember,” he said, with a certain new boldness, “how impudent you used to be to me, and how furious I was when you told me I was not awake.”
“I remember.”
“Now I understand what you meant,” he said, “but then I didn’t.”
She rose to order tea, and then turned impulsively, smiling up to him.
“I think﻿—I’m sure I felt it would come to you; only I was a little impatient.”
And with a happy look she offered him her hand.
“I’m very glad to be your friend,” she said, to make amends; “and I hope you’ll come and talk over with me all that you are thinking. Will you?”
He did not answer. At the touch of her hand, which he held in his, at the new sound in her voice, suddenly something surged up in him, something blinding, intoxicating, that left him hot and cold, rash and silent. She tried to release her hand, but his grip was not to be denied.
Then, seeing him standing head down boyishly unable to speak or act, she understood.
“Oh, please!” she said, with a sudden weakness, again trying to release her fingers.
“I can’t help it,” he said, blurting out the words. “Jean, you know as well as I what it is. I love you.”
The moment the words were out, he had a cold horror of what had been said. He didn’t love her, not as he had said it. Why had he said it?
She remained motionless a moment, gathering her strength against the shock.
“Please let go my hand,” she said quietly.
This time he obeyed. His mind was a vacuum; every little sound came to him distinctly, with the terror of the blunder he had made.

===Chinese===

「想做什么就做得到——想找什么就找得着。

「可我们确实太操心外头那些虚的，把真的忘了；这是实话。布罗克赫斯特说，应该把大学带回校园里来；我越想越明白他的意思。我这一辈子最好、最大的几个星期，就是我发觉自己有想象力、而且用得上它的那几个。」他忽然刹住，飞快看了她一眼，说：

「瞧我，说起话来像匹脱缰的马。一开了头就收不住。」

「谢谢你这样同我谈。」她恳切地说。

他从没在她眼里见过这么多朝他涌来的真切的情意；在这阵兴奋里猛然被唤回到「自己这个人」上，他为他所见的一切颤了一下，颤得奇怪。

「你还记得吗，」他带着一点新的大胆说，「你从前对我多么没大没小，你说我没醒着的时候，我气成什么样。」

「记得。」

「现在我懂你的意思了。」他说，「那时候不懂。」

她起身要去吩咐上茶，又冲动地转过身，仰头朝他一笑。

「我想——我准是早觉出这一天会到你身上；只是我有点急。」

说着，一脸欢喜，她把手伸给他。

「我很高兴做你的朋友。」她说，算是补方才那点补偿；「我希望你把你想着的一切都来同我谈。好不好？」

他没有答。她的手在他掌心里，她嗓音里那簇新的音色——忽然有什么在他体内涌起，一阵叫人发盲、叫人醺然的东西，把他弄得忽热忽冷，又莽又哑。她想抽回手，可他攋住不放。

随后，见他男孩子似的垂头站着，说不出也动不了，她明白了。

「哦，放手！」她说，忽然没了力气，又试着抽手指。

「我忍不住，」他冲口而出。「琼，这是什么，你同我一样清楚。我爱你。」

话一出口，他先自冷冷地惊了。他并不爱她——至少不是他方才那种说法。他干吗要说？

她一动不动立了片刻，攒起力气来扛这一震。

「请放开我的手。」她静静地说。

这回他依了。脑子里一片真空；每一点细小的声响都清清楚楚地进来，带着他闯下这祸的恐惧。

===Original===

She went to the window and stood, her face half turned from him, trying to think; and, misreading her thoughts, a little warm blood came back to him, and he tried to think what he would say if she came back with a light in her eyes.
“Mr. Stover.”
He looked up abruptly﻿—he had scarcely moved. She was before him, her large eyes seeming larger than ever, her face a little frightened, but serious with the seriousness of the woman looking out.
“You have done a very wrong thing,” she said slowly, “and you have placed me in a very difficult position. I do not want to lose you as a friend.” She made a rapid movement of her fingers to check his exclamation. “If what you said were true, and you are too young to have said such solemn words, may I ask what right you had to say them to me?”
“What right?” he said stupidly.
“Yes, what right,” she repeated, looking at him steadily with a certain wistfulness. “Are you in a position to ask me to be your wife?”
“Let me think a moment,” he said, drawing a breath.
He walked away to the table, leaning his weight on it, while, without moving, she followed with a steady gaze, in which was a little pity.
“Let me help you,” she said at last.
He turned and looked up for the first time, a look of wretchedness.
“It would be too bad that one moment should spoil all our friendship,” she said, “and because that would hurt me I don’t want it so. You are a boy, and I am not yet a woman. I have always respected you, no more so than today, before﻿—before you forgot your respect toward me. I want always to keep the respect I had for you.”
“Don’t say any more,” he said suddenly, with a lump in his throat. “I don’t know why﻿—what﻿—why I forgot myself. Please don’t take away from me your friendship. I will keep it very precious.”
“It is very hard to know what to do,” she said. Then she added, with a little heightening of her color: “My friendship means a great deal.”
He put out his hand and gently took the end of a scarf which she wore about her shoulders, and raised it to his lips. It was a boyish, impulsive fantasy, and he inclined his head before her. Then he went out hurriedly, without speaking or turning, while the girl, pale and without moving, continued to stare at the curtain which still moved with his passing.

===Chinese===

她走到窗前站定，半侧着脸避开他，努力想事情；他错读了她的心思，一点热血回了身，便也开始想：她若眼里带着光走回来，他该说什么。

「斯托弗先生。」

他霍然抬头——他几乎没动过。她立在他面前，那双大眼睛显得比任何时候都大，脸上有一点惊，却是严肃的——一个向外张望的女人的严肃。

「你做了一件很不对的事。」她慢慢地说，「也把我放在了一个非常为难的位子上。我不想失掉你这个朋友。」她手指急急一动，止住他的惊呼。「倘若你说的是真话——你也太年轻，说不出这么郑重的话——那我可以问问，你有什么权利对我说它们？」

「什么权利？」他呆呆地说。

「对，什么权利。」她重复道，定定地看着他，带着一点怅然。「你有资格要我做你的妻子吗？」

「让我想一下。」他说，吸了口气。

他走到桌边，把全身重量压上去；她一动不动，目光稳稳随着他，目光里有一丝怜惜。

「让我帮你。」她终于说。

他转过身，头一回抬起脸，一脸凄惶。

「一分钟的差错毁了我们全部的友谊，那就太糟了。」她说，「正因为那会伤到我，我不愿它这样。你还是个少年，我也还不是个女人。我一向敬你——从来没有比今天、比在——在你忘掉你对我的敬意之前——更敬。我想永远保住我对你那份敬意。」

「别再说了。」他忽然开口，喉咙里哽着一团。「我不知道为什么——怎么——怎么就忘了我自己。请别把你的友谊收回去。我会把它当至宝。」

「眼下该怎么办，实在难说。」她说。随后，脸微微一红，又添一句：「我的友谊，分量很重。」

他伸出手，轻轻拈起她肩上披巾的一角，举到唇边。这是一个少年人的、冲动的痴念，他在她面前低下头去。随后他匆匆走了出去，一言不发，也没有回头；姑娘脸白白的，一动不动，一直望着那幅还在因他经过而晃动的门帘。

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

Stover went rushing from the Storys’ home, and away for a long feverish march along dusky avenues, where unseen leaves came whirling against him. He was humiliated, mortified beyond expression, in a panic of self-accusation and remorse.
“It’s all over,” he said, with a groan. “I’ve made a fool of myself. I can never square myself after that. What under the shining stars made me say that? What happened? I hadn’t a thought, and then all at once﻿—Oh, Lord!”
A couple of upper classmen returning nodded to him, and he flung back an abrupt “Hello,” without distinguishing them.
“Why did I do it?﻿—why﻿—why!”
He went plunging along, through the dark regions that lay between the spotted arc lights that began to sputter along the avenue, his ears deafened by the rush and grind of blazing trolley cars. When he had gone breathlessly a good two miles, he stopped and wearily retraced his steps. The return no longer gave him the sensation of flight. He came back laggingly, with reluctance. Each time he thought of the scene which had passed he had a sensation of heat and cold, of anger and of cowardice. Never again he said to himself, would he be able to enter the Storys’ home, to face her, Jean Story.
But after a time, from sheer exhaustion, he ceased to think about his all-important self. He remembered the dignity and gentleness with which the young girl had met the shock of his blunder, and he was overwhelmed with wonder. He saw again her large eyes, filled with pain, trouble, and yet a certain pity. He recalled her quiet voice, the direct meeting of the issue, and deep through all impressions was the memory of the woman, sweet, self-possessed, and gentle, that had been evoked from her eyes.
He forgot himself. He forgot all the wretchedness and hot misery. He remembered only this Jean Story, and the Jean Story that would be. And feeling the revealing acuteness of love for the first time, he said impulsively:
“Oh, yes, I love her. I have always loved her!” And silently, deep in his heart, a little frightened almost to set the thought to words, he made a vow that his life from now on should be earnest and inspired with but one purpose, to win her respect and to win the right to ask her for his wife.
With the resolve, all the fret and fever went from him. He felt a new confidence and a new maturity.
“When I speak again, I shall have the right,” he said solemnly.

===Chinese===

斯托弗冲出斯托里家的门，沿着昏暗的大街拔腿就走——一场发着热的长征，看不见的树叶打着旋朝他扑来。他丢了脸，出尽了丑，没法说出口；自我谴责和悔恨把他泡在恐慌里。
「全完了，」他呻吟着说。「我出了大丑。这一下再也扳不回来了。满天星斗作证，我干吗要说那句话？出了什么事？我脑子里明明什么都没有，忽然就——哦，老天！」
两个迎面走来的高年级生朝他点头，他没看清是谁，硬邦邦甩回去一声「喂」。
「我干吗要那样？——为什么——为什么！」
他一路踉跄往前冲，穿过大街上一盏盏刚点着、噼啪作响的弧光灯之间的一块块黑地，耳朵里灌满烧得雪亮的电车轰轰的冲撞和摩擦。一口气走出足足两英里，他站住，疲惫地折回来。归路不再有奔逃的感觉。他磨磨蹭蹭、老大不情愿地往回挪。每想起方才那一幕，他浑身一阵热一阵冷，一阵恼一阵怯。他对自己说：再也进不了斯托里家的门了，再见不得她——琼·斯托里。
可是过了一阵，纯粹的力竭叫他停下了对这桩要紧的自我的计较。他想起那位姑娘迎接他闯下的大祸时的端严与温柔，心里涨满了惊异。他又看见她那双大眼睛，盛着痛、盛着乱，却还有一份怜惜。他想起她安静的声音，想起她直面的那一下；而所有印象的最深处，是那个从她眼睛里唤出来的女人——甜、自持、温柔。
他忘了他自己。忘了全部的狼狈和滚烫的难堪。他只记得这个琼·斯托里，和将来会有的那个琼·斯托里。头一回尝到爱情那种照透人的锋利，他冲动地说：
「哦，是，我爱她。我一直爱着她！」于是在心里、在沉默的最深处——几乎不敢把念头落成字——他起了誓：从今往后，他的生活要认真，只被一个目的点燃：赢得她的敬重，赢得开口娶她为妻的权利。
随着这个决断，全部的烦躁和热烧退了。他觉出一股新的自信、一种新的成熟。
「等我再开口，我就有这个权利了，」他庄严地说。

===Original===

“And she shall see that I am not a mere boy. That I will show her soon!”
When he came again into the domain of the college, he suddenly felt all the littleness of the ambitions that raged inside those self-sufficient walls.
“Lord, what have I been doing all this time﻿—what does it count for? Brocky is right; it isn’t what you do here, it’s what you are ready to do when you go out. Thank Heaven, I can see it now.” And secure in the knowledge that the honors he rated so lightly were his, he added: “There’s only one thing that counts﻿—that’s your own self.”
It was after the dinner hour, and he hesitated; a little tired of his own company, longing for the diversion another personality would bring, and seeking someone as far removed from his own point of view as possible, he halted before Durfee, and sent his call to the top stories:
“Oh, Ricky Ricketts, stick out your head.”
Above a window went up, and a fuzzy head came curiously forth.
“Wot’ell, Bill?”
“It’s Stover, Dink Stover. Come down.”
“Somethin’ doin’?”
“You bet.”
Presently, Ricketts’s beanstalk figure came flopping out of the entry.
“What’s up, Dink?”
“I’m back too late for supper. Come on down with me to Mory’s and keep me company, and I’ll buy you a drink.”
“Did I hear the word ‘buy’?” said Ricketts, in the manner then made popular by the lamented Pete Dailey.
“You did.”
“Lead me to it.”
At Mory’s, two or three men whom he didn’t know were at the senior table. Le Baron and Reynolds, prospective captain of the crew and chairman of the News, respectively, men of his own society, gave him a hearty, “Hello, Dink,” and then stared curiously at Ricketts, whose general appearance neither conformed to any one fashion nor to any two. Gimbel, the politician, was in the off room with three of the more militant anti-sophomore society leaders. The two parties saluted in regulation style.
“Hello, you fellows.”
“Howdy, there.”
Stover, sitting down, saw Gimbel’s perplexed glance at his companion, and thought to himself:
“I’ve got Gimbel way up a tree. I’ll bet he thinks I’m trying to work out some society combine against him.”
The thought recalled to him all the increasing bitterness of the anti-sophomore society fight which had swept the college. There was talk even of an open mass meeting. He remembered that Hunter had mentioned it, and for a moment he was inclined to put the question direct to Gimbel.

===Chinese===

「她也会看见：我不是个毛孩子。这个我很快就做给她看！」
重新走进学院的疆界，他忽然觉出那些在自给自足的围墙里头烧得正凶的野心的渺小。
「老天，我这一向都在干什么——这算得了什么？布罗基说得对；要紧的不是你在这儿干了什么，而是你出去时准备好干什么。谢天谢地，我现在看见了。」而且他看得很轻的那些荣誉反正跑不了，于是他又添一句：「算数的只有一样——就是你自己。」
晚饭时间已过。他犹豫着——有点厌烦同自己做伴，渴望另一个性子带来的散心，专挑一个同自己的眼光离得越远越好的人——他在德菲楼前站住，朝顶楼喊：
「喂，里基·里基茨，伸出头来。」
一扇窗推上去，一颗毛茸茸的脑袋好奇地探出来。
「啥事，比尔？」
「我是斯托弗，丁克·斯托弗。下来。」
「有好戏？」
「可不。」
不一会儿，里基茨那副豆芽架似的身条从门洞里拍打出来。
「什么事，丁克？」
「我回来晚了，晚饭赶不上。陪我上莫里饭店坐坐，我请你喝一杯。」
「我方才听见『请』字没有？」里基茨说，学着那位令人怀念的皮特·戴利走红的腔调。
「听见了。」
「带我去。」
莫里饭店里，几张他不认得的面孔坐在毕业班的桌边。勒巴龙和雷诺兹——划船队的候补队长、《新闻》的主席——都是他会社里的人，热络地喊了声「喂，丁克」，随后就好奇地瞪里基茨：此人的整个外表，既不合哪一种时髦，也不合哪两种。政治家金贝尔带着三位更激烈的反二年级会社头领在里间。两派按规矩见礼。
「喂，你们各位。」
「好啊，那儿。」
斯托弗坐下，看见金贝尔朝他同伴投来的困惑的一瞥，心想：
「我把金贝尔吊到树上下不来了。保准当他要炮制什么会社联盟对付他。」
这个念头把反二年级会社那场席卷全校、越斗越狠的仗端回他心里。甚至有风声要开公开的大会。他记得亨特提起过，有一瞬间他有心把问题当面摆给金贝尔。

===Original===

But his mood was alien to controversy, and Louis, with sidelong, beady eyes, and a fragrant aroma, was waiting the order.
Ricketts had, among twenty Yankee devices for greasing his journey through college, a specialty of breaking in new pipes, one of which he now produced, with an apologetic:
“You don’t mind, do you, if I crack my lungs on this appetizing little trifle?”
“I say, Ricketts,” said Stover, trying to keep off his mind the one subject, “is that all a joke about your breaking in pipes?”
“Straightest thing in the world.”
“What do you charge?”
“Thirty-five cents and the tobacco.”
“You ought to charge fifty.”
“I’m going to next year. You think I’m loony?” said Ricketts.
“I’m not sure.”
“Dink, my boy, I’ll be a millionaire in ten years. You know what I’m figuring out all this time? I’m going at this scientifically. I’m figuring out the number of fools there are on the top of this globe, classifying ’em, looking out what they want to be fooled on. I’m making an exact science of it.”
“Go on,” said Dink, amused and perplexed, for he was trying to distinguish the serious and the humorous.
“What’s the principle of a patent medicine?﻿—advertise first, then concoct your medicine. All the science of Foolology is: first, find something all the fools love and enjoy, tell them it’s wrong, hammer it into them, give them a substitute and sit back, chuckle, and shovel away the ducats. Bread’s wrong, coffee’s wrong, beer’s wrong. Why, Dink, in the next twenty years all the fools will be feeding on substitutes for everything they want; no salt﻿—denatured sugar﻿—anti-tea﻿—oiloline﻿—peanut butter﻿—whale’s milk﻿—et cetera, et ceteray, and blessing the name of the fool-master who fooled them.”
“By Jingo,” said Stover, listening to this jumble of words, entranced, “I believe you’re right. And so you’ve reduced it to a science, eh﻿—Foolology?”
Ricketts, half in earnest, never entirely in jest, abetted by newly arriving tobies, was off again on his pet theories of business imagination, disdaining the occasional gibes that were flung at him from Gimbel’s table.
When Le Baron and Reynolds passed out, with curious glances, Stover was weak with laughter. Later arrivals dropping in joined them, egging on the inventor.
Stover, who had been busily consulting his watch, left at half-past eight on a sudden resolve.

===Chinese===

可他的心境同论战无缘；何况侍者路易斯正等着点单，斜着一双珠子眼，带着一身香喷喷的气味。
里基茨在他的二十种给大学之旅上油的北方佬巧计之外，还有一手开新烟斗的绝活；这会儿他掏出一只，带一句告罪：
「不介意吧，叫我拿这件开胃的小玩意儿劈一劈我的肺？」
「我说，里基茨，」斯托弗说，竭力把那件事摁在脑子外头，「开烟斗这一手，全是玩笑吧？」
「天下最正经的事。」
「收多少钱？」
「三毛五，烟丝另算。」
「你该收五毛。」
「明年就收五毛。你当我疯了？」里基茨说。
「说不准。」
「丁克，我的孩子，十年后我就是百万富翁。你知道我这一向在算什么？我搞这个是按科学来的。我在算这颗星球顶上有多少傻瓜，给他们分类，查清他们爱在哪上头被骗。我把它造成一门精密科学。」
「接着说，」丁克说，又好笑又发懵——他正努力分辨哪是正经哪是诙谐。
「专利药的原理是什么？——先打广告，再配药。『傻瓜学』的全部学问就是：先找到一样所有傻瓜都爱都享受的东西，告诉他们这是错的，敲进他们脑子里，再递上一件代用品，然后往椅背上一靠，笑眯眯地铲钞票。面包是错的，咖啡是错的，啤酒是错的。嗐，丁克，往后二十年，所有的傻瓜将拿代用品喂他们想要的一切：无盐——变性糖——反茶——油精——花生酱——鲸奶——等等等等，还要祝福那位骗了他们的傻瓜大师的圣名。」
「乖乖，」斯托弗听着这一锅词，听得入了迷，「我看你说得对。这么说你真把它归成一门科学了，嗯——傻瓜学？」
里基茨半真半假——从没有全假——又有新到的啤酒助阵，又驾上了他那一套生意想象力的得意理论，金贝尔那桌不时甩过来的挖苦，他一概不理。
勒巴龙和雷诺兹带着好奇的目光出门去时，斯托弗已笑瘫了。随后进来的人又加入进来，一齐撺掇这位发明家。
斯托弗频频看表，八点半，凭一个突来的决断起身走了。

===Original===

The farcical interruption that had temporarily drawn him out of himself, had cleared his head, and brought him a sudden authoritative decision.
He went directly to the Storys’, and, entering the parlor, found a group of his crowd there, dinner finished, trying out the latest comic opera chorus.
He came in quite coldly self-possessed, shook hands, and immediately jumped into the conversation, which was all on the crisis in the sophomore societies. Jean Story was at the piano, a little more serious than usual. At his entrance, she looked up with sudden wonder and confusion. He came to her, and in taking her hand inclined his head in great respect, but did not speak to her. He had but one desire, to show her that he was not a boy but a man, and that he could rise to the crisis which he had brought on himself.
Hunter and Tommy Bain had been arguing for no compromise, Bob Story and Hungerford were of the opinion that the time had come to enlarge the membership of the societies, and to destroy their exclusiveness.
On the sofa, the little Judge, a spectator, never intimating his opinion, studying each man as he spoke, appealed to Stover:
“Well, now, Judge Dink, what is your learned opinion on this situation? Here is the dickens to pay; three-fourths the college lined up against you fellows, and a public mass meeting coming. Jim Hunter here believes in sitting back and letting the storm blow over; Bob, who of course can regulate it all, wants to double the membership and meet some objections. Now what do you say? Mr. Stover has the floor. My daughter will please come to order.”
Jean Story abruptly turned from the piano, where her fingers had been absentmindedly running over the keys.
“Frankly, I haven’t made up my mind just yet,” said Stover. “There are a great many sides to it. I’ve listened to a good many opinions, but haven’t yet chosen mine. Everyone is talking about the effect on the college, but what has impressed me most is the effect on the sophomore society men themselves. If the outsiders only knew the danger and handicap they are to us!”
“Hello,” said the Judge, shifting with a little interest.
“What do you mean?” said Hunter aggressively.
“I mean we are the ones who are limited, who are liable to miss the big opportunities of college life. We have got into the habit, under the pretense of good fellowship, of herding together.”
“Why shouldn’t we?”

===Chinese===

这场把他暂时拖出自身的滑稽插曲，清了他的脑子，给他送来一个斩钉截铁的决定。
他径直到斯托里家，进客厅一看，他那一伙人正在——晚饭已毕，试唱最新喜歌剧的合唱。琼·斯托里在钢琴边，比平常略沉。他一进来，她抬头，一阵突兀的惊讶和慌乱。他走到她跟前，握手时深深俯了一下头，恭敬之至，却没有同她说话。他只有一个心愿：让她看见他不是个孩子，是个男人，能挺住他自己惹出来的这一场危局。
亨特和汤米·贝恩一直主张决不让步；鲍勃·斯托里和亨格福德认为，扩大会社成员、废掉它的排他性的时机到了。
沙发上，小个子法官作壁上观，从不透露己见，只把每个发言的人打量一遍，转向斯托弗说：
「那么，丁克法官，这个局面您的高见如何？眼下乱子闹大了：四分之三的学院同你们作对，公开大会眼看要开。这位吉姆·亨特主张坐着不动，等风头过去；鲍勃呢——他当然能一手摆平一切——主张成员翻倍，应付几条意见。你怎么说？斯托弗先生发言。小女请维持会场秩序。」
琼·斯托里猛地从琴边转过身——她的指头方才一直在键上心不在焉地滑。
「坦白说，我还没拿定主意，」斯托弗说。「这件事面太多。我听了许多意见，还没有挑中我自己的。人人都在谈对学院的影响；可最敲我的，是对二年级会社人自己的影响。要是外围的人知道他们对我们是多大的危险和拖累就好了！」
「嗬，」法官说，换了个姿势，来了点兴味。
「什么意思？」亨特咄咄逼人地说。
「我是说：受限制的是我们，可能错过大学生活大机会的也是我们。我们养成了习惯——打着好交情的旗号，扎堆抱团。」
「凭什么不？」

===Original===

persisted Hunter.
“Because we shut ourselves up, withdraw from the big life of the college, know only our own kind, the kind we’ll know all our life; surrender our imagination. We represent only a social idea, a good time, good friends, good figureheads on the different machines of the college. But we miss the big chance﻿—to go out, to mingle with everyone, to educate ourselves by knowing opposite lives, fellows who see things as we never have seen them, who are going back to a life a thousand miles away from what we will lead.” He expressed himself badly, and, realizing it, said impatiently: “Here, what I mean is this. It’s not my idea, it’s Brockhurst’s, it’s Tom Regan’s. The biggest thing we can do is to reflect the nation, to be the inspiration of the democracy of the country, to be alive to the fight among the people for real political independence. We ought to get a great vision when we come up here, as young men, of the bigness of our country, of the privilege of fighting out its political freedom, of what American manhood means in the towns of Georgia and Texas, in the little manufacturing cities of New England, in the great West, and in the small homes of the big cities. We ought to really know one another, meet, discuss, respect each other’s point of view, independence﻿—odd ways if you wish. We don’t do it. We did once﻿—we don’t now. Princeton doesn’t do it, Harvard doesn’t do it. We’re over-organized away from the vital thing﻿—the knowledge of ourselves.”
“Then you’d abolish the sophomore societies?” said Hunter, crowding him to the wall.
“I don’t know. Sometimes I’ve felt it’s the system that is wrong,” said Stover frankly. “Lately, I’ve changed my mind. I think we can do what we want﻿—at least I know I’ve gone out and met whom I wanted to without my being in a sophomore society making the slightest difference. I say I don’t know where the trouble is; whether the whole social system here and elsewhere is the cause or the effect. It may be that it is the whole development of America that has changed our college life. I don’t know; those questions are too big for me to work out. But I know one thing, that my own ideas of what I want here have taken a back somersault, and that I’m going out of here knowing everything I can of every man in the class.”

===Chinese===

亨特追问。
「因为我们把自己关起来，从学院的大生活里退出去，只认得自己这一路的人——这一路我们一辈子都会认得；把想象力缴了械。我们只代表一个社交观念：好时光、好朋友、学院各部机器上好看的招牌。可我们错过了大机会——出去，同人人相混，靠认识相反的人生来教育自己：那些看事物同我们从来没看过的一样的人们，那些要回到一种同我们将要过的相距一千里的生活里去的人们。」他表达得很糟，自己也知道，不耐烦地说：「这么说吧，我的意思是这个。这不是我的主意，是布罗克赫斯特的，是汤姆·里甘的。我们能做的最大的事，是照出这个国家，做国家民主的火种，对人民为真正的政治独立而进行的那场斗争保持醒着。我们上来的时候，作为年轻人，就该得一个大视野：我们这个国家之大，为它的政治自由而战这一特权，美利坚的男子气概在佐治亚和德克萨斯的城镇里、在新英格兰的小制造城里、在大西部、在大城市的小小人家里，意味着什么。我们该真正相知，相会，讨论，尊重彼此的眼光——独立——古怪的路数也随它。我们没有这样做。我们曾经有过——现在没有了。普林斯顿没有，哈佛也没有。我们组织过了头，离了那件命脉的东西——对自己的认识。」
「这么说，你要废掉二年级会社？」亨特说，把他逼到墙根。
「不知道。有时候我觉得错的是这套制度，」斯托弗坦白地说。「近来我改了主意。我想我们想做还是做得到——至少我知道，我出去见我想见的人，在不在二年级会社，没起过一丝一毫的差别。我说不好毛病出在哪儿；这里的、别处的整套社交制度，是因还是果。也许是我们这整个美国的发展，改变了我们的大学生活。我不知道；那些问题太大，我算不动。可有一件事我知道：我自己对在这儿要什么的想法，翻了一个大跟头；走出这儿的时候，我要把班里每一个人身上能学到的东西学到手。」

===Original===

Suddenly he remembered Hunter’s opposition, and turning, concluded: “One thing more; if ever I make up my mind that the sophomore society system or any other system ought to be abolished, I’ll stand out and say so.”
When he had finished, his classmates began talking all at once, Hunter and Bain in bitter opposition, Bob Story in warm defense, Hungerford, in his big-souled way, coming ponderously to his assistance.
Stover withdrew from the conversation. He glanced at Jean Story, wondering if she had understood the reason of his return, and that he had spoken for her ears alone. She was still at the piano, one hand resting on the keyboard, looking at him with the same serious, half-troubled expression in her large eyes. He made an excuse to leave, and for the second that he stood by her, he looked into her eyes boldly, with even a little bravado, as though to ask:
“Do you understand?”
But the young girl, without speaking, nodded her head slightly, continuing to look at him with her wistful, a little wounded glance.

===Chinese===

他忽然记起亨特的反对，转过身，收了尾：「还有一句：哪天我拿定了主意，认为二年级会社制度、或者随便什么别的制度该废——我一定站出来，把它说出口。」
他说完，同学们一齐开了腔：亨特和贝恩苦苦反对，鲍勃·斯托里热烈辩护，亨格福德以他那副大心肠，沉甸甸地赶去支援。
斯托弗从谈话里退出来。他望了琼·斯托里一眼，不知道她懂没懂他回来的原委，懂没懂他这一篇话只说给她的耳朵。她还在琴边，一只手搭在键盘上，望着他，那双大眼睛里还是那副郑重的、微含着痛的神情。他寻了个由头告辞，站在她身边的那一秒，他大胆地——甚至带着一点逶强——望进她的眼睛，像在问：
「你懂吗？」
可姑娘没有说话，只微微点了点头，继续用她那副恋恋的、略带受伤的目光望着他。

## XX / 二十
===Original===

For an hour, bareheaded, he went plunging into the darkness, a prey to a nervous crisis, that left him shaking in every muscle. He knew the extent of his passions, and the anger which had swept over him left him weak and frightened.
“It’s lucky that runt of a Reynolds held his tongue,” he said hotly. “By the Lord, I don’t know what I would have done to him. Here, I must get hold of myself. This is terrible. Well, thank Heaven, it’s over.”
He controlled himself slowly, and came back, limp and weak; yet beyond the physical reaction was a liberated soaring of the spirit.
“I’m glad I did it! I never was gladder!” he said solemnly. “Goodbye to the whole society game, Skull and Bones, and all the rest. But I take my stand from now on, and I stand on my own feet. I’m glad of it.” Then he thought of Jean Story, and he was troubled. “I wonder if she’ll understand? I can’t help it. I couldn’t do anything else. Now, I suppose the whole bunch will turn on me. So be it.”
It was long after midnight when he came back gloomily to the light still staring from his window, and toiled up the heavy steps. When he entered the room, Le Baron, Bob Story, and Joe Hungerford were sitting silently, waiting for him, and in Story’s hand was the pin bruised by his furious heel.
He saw at once the full strength of the appeal that was to be made to him, and he closed the door wearily.
“I don’t want to talk about it,” he said slowly. “The whole thing is done and buried.”
Bob Story, agitated and solemn, came to him.
“Dink, this is awful﻿—the whole thing is awful,” he said earnestly. “You’ve got to talk it out with us.”
“Do you understand, Bob,” Stover said suddenly, “just what happened in this room?”
“Yes, I think I do.”
“I don’t believe it.”
“Dink, I want you to listen to me a moment,” said Le Baron. “It’s been rotten business, the whole wretched thing. I can understand how you felt. Reynolds and you got on each other’s nerves. You each said what you didn’t mean. It was damned unfortunate. He put things to you like a fool, and I was telling him so when you broke into the room. He was all up on edge from something that had gone before.”
“Oh, I lost my temper,” said Stover. “I know it.”
“I’d have done the same,” said Hungerford openly.
“Now, Dink, there isn’t one of us here that doesn’t like you, and look up to you,” said Story, with his irresistible charm.

===Chinese===

一个钟头，他光着头扎进黑暗里，被一场神经的风暴攋住，浑身上下每一条肌肉都在抖。他知道自己的激情有多猛，刚才漫过他的那阵怒，把他冲得又虚又怕。

「亏得雷诺兹那个小矮子没还嘴，」他恨恨地说。「老天在上，我真不知道我会对他干出什么。喂，我得攋住自己。这太可怕了。好了，谢天谢地，都过去了。」

他一点一点把自己摁住，回来时又软又虚；可在肉体的反应之外，有一股挣脱出来的、直往上飞的精神。

「我高兴我干了！从没这么高兴过！」他郑重地说。「会社这场把戏，髅髅会，还有其余一切，再会了。从今往后我自己站队，站在自己脚上。我乐意。」随后他想起琼·斯托里，心里乱了。「她会懂吗？没办法。我别的什么都做不出。这下，我估摸那一整帮人都要反过我来了。那就来吧。」

后半夜早已过了，他才阴沉沉地回到那扇还瞪着光的窗前，一步步蹭上沉重的楼梯。他进屋时，勒巴龙、鲍勃·斯托里和乔·亨格福德正默默坐着等他，斯托里手里，捏着那枚被他暴怒的鞋跟碾伤的徽针。

他一眼看清了即将向他发动的那场恳求的全部分量，疲惫地关上门。

「我不想谈这个。」他慢慢地说。「整件事已经办完、埋了。」

鲍勃·斯托里激动而郑重地走上前。

「丁克，这太糟了——整件事都太糟了。」他恳切地说。「你必须同我们把它谈开。」

「你明白吗，鲍勃，」斯托弗忽然说，「这间屋里到底出了什么？」

「我想我明白。」

「我不信。」

「丁克，我要你听我说一会儿。」勒巴龙说。「这事办得烂透了，这桩倒霉事全盘。你怎么会那样感受，我懂。雷诺兹和你戳了彼此的神经。你们俩都说了不想说的话。倒霉透顶。他像个傻子那样跟你说话，你闯进来的时候我正这么数落他。他也是被先前的事弄得满身是刺。」

「哦，是我发了火。」斯托弗说。「我知道。」

「换我也一样。」亨格福德坦率地说。

「这么说吧，丁克，我们这几个，没有一个不喜欢你、不敬你。」斯托里说，带着他那股挡不住的魅力。

===Original===

“We know you’re every inch a man, and what you do you believe in. But, Dink, we’re all friends together, and this is a terrible thing to us. We want you to take back your pin, and shut up this whole business. Will you?”
“I’d do a great deal for you, Bob Story,” said Stover, looking him in the eyes, “more than for anyone else, but I can’t do this.”
He said it calmly, with a little sadness. The three were impressed with the finality of the judgment. Story, standing with the cast-off pin in his hand, turning and twisting it, said slowly:
“Dink, do you really mean it?”
“I do.”
“It’s a serious thing you’re doing, Stover,” said Le Baron, with the first touch of formality, “and I don’t think it should be done in anger.”
“I’m not.”
“Remember that you are judging a whole society﻿—your own friends﻿—by what one man happened to say to you in a moment of irritation.”
“I don’t want to talk of what’s done,” said Stover slowly, for his head was throbbing. “I know myself, and I know nothing is going to make me go back on what I’ve said. I’m only going to say a word, and then I’m going into my room and going to bed. Le Baron”﻿—with a sudden rise of his voice he turned and faced the junior﻿—“don’t think I don’t understand what it means that I’m giving up. I get what you mean when you start in calling me Stover. I know as well as I’m standing here that you and Reynolds will keep me out of Bones, whether I make captain or not. And that’ll hurt me a good bit﻿—I admit it. Now don’t let’s quibble. It isn’t the way Reynolds said what he did﻿—though that did rile me﻿—it’s what was told me, indirectly or directly﻿—it’s the same thing; you men in sophomore societies would limit my freedom of choice. There you are. I’m against you now, because for the first time I see how the thing works out, because you’re wrong! You’re a bad influence for those who are in, and a rotten influence for the whole college. Now I’ve made up my mind to just one thing. I’m going to finish up here at the head of my own business﻿—my own master; and I’m not going to be in a position to be told by anyone in your class or my class what I’m to do.”
“One moment.” Le Baron rose as Stover moved towards the bedroom. “There’s another side to it.”
“What other side?”

===Chinese===

「我们知道你是个十足的男子汉，做的事都是信得过的。可是丁克，我们都是朋友，这件事对我们太可怕了。我们要你收回徽针，把整件事封死。行不行？」

「为你我什么都肯干，鲍勃·斯托里，」斯托弗望着他的眼睛说，「比为谁都肯干，可这件事不行。」

他说得平静，带一点哀伤。三个人都被这裁决里的终审之气镇住了。斯托里手里捏着那枚被弃的徽针，翻来转去，慢慢说：

「丁克，你是当真的？」

「当真。」

「你这是在干一件大事，斯托弗。」勒巴龙说，头一次带上了正式的腔调，「而且我认为不该在火头上做。」

「我不在火头上。」

「记住，你是在凭一个人一时上火对你说的话，审判一整个会社——你自己的朋友们。」

「我不想谈已成的事。」斯托弗慢慢地说，他的头正突突地跳。「我了解我自己，我知道什么也不能让我收回说过的话。我只再说一句，然后就进屋睡觉。勒巴龙」——嗓音陡然一扬，他转身正对那位三年级生——「别以为我不懂我正放弃的是什么。你们开口改口叫我『斯托弗』，这意思我接得住。我像站在这儿一样清楚地知道，你和雷诺兹会把我挡在髅髅会外头，不管我当不当得上队长。这一下会伤我不轻——我认。好了，咱们别抠字眼。问题不在雷诺兹说话的方式——虽然那也着实撩了我——在于人家递给我的话，拐弯抹角也罢，直截了当也罢——是一回事：你们这些二年级会社里的人，要限制我择友的自由。就这么回事。我现在同你们对着干，因为我头一回看清了这套东西怎么运转，因为你们错了！对会里的人，你们是一股坏影响；对整个学校，是一股烂影响。如今我只拿定了一件事：我要在这里念完，当自己那摊事的主——自己的主人；我不打算落在一个位子上，任凭你们班还是我们班里的什么人，来吩咐我该做什么。」

「慢一步。」斯托弗朝卧室走去时，勒巴龙起身。「这事还有另一面。」

「什么另一面？」

===Original===

“Whatever you decide, and I won’t take your answer until the morning,” said Le Baron solemnly, “I want you to give me your word that what’s happened tonight remains a secret.”
“I won’t give my word to that or anything else,” said Dink defiantly. “I shall do exactly what I think is right to be done, and for that reason only. Now you’ll have to excuse me. Good night.”
He went to his bedroom, shut the door, and without undressing tumbled on the bed, and, still hearing in a confused jumble the murmur of voices, dropped off to sleep.
He was startled out of heavy dreams by a beating in his ears, and sprang up to find Bob Story thundering on his door. He looked at his watch. It was still an hour before chapel.
When he entered his dim study, Story was waiting, and Hungerford uncoiling from the couch where he had passed the night.
“Have you fellows been here all night?” said Stover, stopping short.
“Dink, we want a last chance to talk this over,” said Story solemnly. “We’ve all had a chance to sleep it out. Le Baron isn’t here, just Joe and myself﻿—your friends.”
“You make it hard for me, boys,” said Dink, shaking his head.
Hungerford rose with the stiffness of the night, and coming to Stover, took him by the shoulders.
“Damn you, Dink,” he said, “get this straight, we’re not thinking about the society, we’re thinking about you﻿—about your future. And I want you to know this: whatever you decide, I’m your friend and proud to be it.”
“What Joe says is what I feel,” said Story, as Stover, much affected, stood looking at the ground. “We’re sticking by you, Dink﻿—that’s why I’m going to try once more. Can’t you go on in the society, make no open break, and still fight for what you believe in﻿—what Joe and I believe in, too?”
“But, Bob, I think they’re wrong through and through﻿—you don’t understand﻿—I’m for wiping them out now.”
“That whole question’s coming up, and coming up soon,” continued Story earnestly, “and a lot of our own crowd will line up for you. Work inside the crowd, if you can see it that way, Dink. There are only five of us know what’s happened, and no one else need know.”
“Wait a moment, Bob, old fellow,” said Dink, stopping him. “You two have got down under my skin, and I won’t forget it. Now I’m going to ask you fellows a couple of questions. First: you think if I stick to my determination that most of the crowd’ll turn on me?”
“Yes.”

===Chinese===

「不论你怎么决定——你的答复我等到明早再收，」勒巴龙郑重地说，「我要你给我一句承诺：今晚发生的事，永远保密。」

「这个我不承诺，别的什么都不承诺。」丁克堕头堕脑地说。「我只做我认为该做的事，只为这个缘故。现在恕不奉陪。晚安。」

他进了卧室，关上门，衣服没脱就栽倒在床上，耳朵里还嗡嗡地响着那阵含混的低语，睡着了。

一阵擂门声把他从沉梦里惊起，他跳起来，发现鲍勃·斯托里正砸他的门。他看了看表。离礼拜堂还有一个钟头。

他走进昏暗的书房，斯托里等着，亨格福德正从沙发里舒展开——他在那儿过了夜。

「你们俩在这儿待了一整夜？」斯托弗站住说。

「丁克，我们要最后一回把它谈开。」斯托里郑重地说。「大家都睡了下去，让它沉了沉。勒巴龙不在，只有乔和我——你的朋友。」

「你们这是让我为难啊，弟兄们。」丁克摇着头说。

亨格福德带着一夜的僵直站起来，走到斯托弗跟前，抓住他的双肩。

「你这个可恶的丁克，」他说，「把这听明白：我们想的不是会社，是你——是你的前程。我还要你知道这一条：不管你怎么决定，我是你的朋友，还以此为荣。」

「乔说的就是我心里的话。」斯托里说；斯托弗大受触动，站着望地上。「我们都站在你这边，丁克——所以我才要再试一回。你不能留在会社里，不公开决裂，照样为你信的东西斗争——为乔和我也信的东西？」

「可是鲍勃，我认为他们从里到外全错了——你不明白——我如今就要把它们铲了。」

「那整个问题就要摆上台面了，而且快了。」斯托里恳切地接着说，「咱们自己圈里好多人会站到你这边。在圈子里头干，你要能这么看的话，丁克。眼下知道这件事的只有我们五个，别人不必知道。」

「等一下，鲍勃老伙计。」丁克拦住他。「你们两个钻到我皮底下去了，这我忘不了。现在我要问你们俩两个问题。第一：你们认为，我要是咬定这个决心，那帮人多半会反过我？」

「会。」

“That I have as much chance of being tapped for Bones as Jackson, the sweep?”
“Yes, Dink.”

Neither answered.
“What would you think of me, Joe﻿—Bob?”
“Damn the luck,” said Hungerford. He did not attempt to answer the question. Neither did Bob Story. They shook hands with Stover, and went out defeated.
Just how big a change in his college career his renunciation would make, Stover had not understood until in the weeks that succeeded he came to feel the full effects of the resentment he had aroused in the society crowds, now at bay before a determined opposition.
The second morning, as he went down High Street to his eating-joint, Hungerford was loafing ahead of him, ostensibly conning a lesson. Stover joined him, unaware of the friendly intent of the action. They went inside, laughing together, to where a score of men were rubbing their eyes over hasty breakfasts. Four-fifths of them belonged to sophomore societies.
“Morning, everybody,” said the new arrivals, in unison, and the answer came back:
“Hello, Joe.”
“Hello, Dink.”
“Shove in here.”
At their arrival a little constrained silence was felt, for the news had somehow passed into rumor. Opposite Stover, Jim Hunter was sitting. He nodded to Hungerford, and then with deliberation continued a conversation with Tommy Bain, who sat next to him.
Stover perceived the cut instantly, as others had perceived it. He sat a moment quietly, his glance concentrated on Hunter.
“Oatmeal or hominy?” said the waiter at his back.
“One moment.” He raised his hand, and the gesture concentrated the attention of the table on him. “Why, how do *you* do, Jim Hunter?” he said, with every word cut sharp.
There was a breathless moment, and a nervous stirring under foot, as Hunter turned and looked at Stover. Their glances matched one another a long moment, and then Hunter, with an excess of politeness, said:
“Oh, hello﻿—Stover.”
Instantly there was a relieved hum of voices, and a clatter of cutlery.
“I’ll take oatmeal now,” said Stover calmly. Story, glancing over, saw two spots of scarlet standing out on his cheeks, and realized how near the moment had come to a violent scene.
“Dink, old gazabo,” said Hungerford, as they walked over to chapel, “what are you going to do?

===Chinese===

「第二：我入选髅髅会的机会，同扫楼的那个杰克逊（Jackson）一样多？」

「一样，丁克。」

「那么，弟兄们，凭良心，我要是为了这样的缘故收回徽针，我算不算一个没骨头、会算计的小慫包？」

两人都没答。

「你们会怎么看我，乔——鲍勃？」

「该死的运气。」亨格福德说。这个问题他不打算答。鲍勃·斯托里也没答。他们同斯托弗握了手，败下阵，走了。

这一弃绝会给他的大学前程带来多大的翻转，斯托弗原本并不明白；直到后来那几个星期，他才渐渐尝足了他在会社各圈子里激起的怨毒的全部滋味——那些圈子，如今正被一场决绝的反对逼到墙角。

第二天早晨，他沿高街去膳堂，亨格福德在前头晃晃悠悠，做样子温书。斯托弗赶上去同他并排——没觉出这一步里护送的心思。两人一路笑着进了门，二十来个人正就着匆忙的早饭揉眼睛。五分之四，是二年级会社的人。

「各位早。」两位新到的齐声说，回答涌过来：

「你好，乔。」

「你好，丁克。」

「坐这边。」

两人一到，席间掠过一阵小小的拘谨的沉默——消息不知怎么已走成了风声。斯托弗对面，坐着吉姆·亨特。他朝亨格福德点了点头，随后不紧不慢，接着同邻座的汤米·贝恩聊下去。

这一记冷刀斯托弗立刻接住了，正如旁人也都接住了。他静静坐了片刻，目光聚在亨特身上。

「燕麦粥还是玉米糊？」身后的侍者问。

「慢着。」他抬起手，这一抬，把全桌的注意都聚了过来。「哦，你*好*哇，吉姆·亨特？」他说，一字一斧。

一阵屏息，桌下神经质的挪动——亨特转过脸来，看着斯托弗。两道目光对峙了好一阵，随后亨特以一种过分的客气说：

「哦，你好——斯托弗。」

立时一片松了口气的嗡嗡，刀叉叮当起来。

「现在可以上燕麦粥了。」斯托弗平静地说。斯托里望过来，看见他颧骨上顶出两块猩红，明白了刚才那一刻离一场掀桌只差几分。

「丁克，老伙计，」走去礼拜堂的路上亨格福德说，「你打算怎么办？

===Original===

You can’t go about the whole time with a chip on your shoulder.”
“Oh, yes, I can,” said Dink between his teeth. “I’ll stick right where I am. And I’d like to see Jim Hunter or anyone else try that again on me!”
Hungerford shook his head.
“You know, Dink, you must see both sides. Now from Hunter’s side, you’ve smashed all traditions, and given us a blow that may be a knockout, considering the state of feeling in the college. Hunter’s a society man, believes in them heart and soul.”
“Then let him come to me and say what he thinks.”
“Are you quite sure, Dink,” said Joe, with a glance, “that there isn’t some other reason for the way you two feel about each other?”
“You mean jealousy?” said Dink, flushing a little. “Bob’s sister? Yes, there’s that. But from the first we’ve been on opposite sides.” He hesitated a moment, and then asked: “I say, Joe, what does Bob think about what I’ve done? Tell me straight.”
“Of course he respects you,” said Hungerford carefully, “more now than I think he did last year, but﻿—Bob’s a society man﻿—all these Andover fellows are brought up in the idea, you know﻿—and I think it’s kind of a jolt.”
“I suppose it is,” said Stover, with a little depression.
He would like to have asked Hungerford to state his case to Jean Story, but he lacked the courage of his boyish impulse. The thought of Jean Story, as he sat in chapel, came to him like a temptation. The Judge was of the Skull and Bones alumni, Bob was sure to go; all the influences about her were of belief in the finality of that judgment.
“Yes, and Hunter will go in with sailing colors; he’ll never risk anything,” he said bitterly, “and I’ll stand up and take my medicine, for doing what? For showing I had a backbone. But no one will ever know it outside. They’ll think it’s something wrong in my character﻿—they always do. Stover, Yale’s star end, misses out for Bones! That’s the slogan. Cheating at cards or bumming. I wonder what she’ll think? Lord, that’s the hard part!”
For a week, proud as Lucifer, on edge for an opportunity, he stuck it out at the eating-joint, knowing the hopelessness of it all﻿—that what he wanted had gone, and no amount of bravado could make him wink the fact, that in the midst of his own crowd, where he had stood as a leader, he was now regarded as an outsider.

===Chinese===

「你总不能成天肩膀上扛着火引子。」

「哦，我能。」丁克咬着牙说。「我就钉在这儿不动。我倒要看看吉姆·亨特，或者随便哪个，还敢再冲我来一回！」

亨格福德摇头。

「你知道，丁克，两面你都得看。站在亨特的立场上：你砸碎了一切传统，照眼下全校的情绪，给了我们一记兴许是击倒的闷棍。亨特是会社人，全心全意信着它们。」

「那就让他来找我，把他的想法当面说。」

「你敢肯定吗，丁克，」乔瞄了他一眼，「你们俩之间这股劲，就没有别的原因？」

「你是指吃醋？」丁克说，脸微微一红。「鲍勃的妹妹？是有这么一条。可打头起我们就站在两边。」他顿了顿，问：「我说，乔，鲍勃怎么看我这事？直说。」

「他当然敬你，」亨格福德斟酌着说，「比去年敬得多了，可是——鲍勃是会社人——这帮安多弗的家伙全是泡在这个观念里长大的，你知道——我想这一下对他有点震。」

「大概吧。」斯托弗说，有点消沉。

他本想让亨格福德替他去向琼·斯托里陈情，却没了少年人那股冲动的胆气。坐在礼拜堂里，琼·斯托里的念头像一种诱惑来到他身上。老法官是髅髅会的校友，鲍勃笃定要进去；她周遭的一切影响，都信着那纸判决的终审效力。

「是啊，亨特会打着全副旌旗进去；他从不冒一分险。」他愤愤地说，「而我要站起来吞我的药——为的什么？为的是亮出我一根脊梁。可外面永远没人知道。他们会当我品行上出了毛病——他们向来如此。斯托弗，耶鲁的明星边锋，髅髅会落选！口号就这么喊。打牌作弊，或者混日子。她会怎么想？老天，这才是最难过的一关！」

整整一星期，他傲得同路西法一般，浑身竖着等一个机会，硬撑在膳堂里——心里明白这一切全无指望：他要的东西没了，再多的虚张也眨不掉这个事实——在他自己那拨人中间，他曾以领袖站立的地方，如今他被当作一个圈外人。

===Original===

In the second week he gave up the useless fight, and went to Commons, to the table where Regan, Gimbel, and Brockhurst ate. They forebore to ask him the reasons of the change, and he gave no explanation. That something had happened which had caused him to break away from his society was soon a matter of common rumor, and several incorrect versions circulated, all vastly to his credit. His influence in the body of the class was correspondingly increased, and Gimbel once or twice approached him with offers to run him for manager of the crew or the Junior Prom.
One day, about a month after his withdrawal, when, bundled up in his dressing-gown, he went shuffling into the basement for a cold tub, he had quite a shock, that brought home visually to him the realization of the price he had paid.
It had been the practise from long custom to inscribe on the walls tentative lists of the probable selections from the class for the three senior societies. On this particular list his name had stood at the head from the beginning, and the constant familiar sight of it had always brought him a warm, secure pleasure.
All at once, as he looked at it, he perceived a leaden blur where his name had stood, and the names of Bain and Hunter heading the list.
“I suppose they’ve got me down among the last now,” he said, with a long breath. He searched the list, his name was not even on it. This popular estimation of what he himself believed had nevertheless power to wound him deeply.
“Well, it’s so﻿—I knew it,” he said; but it was said in bitterness, with a newer and keener realization.
He began indeed to feel like an outsider, and, rebelling against the injustice of it all, to set his heart in bitterness. Hungerford and Bob Story, Dopey McNab often, tried to keep up with him, but, understanding their motives, he was proudly sensitive, and sought rather to avoid them.
Meanwhile the opposition to the sophomore societies reached the point of open revolt, and a mass meeting was held, which, as had been planned, caused a stir throughout the press of the country, and brought in from the alumni a storm of protest.
Stover, himself, despite his inclination to come forward in direct opposition, after a long debate, remained silent, feeling bound by the oath he had given at his initiation.

===Chinese===

第二个星期，他放弃了这场无用的仗，去了公共食堂，坐到里根、金贝尔和布罗克赫斯特那一桌。他们忍着没问他换桌的缘由，他也不给解释。他出了事、因此同会社决裂——这很快成了通行的小道消息，几个走了样的版本四处流传，个个都大大替他增光。他在班级主体中的影响随之见涨，金贝尔有一两回凑上来，提出推他竞选赛艇干事或者三年级舞会干事。

退会约一个月后的一天，他裹着晨衣，趿拉着下去洗冷水澡，结结实实吃了一惊——这一眼，把他付出的代价活生生摆到了他脸上。

依照多年的老例，墙上惯会写着几份试探性的名单——本班可能入选三个毕业班会社的人选。有那么一份名单，打从起头他的名字就高居榜首，那天天照面的熟稔景象，一直给他一种暖烘烘的、稳稳的快意。

募地，他往墙上一看：他名字原来的位置糊着一团铅灰，领头的，换成了贝恩和亨特。

「估摸这会儿他们把我挪到最后几名去了。」他长出一口气说。他把名单搜了一遍——他的名字连上头都没有。这份他本人也认的公论，竟还有这样的力道，把他伤到深处。

「就这样了——我早知道。」他说；可这话是说在苦味里的，带着一层更新、更尖的领会。

他当真开始觉出圈外人的滋味，为一锅端的全部不公起而反抗，心一寸寸硬进苦味里。亨格福德和鲍勃·斯托里，还有多皮·麦克纳布，常想同他走动，可他看穿了他们的用心，傲气一触即跳，反倒躲着他们。

与此同时，对二年级会社的反对升到公然造反的地步，一场群众大会开了起来——如预谋的那样，搅动了全国的报界，也从校友那里卷来一阵抗议的风暴。

至于斯托弗自己，尽管一心想跳到前台直接开炮，几番天人交战之后，还是沉默了——他觉得自己被入会那天立下的誓捆着。

===Original===

Shortly after the news spread like wildfire that the President, taking cognizance of the intolerable state of affairs, had summoned representatives of the three sophomore societies before him, and given them a month to deliberate and decide on some scheme of reform that would be comprehensive and adequate.
Rightly or wrongly, Stover felt that these developments intensified the feeling of the society element against him. A few weeks outside the boundaries, despite all his bravado, had brought home to him how much he cared for the companionship of those from whom he had separated.
Regan was his one friend; Brockhurst stimulated him; and in the intercourse with Swazey, Pike, Lake, Ricketts, and others he had found a certain inspiration. But after all, the men of his own kind﻿—Story, Hungerford, and others, whom from pride he now avoided﻿—were largely the men of the society crowd. They spoke a language he understood, they came from a home that was like his home, and their judgment of him would go with him out into the new relations in life.
It was a time of depression and bitter revolt at what he knew was the injustice of his ostracism, forgetting how much was of his own proud choosing.
He wandered from crowd to crowd, rather taciturn and restless, seeking diversion with a consuming nervousness. The new restlessness of spirit drove him away from the conferences in Regan’s and Swazey’s rooms to the company of idlers. For a period, in his pride and bitterness, he let go of himself, flung the reins to the wind, and started down hill with a gallop.
In pursuance of his policy of open defiance, he chose to appear at Mory’s with the wildest element of the class. His companions were a little in awe of his grim, concentrated figure; when he sat into a game of poker or joined a table of revelers, he did it with no zest. He never joined in the chorus, and if he occasionally broke out into a boisterous laugh, there was always a jarring note to it, that caused his companions to glance at him uneasily. With the impetuousness of his nature, he outstripped his associates, plunging deeper and deeper, obstinately resolved, into the black gulf of his cynicism. In a week his excesses became college gossip, and, unknown to Stover, the subject of many long conferences among his friends.

===Chinese===

不久，一个消息野火般传开：校长过问了这桩忍无可忍的局面，把三个二年级会社的代表召到跟前，限他们一个月，议决出一套全面而够用的整改方案。

对也罢错也罢，斯托弗觉得这些动静把会社一派对他的情绪烧得更旺了。界外这几个星期，任凭他怎么虚张，也让他搋明白了：他多么在乎他割舍掉的那些人的陪伴。

里根是他独一无二的朋友；布罗克赫斯特给他刺激；同斯韦齐、派克、莱克、里基茨这些人的往来，也给他某种启发。可说到底，他自己那一类人——斯托里、亨格福德，还有别的如今出于傲气躲着的人——大多正是会社圈里的人。他们说他听得懂的话，他们出自一个同他家一样的家，而他们对他的评判，会跟着他走进此后人生的一重重新关系里。

那是一段消沉而又苦涩反抗的日子——他反抗着他明知不公的放逐，却忘了这放逐里有多少是他自己傲着挑下的。

他一伙一伙地流浪，话少，心浮，带着一股烧人的躁动着找消遣。这股新的心浮，把他从里根和斯韦齐屋里的论道，赶去了闲人堆里。有一阵子，在傲气与苦味里，他撒开了自己，缰绳丢给风，一路纵马下坡。

本着公然叫板的路数，他专挑班里最野的一路，出没于莫里氏馆。伙伴们对他那阴沉专注的身影有点发怵；他坐进一局扑克，或凑上一桌欢宴，全无兴致。他从不跟着唱和；偶尔爆出一声大笑，笑声里总有一根刺，叫同伴们不放心地眓他。凭着天性里的那股横劲，他甩开了同伴，一头比一头扎得深——固执地扎进他那愤世的黑渊。不出一星期，他的荒唐成了全校的谈资；而他不知道，也成了朋友们一场又一场长议的题目。

===Original===

One Friday night, as, straying aimlessly from room to room, he set out for Mory’s in quest of Tom Kelly and a group of Sheff pagans, he was trudging along the hard ways in front of Welch Hall, fists sunk in his pockets, head down under a slouch hat, when he chanced on Tom Regan coming out of the Brick Row.
“Hello there, bantam,” said Regan, with the prerogative of his size.
“Hello, Tom,” he said, but without enthusiasm, for he had rather avoided him in company with the rest of his old friends.
“That’s a deuced cordial greeting! Where are you bound, stranger?”
“Mory’s.”
“Mory’s,” said Regan, appearing to consider. “Good idea. I’ve got a hankering after a toby of musty ale and a rabbit myself. Wait till I stow these books and I’ll join you.”
Stover stood frowning, suspicious and rebelling, for at that age it is a point of honor, when a man of the world resolves to run his head against a stone wall, that any interference from a friend is regarded as an unwarranted insult.
“He thinks he’ll try the big brother act on me,” he said, scowling. He was not in a particularly good humor, nor was his head clear from several nights that had gone their reeling way.
When they entered Mory’s, Tom Kelly, Dopey McNab, and Buck Waters were already grouped in the inner room.
“Well, old flinthead, how do you feel after last night?” said Kelly, making room for them.
“Fine,” said Dink mendaciously, secretly pleased at the tribute to his sporting talents before Regan.
“More’n I can say,” said Dopey, affectionately feeling of his head. “Curse the man who invented fish-house punch.”
“Get home all right?” continued Kelly.
“Sure.”
“I had a little tiff with a cop. If he’d been smaller, I’d have taken his shield away. He was most impudent. Never mind, I beat him in a foot race.”
“Cocktails,” said Stover, resolved that Regan should be well punished. “Make it two for me, Louis, I’ll have to catch up.”
“I’ll stick to a toby and a rabbit,” said Regan, without a change of expression.
“Cocktail, Dopey?” continued Stover, with a millionaire gesture.
“I never refuse,” said Dopey, who planned to go through life on that virtuous method.
With such a beginning, matters progressed with remarkable facility. Stover, taciturn and in an ugly mood, constantly hurried the rounds, matching drink for drink, secretly resolved to prove his supremacy here as elsewhere.

===Chinese===

一个星期五夜里，他挨屋游荡了一阵，又动身去莫里氏馆找汤姆·凯利和一帮谢菲尔的异教徒，正埋着头、塌着帽檐、两手插兜，沿韦尔奇馆前的硬路蹭着走，迎面撞上从砖楼排出来的汤姆·里根。

「好啊，小斗鸡。」里根说，端着他那身块的特权。

「你好，汤姆。」他说，却不带热乎——旧友那一拨，他近来都躲，里根也在内。

「这招呼可真够亲热的！上哪儿去，外乡人？」

「莫里氏。」

「莫里氏。」里根说，做沉思状。「好主意。我正好馋一壶陈麦酒加一份兔肉。等我把这几本书安顿了，同你一道。」

斯托弗皱眉站着，又疑又抗——在那个年纪，江湖汉子既已铁了心拿脑袋去撞石墙，朋友任何的插手，都算一条铁律：视同无端的侮辱。

「他打算跟我演大哥这出戏。」他沉着脸想。他心情本就不佳，脑袋也还被前几天东倒西歪的夜熬得不清爽。

两人进莫里氏，汤姆·凯利、多皮·麦克纳布和巴克·沃特斯已经聚在里间。

「哟，老打火石，昨晚之后感觉如何？」凯利挪出位子说。

「好极了。」丁克扯着谎说，当着里根的面被夸酒量，暗暗受用。

「比不上我。」多皮疼爱地摸着自己的脑袋说。「咒那个发明渔屋潘趣的家伙。」

「到家没出事吧？」凯利接着问。

「没事。」

「我同一个条子小小吵了一架。他要是再矮点，我就把他徽章卸了。放肆得很。不碍事，赛跑我赢了他。」

「鸡尾酒。」斯托弗说，铁了心要让里根好好吃一顿教训。「给我来双份，路易斯，我得追上来。」

「我还是一壶陈酒加兔肉。」里根说，面色纹丝不动。

「多皮，鸡尾酒？」斯托弗接着问，甩出一个百万富翁的手势。

「我从不拒绝。」多皮说——他打算一辈子靠这条美德过活。

有了这样的开头，事情进展得异乎寻常地顺溜。斯托弗话少，脸色不善，一轮轮地催，一杯对一杯地拼，心里暗暗拿定主意：在这里也要像别处一样，证明他是老大。

===Original===

Regan, after two tobies, withdrew from the contest, sitting silently puffing on his huge pipe, but without attempt at interference. Bob Story and Hungerford came in, and went away with a glance at Stover’s clouded face and Regan’s stolid, unfathomable expression. When midnight arrived, and Louis came in with apologies to announce the closing, there was quite a reckoning to be paid.
Stover was the best of the lot, doggedly resolved to show no effects of what he had taken. He felt a haziness in his vision, and words that were spoken seemed to be whirled away without record, but his legs stood firm, and his head was still under control. Buck Waters and a Sheff man took Tom Kelly home by a circuitous route to avoid either a wrestling match or a foot race with too zealous members of the New Haven police force; and Stover had the fierce pride of showing Regan that he could take charge of the hilarious but wabbly Dopey McNab, who, moved by the finest feelings of the brotherhood of man, was determined to scatter his superfluous change among his brother beings.
With great dignity and impressiveness, Stover, supporting one side, continued to give foggy directions to Regan on the other, until, come to McNab’s quarters, they delivered that joyously exuberant person into his bed, propped up his head, opened the window, locked the door and left the key outside, to insure the termination of the night’s adventure.
Stover went down the steep, endless stairs with great deliberation and minute pains.
“Dopey’s got weak head﻿—no good﻿—stand nothing,” he said seriously to Regan.
“Well, we’ve fixed him up for the night,” said Regan cheerily. “You’ve got a wonderful top, old sport.”
“I’m pretty good﻿—Dopey’s got the weak head,” said Stover, taking his arm. “I’m good, I can put ’em under the table﻿—all under the table.”
“Good for you.”
“Tom, you aren’t﻿—aren’t in critical at‑attochood, are you?” said Dink, with all feeling of resentment gone.
“Lord, no, boy.”
“ ’Cause it does me good﻿—this does me good. I feel bad﻿—pretty bad, Tom, about some things. You don’t know﻿—can’t tell﻿—but I feel bad﻿—this does me good﻿—forget﻿—you understand.”
“I understand.”
“You’re a good friend, Tom. They don’t understand﻿—no one else understands. I’d like to shake hands. Thank you. Good night.”

===Chinese===

两壶陈酒下肚，里根退出了比赛，坐着闷头抽他那杆大烟斗，却半点没有干涉的意思。鲍勃·斯托里和亨格福德进来了一趟，看一眼斯托弗阴下来的脸、里根那副木然莫测的神色，走了。午夜到了，路易斯一路道歉进来宣布打烊，账单相当可观。

斯托弗是这一拨里状态最好的，咬着牙一滴酒意也不肯露。他眼里起了一层雾，人家说的话像被风卷走、不留记录，可两条腿站得笔直，脑袋还握在手里。巴克·沃特斯和一名谢菲尔的绕着远道送汤姆·凯利回家，免得同纽黑文警察局里过于热心的成员摔一场跤或者赛一场跑；而斯托弗则怀着凶狠的骄傲，向里根显示他能接管兴高采烈却脚下打晃的多皮·麦克纳布——此君被人类手足之情的最美好情感打动，一心要把多余的零钱撒给天下弟兄。

斯托弗架着一边，威仪赫赫地继续给另一边的里根下达雾腾腾的指令，直到麦克纳布的住处，把那位欢腾人物安进床里，垫好头，开了窗，锁了门，钥匙留在外头，以确保今夜这场历险就此收场。

斯托弗一级一级下那陡得没有尽头的楼梯，拿出十二分的从容、一寸一寸的小心。

「多皮脑袋不行——没本事——顶不住事。」他一本正经地对里根说。

「嗯，夜里这就算安顿他了。」里根轻快地说。「你这块天灵盖真神，老伙计。」

「我相当能喝——多皮脑袋不行。」斯托弗挽住他的胳膊。「我行，我能把他们都放桌子底下去——全放桌子底下去。」

「有你的。」

「汤姆，你不——不摆批判姿——姿态吧？」丁克说，一肚子怨气全消了。

「天哪，不，伙计。」

「因为这让我痛快——这让我痛快。我心里难受——挺难受，汤姆，为一些事。你不知道——说不上——可我难受——这让我痛快——忘了——你懂。」

「我懂。」

「你是好朋友，汤姆。他们不懂——没别人懂。我想握个手。谢谢你。晚安。」

===Original===

They had come opposite the Brick Row, and Regan, knowing the other’s true condition, would have preferred to see him along to his room. But he knew of old the danger of making mistakes, so he said:
“Feel all right, old bantam?”
“Fine.” Stover took a step or two, and then returned. “I put ’em to bed, didn’t I?”
“You certainly did.”
“Never ’fects me.”
“You’re a wonder.”
“I thank you for your company.”
“Good night.”
Stover, intent only on making his entry, a hundred yards away, felt a roaring in his ears, and sudden jumble and confusion before him.
“Must get there﻿—self-control﻿—that’s it, self-control,” he said to himself, and by a supreme effort he reached his entry, pushed open the door, and, stumbling in out of Regan’s vision, sat heavily down on the steps.
Some indistinct time after he beheld before him a little spectacled figure in pink pajamas.
“Who are you?” he said.
“Wookey, sir.”
“What’s your class?”
“Freshman, sir.”
“Very well. All right. You can help me﻿—help me up. You know me?”
“Yes, sir.”
The pink pajamas approached, and with an effort he rose, and, grasping the proffered shoulder, tumbled up the steps. When he reached his room his mind seemed to clear a moment, like the sudden drifting to and fro of a fog.
“Who are you?” he said, frowning.
“Wookey, sir.”
“Where do you room?”
“On the first landing, sir.”
“Why do you wear pink ones?”
The little freshman, hero-worshipper, face to face with his first great emotion, the conduct of an intoxicated man, blurted out:
“Don’t you like ’em, sir?”
“Keep ’em on,” said Stover magnanimously. “So you’re a freshman.”
“Yes, sir.”
Suddenly he felt impressed with his duty, his obvious duty to one below him.
“Freshman,” he said thickly, “I want you listen to me. Never drink to excess﻿—understand. You beginning college﻿—school of character﻿—hold on yourself﻿—lead a good life﻿—self-control’s the great thing﻿—take it from me﻿—understand?”
“Yes, sir,” said Wookey, awed and a little frightened at the service he was rendering to the great Dink Stover.
“That’s all,” said Stover benignly. “Is﻿—is my bedroom still there?”
“Yes, sir.”
“You may lead me to it.”
When he had been brought to his bed he recalled the pink pajamas, and said:
“I thank you for your courtesy and your kindness.” Then he said to himself: “It does me good﻿—forget﻿—happy now.”
A moment later the fog closed over his consciousness again and he was asleep.

===Chinese===

两人已走到砖楼排对面，里根知道他的真实状况，本想送他到门口。可他老早就懂得多此一举的危险，便说：

「没事吧，老斗鸡？」

「好得很。」斯托弗走出一两步，又折回来。「我把他们安上床了，是不是？」

「可不。」

「从来醉不倒我。」

「你是个奇迹。」

「多谢你作陪。」

「晚安。」

斯托弗一门心思只求走进百码外的楼门，耳边却轰的一声，眼前忽然乱作一团。

「必须走到——自控——就是这个，自控。」他对自己说，凭着一记超人的努力捾到楼门，推开门，跌进里根视线之外，沉沉地坐在了台阶上。

不知过了多久一段模糊的时间，他看见面前一个戴眼镜的小个子身影，穿着粉色睡衣。

「你是谁？」他说。

「伍基（Wookey），先生。」

「几年级？」

「一年级，先生。」

「很好。行。你可以帮我——扶我一把。认识我吗？」

「认识，先生。」

粉睡衣凑上来，他一使劲起身，抓住递过来的肩膀，跌跌撞撞上了台阶。到了房门口，他的脑子仿佛清明了一瞬，像一阵雾忽然来回漂开。

「你是谁？」他皱着眉说。

「伍基，先生。」

「住哪儿？」

「一楼楼梯口，先生。」

「你怎么穿粉色的？」

小个子新生，一个崇拜英雄的少年，平生头一遭直面大事——一个醉汉的行止——脱口而出：

「您不喜欢吗，先生？」

「接着穿。」斯托弗宽宏大量地说。「这么说你是一年级。」

「是，先生。」

忽然他搋出了自己的责任——他对一个低年级者显而易见的责任。

「一年级生，」他口齿含混地说，「我要你听我说。永远不要喝多——明白。你刚进大学——品格的学校——攋住自己——过好日子——自控是头一件——听我的——明白？」

「是，先生。」伍基说，为伟大的丁克·斯托弗效这一场劳，又敬畏又有点怕。

「就这样。」斯托弗慈祥地说。「我——我的卧室还在那儿吗？」

「在，先生。」

「你可以领我过去。」

被送到床上后，他想起了那身粉睡衣，说：

「多谢你的礼数和好意。」随后他对自己说：「这让我痛快——忘了——现在高兴了。」

过了一会儿，雾重新合拢他的意识，他睡着了。

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

Night after night, Wookey, the little freshman from a mountain village of Maine, the shadow of a grind, whom no one knew in his class, and who would never know anyone, waited over his books the hour of twelve and the arrival of the great man gone wrong, whose secret only he possessed. Sometimes at the clatter on the stairs, when he went out eagerly, the hero would be in control, and would say:
“Hello, Wookey, how are you tonight?”
“All right, sir,” he would answer, shifting from foot to foot, afraid to volunteer assistance.
“All right myself,” Stover would answer. “See you tomorrow. Good night.”
Gradually, however, to his delight, Stover grew to like the strange meetings, and permitted him to accompany him to his room to open the window, draw off the boots and disappear with the promise to thunder on his door in time for chapel. In the daytime they never met.
Stover never failed to thank him with the utmost ceremony. Often the dialogue that ensued was farcically humorous, only little Wookey, solemn as an owl, never laughed.
One night Stover, draped in difficult equilibrium on the mantelpiece, suddenly, in his new parental solicitude for the freshman, bethought himself of the curriculum.
“Wookey.”
“Yes, sir.”
“One thing must speak about﻿—meant speak about long time ago.”
“What, sir?” said Wookey, looking up apprehensively over his spectacles.
“Study,” said Stover, with terrific solemnity. “Want you be good scholar.”
“Oh, yes, sir.”
“Want you be validict﻿—you understand what mean?”
“Yes, sir.”
“Wookey, college life serious, finest thing in it’s study, don’t neglect study, you understand.”
“Yes, sir; I do study pretty hard.”
“Not enough,” said Stover furiously. “Study all time! What ’cher do today? Recite in﻿—in Greek, Latin, eh?”
“Yes, sir﻿—all right.”
“Good, very good﻿—proud of you, Wookey,” said Stover, satisfied. “Must be good influence﻿—understand that, Wookey. Going to ask every night.”
“Yes, sir.”
“All right. Go an’ study now. Study lot more.”
This feeling of the influence he was exerting for Wookey’s academic betterment was so strong in Dink when the hour of midnight had passed that shortly after he brought McNab home with him to witness his works.
When Wookey appeared, something displeased Stover. His protégé was not as he should be presented. Suddenly he remembered﻿—Wookey was not in the pink pajamas!
“Wookey,” he said sternly.
“Yes, sir.”

===Chinese===

一夜又一夜，伍基（Wookey）——那个从缅因州山村里来的一年级小个子，一个书呆子的影子，班里没人认得他、他也不会去认得谁——守着他的书，守着十二点的钟和那位走错了路的大人物的到来；那人的秘密只有他一个人握着。有时楼梯上一响，他急急迎出去，英雄还自己管着自己，会说：
「喂，伍基，今晚可好？」
「好，学长，」他答道，两脚倒来倒去，不敢主动上去擕扶。
「我也好，」斯托弗答。「明天见。晚安。」
可是渐渐地，叫他欢喜的是，斯托弗竟喜欢上了这奇怪的相会，容他陪进屋，开窗、脱靴，再带着一句「礼拜堂前准时擂你的门」的许诺退下。白天，两人从不照面。
斯托弗从不忘以最高的礼数谢他。随后的对白常常滑稽透顶，只是小个子伍基，严肃得像只猫头鹰，从来不笑。
一天夜里，斯托弗以危难的平衡披在壁炉架上，忽然，凭着新添的家长式的关切，想起了课程的事。
「伍基。」
「是，学长。」
「一件事必须说——早该说，早该说。」
「什么，学长？」伍基说，从眼镜上头担心地抬眼。
「用功，」斯托弗以雷霆般的庄严说。「要你——做好学生。」
「哦，是，学长。」
「要你——当告别辞代表——懂我意思吗？」
「懂，学长。」
「伍基，大学生活是严肃的，里头顶好的东西是用功；不许荒废功课，明白。」
「是，学长；我挺用功的。」
「不够，」斯托弗大怒。「要时时刻刻用功！今天干什么了？背——背希腊文、拉丁文，嗯？」
「是，学长——背了。」
「好，很好——为你骄傲，伍基，」斯托弗满意地说。「要做好影响——明白吗，伍基。往后每晚都要问。」
「是，学长。」
「好。去吧，用功去。多用功。」
午夜一过，这份自己正在为伍基的学业造福的感觉，在丁克身上如此强烈，不久他便把麦克纳布带回家来参观他的善举。
伍基一露面，斯托弗有点不痛快。他的被保护人，出场不够规格。他猛然想起——伍基没穿那身粉红睡衣！
「伍基，」他严厉地说。
「是，学长。」

===Original===

“The pink ones,” he said solemnly.
“Very well, sir.”
“Hurry.”
“Yes, sir.”
“Study’s better in pink,” said Stover wisely to McNab, who was trying to exceed him in dignity. “Most becomin’.”
“Aha!”
“Make him study, Dopey,” continued Stover. “I make him study.”
“Want hear’m reshite,” said McNab, unconvinced.
When Wookey, in changed costume, came puffing upstairs, books under his arm, McNab, who had been exhorted by Stover, viewed the pink pajamas with deliberation, and said:
“Like you in pink, Wookey; always wear ’em. Want to hear you reshite.”
“Reshite,” said Stover.
“Hold up,” said Dopey, scratching his head.
“What’s matter?”
“Where going to sleep?”
“Wookey, suggestions?” said Stover, who added in a thundering whisper to McNab, “Always leave such things to Wookey.”
The freshman busily took down the cushions from the window seat, piled up the pillows at one end before the fire, and brought up a rug.
“Thank Mr. Wookey,” said Stover severely.
“Mr. Wookey, I thank you,” said McNab, who sat down tailor fashion, and, staring at a book of geometry open on his lap, said: “I’m most﻿—interested﻿—most, very fond of Horace﻿—reshite.”
Wookey in the pink pajamas, seated in a sort of spinal bend, overwhelmed by the terrifying delight of being admitted to the company of Olympians, began directly to translate an ode of Horace.
McNab, staring at the geometry, turned a casual page, remarking from time to time severely:
“What’s that!﻿—oh, yes, h’m﻿—quite right﻿—free, rather free, Dink﻿—not bad, not bad for freshman.”
“Is it all right?” said Stover anxiously.
“All right.”
“All my influence,” said Stover.
“Wookey,” said McNab, as a judge would say it, “very fortunate, sir, have such good infloonce. Con‑grath‑ulate you.”
Wookey, whether deceived by their drunken assumption of sobriety, or to conciliate dangerous men, remained in his corner, his book closed, blinking out from his wide glasses.
McNab, remembering the beginning of a discussion in which he had engaged with serious purpose, suddenly began, shaking his head:
“Dink, you ought be better infloonce than y’are.”
Stover chose to be offended.
“Why you say that?”
“ ’Cause ’m right; y’oughtn’t drink, not a drop!”
“What right you got to say that?”
“Every right﻿—every,” said McNab, trying to remember what was the original destination of his argument. “I’m bad example ’n you’re good infloonce, there’s diff, see?”
“Ratsh!”

新生手忙脚乱地把窗座上的垫子卸下来，把枕头在炉火前一头堆起来，又拖来一条毯子。
「谢谢伍基先生，」斯托弗严厉地说。
「伍基先生，谢谢您，」麦克纳布说，盘腿坐下，蹬着摊在膝头的一本几何，说：「我最——感兴趣——顶顶喜欢贺拉斯——背吧。」
伍基穿着粉红睡衣，弓成一段脊椎的弯，被准许进入奥林匹斯诸神会社的骇人的欢喜压垮了，开口就译起贺拉斯的一首颂歌。
麦克纳布蹬着几何，随手翻过一页，不时严厉地评点：
「这是什么！——哦，对，嗯——没错——太自由了，相当自由，丁克——不赖，新生不赖。」
「还行吗？」斯托弗焦灼地问。
「行。」
「全是我的影响，」斯托弗说。
「伍基，」麦克纳布法官附体似的说，「好福气，先生，有这样好的影响。恭——贺——你。」
伍基——是被他们醉中装清醒的把戏骗过了，还是要安抚两个危险人物——缩在他的角落里，书合上了，从宽边眼镜后面眨着眼。
麦克纳布想起他本是一场正经讨论的开头，忽然摇着头开了腔：
「丁克，你该做个比现在更好的影响。」
斯托弗决定生气。
「你凭什么说这话？」
「凭我说得对；你不该喝，一滴也不该！」
「你有什么权说这话？」
「有全权——全权，」麦克纳布说，努力回想他这段论证原本要开到哪里去。「我是坏榜样，你是好影响，这里头有分别，明白？」
「呸！」

“I remember,” said McNab all at once. “I know what I want say. I’m going to leave it to Wookey. Wookey’ll be the judge﻿—referee﻿—y’willin’?”

When Stover drinks he goes at in bad way, no love humanity, joy of youth. That’s the point, y’understand. I want him to stop it, ’cause he’s my friend, he’s good infloonce﻿—I’m bad example.”

“Shake hands.”

“Never!”

“No!”

“Must stop it,” said McNab, firmly closing one eye, and gazing fearfully at the yellow owls in front.

“Promish?”
“Promise! Y’understand, want to forget.”
“Must stop it,” repeated McNab, turning from the yellow-eyed owls to Stover.
“Promish,” repeated Stover solemnly. A moment later he said sleepily: “I shay.”
“Shay it.”
“What﻿—what I going to stop?”
“What you, what﻿—” McNab frowned terrifically at the owls. “Stop﻿—must stop﻿—promish﻿—what﻿—what stop?”
The question being transferred to Stover, he in turn scratched his head and sought to concentrate his memory.
“I promished,” he said slowly, “remember that﻿—stop﻿—promish stop. Wookey!”
“Yes, sir.”
The pink pajamas approached with reluctance, and waited at a safe distance.
“Wookey! What﻿—what’s this all about? What’s it?”
Wookey, facing the crisis of his life, hesitated between two impulses; but at this moment the two took solemn hold of each other’s hands, vacillated and rolled over on the cushions.

But despite McNab’s more sober remonstrances and his own proclamation, Stover did not cease his headlong gallop down the hill of Rake’s Progress. He still avoided his old friends﻿—he had not been to the Storys’ home for weeks. Regan occasionally forced himself upon him, but never offered a suggestion. The truth was, Stover began to have a horror of his own society, of being left alone. What he did, he did without restraint. At the card tables to which he wandered he was always clamoring for the raising of the limit; always ready to eat up the night. Even the most inveterate of the gamblers in his class perceived what McNab perceived, that there was no pleasure in what he did, but a sort of self-immolation. They were a little in awe of him, uneasy when he was around. He wandered over into Sheff, and among a group of hard livers in the Law School, getting deeper and deeper into the maelstrom. Several times, returning unsteadily late at night, he had met Le Baron, who stood aside, and watched him go with difficulty towards the haven of his own entry, for Stover always made it a point of pride to reach home and Wookey unaided. He never was offensive or quarrelsome. On the contrary, his struggle was always for self-control and an excess of politeness.
The climax arrived one Friday night when, having outlasted the party, he had put Tom Kelly to bed, and was returning from Sheff alone. He was very well pleased with himself. He had delivered Tom Kelly to his friends and gone away without assistance.
“Weak head, all weak head,” he said to himself valiantly, “all but Stover, Dink Stover, old Rinky Dink. Self-control, great self-control. That’s it, that’s the point. Never taken home﻿—walk myself﻿—self-control.” He began to laugh at the memory of Tom Kelly, who had insisted on going to bed with one boot under the pillow and his watch on the floor. The excruciating humor of it almost made him collapse. He clung to the nearest tree and wept for joy.
“Never hear end of it﻿—Tom Kelly﻿—boots﻿—wonderful﻿—poor old Tom﻿—’n I walkin’ home﻿—alone.”
Someone on the opposite sidewalk, seeing him clinging hilariously, stopped. Stover straightened up instantly, adjusted his hat and started off.
“Mustn’t create false impression﻿—all right!

然而，尽管有麦克纳布后来清醒些的规劝，也有他自己的宣告，斯托弗并没有勒住他那一路朝《浪子历程》山下狂奔的马。他依旧躲着旧友——斯托里家他已有几个星期没去。里甘偶尔硬闯进来，却从不提一句建议。实情是，斯托弗开始害怕独处、害怕自己的会社。他做的事，做得毫无节制。在他流连的牌桌上，他永远嚷着抬高赌注；永远准备把整个夜晚吃下去。连班里最老牌的赌徒都看出了麦克纳布看出的事：他所做的没有一点乐趣，只有一种自我献祭。他们有点怕他，他在场就不自在。他荡到谢菲尔德去，荡进法学院一伙狂喝的人中间，在漩涡里越陷越深。有几回，深夜里他步子不稳地回来，撞见勒巴龙；勒巴龙闪在一边，看他艰难地挪向自己门洞的避风港——因为斯托弗向来把不需要人扶、自己走到家和伍基跟前当作体面。他从不撒野、从不寻衅。相反，他的挣扎永远是为了自制和过分的客气。
顶点在一个礼拜五夜里到了：他把全席熬散了，把汤姆·凯利安顿上床，独自从谢菲尔德往回走。他对自己满意得很。他把汤姆·凯利交到了他朋友们手上，独自走开，没要人扶。
「脑袋软，全都是脑袋软，」他豪迈地自语，「除了斯托弗——丁克·斯托弗，老叮当丁克。自制，了不起的自制。就是这个，这就是要点。从没让人送回家——自己走——自制。」想起汤姆·凯利他笑起来：那位非要枕头上底下一只靴子、手表搁在地板上才肯睡。这份绝妙的幽默差点叫他笑瘫。他抱住最近的一棵树，乐得直哭。
「这事没个完——汤姆·凯利——靴子——妙——可怜的老汤姆——我可是自己走回家——一个人。」
对面人行道上有人看见他抱着树乐，停了脚。斯托弗立刻站直，正一正帽子，上路。
