她忽然又加一句：「你自己掌起来，全由你自己办。这正是你该做的。」
他一门心思铺陈这个主意，没留意她对他的这份关切。过了一阵，话漂到别的题目上。他说起夏天。
「乔要我出海巡游，鲍勃要我上你们营地做客；可我差不多定了，哪样也不去。」
她抬起头。
「为什么不去？」
「我跟里甘过夏天——有人该叫它作逛贫民窟；汤姆叫它受真正的教育。我们下去，同干活的男人们一起干活，弄明白他们想什么、要什么——还有他们怎么看我们。这件事迷死我了。我要一副四平八稳的眼光。如今机会这么多，我要全抓住——能学的全学。你说呢？」
「好主意，眼下正该是你做的事。会把你撑大，」她说，坚决地把头一点。「鲍勃怎么从不把里甘带来？听着是个有趣的人。」
「不知道——他独来独往。搬不动他。咱们四个合住的事，鲍勃同你说了？」
「说了。」
「我纳闷——」
「什么？」见他停住，她问。
「今天下午他对我说的那些，是你授意的吗？」他直截了当地问。
她望着他，又乱又拿不定；他猛地猜到了缘故。
「哦，你就不能信我一回，告诉我，」他孩子气地说，「是不是你？」
她又往他眼睛里望了一刻。
「他怕你不高兴，」她简单地说。「是，是我叫他去 的。」
十来句话涌到他嘴边，他一句也没说。兴许是他的沉默比任何言语都更合她的意，因为她又添一句：
「往后你要做大事情了，对不对？你要明白，我要看见你最大的时候。」
---
当夜回家，他脚下像踩着云。他没有乘她友谊之危——尽管她声音里的那份温存和直直的恳求，几乎超过了他的定力。他必须找个人说一说——不是说她对他露出的那份关切，而是说他自己的全部倾倒和最高的献祭。于是他把亨格福德拽上楼，亨格福德听完斯托弗的心境汇报，又惊又喜，仿佛这是天下最不可信、最费解、最摸不着头脑的一条新闻。

## XXIII / 二十三
===Original===

When Stover returned to college as a junior, he showed the results of his summer with Regan. He had gone into construction gangs, and learned to obey and to command. He had had a glimpse of what the struggle for existence meant in the stirring masses; and he had known the keenness of a little joy and the reality of sorrow to those for whom everything in life was real.
He had long ago surrendered the idea of entering Skull and Bones over the enmity of Reynolds and Le Baron, and this relinquishing somehow robbed him of all the awe that he had once felt. He had returned a man, tempered by knowledge of the world, distinguishing between the incidental in college life and the vital opportunity within his grasp.
The new debating club, launched in the previous spring, had been an instant success, and its composition, carefully representative, had become the nucleus of a new comradeship in the class. With the one idea of proving his fitness to lead in this new harmonizing development, Stover made his room a true meeting-place of the class, and, loyally aided by Hungerford and Story, sought to restore all the old-time zest and goodwill to the gatherings about the sophomore fence. His efforts were met by a latent opposition from Hunter and Bain, on one side, who never outgrew their wounded resentment, and from Gimbel on the other, who, though enthusiastically seconding him in the open, felt secretly that he was being supplanted.
But, as Story had foreseen, Stover had the magnetism and the energy to carry through what no other leader would have accomplished. Once resolved on the accomplishment, upheld by a strong sentimental devotion, Stover went at his task with a blunt directness that disdains all objections.
Each Saturday night was given over to a rally of the class en masse at the Tontine. Certain groups held off at first, but soon came into the fold when Stover, who was no respecter of persons, would find occasion to say publicly:
“Hello there, what happened to you last night? Get out of that silklined atmosphere of yours! Wake up! You’re not too good for us, are you?”
“Well, why weren’t you there? It’s no orgy﻿—you can get lemonade or milk if you want. There are bad men present, but we keep ’em from biting.”
“I say, forget your poker game for one night. We all know you’re dead game sports. That’s why we want you﻿—to give us an atmosphere of real life.”

===Chinese===

斯托弗以三年级生的身份回到学校，身上带着同里根过了一个暑假的成果。他进了工程队，学会了服从，也学会了指挥。他瞥见了在翻涌的人群中生存斗争意味着什么；也尝到了一点小欢喜的锋利、悲伤的实在——对那些生活中样样都当真的人。

他早已断了越过雷诺兹和勒巴龙的敌意进髅髅会的念头，而这份舍弃，不知怎么把他从前的那点敬畏也一并夺走了。他回来时是个男子汉了，被对世界的认识淬过火，分得清大学生活里的枝节、和握在手中的要紧机会。

上一个春天创办的新辩论俱乐部一炮而响，成员经过斟酌、颇具代表性，成了班里一种新型情谊的核。抱着唯一一个念头——证明自己配领导这股新的和合之风——斯托弗把自己的屋子变成全班名副其实的聚点，又有亨格福德和斯托里死心塌地帮衬，一心要把旧日的热络与善意，还给二年级围栏边的一场场聚会。他的努力遇到了暗中的抵制：一头是亨特和贝恩，那点受了伤的怨气一辈子没长出去；另一头是金贝尔——明面上兴致勃勃给他帮腔，私下里只觉得自己正被取而代之。

可正如斯托里所料，斯托弗有那种磁力和那股劲，能办成任何别的领袖都办不成的事。目标一定，又有一股深切的赤忱托着，斯托弗干起来直不笼统，把一切异议都不放在眼里。

每个星期六晚上，全班倾巢出动，去唐丁（Tontine）大聚。有几个圈子起初观望，但很快就归了队——因为斯托弗这个不看来头的人，总寻得到机会当众说：

「喂，昨晚你怎么没来？从你那绸缎衬里的空气里出来！醒醒！你没那么金贵，不跟我们玩，是吧？」

「怎么了，昨晚你怎么不在？那不是酒池肉林——想喝柠檬水牛奶都有。在场是有几个坏人，我们看着，不让他们咬人。」

「我说，一个晚上忘掉你的扑克吧。谁不知道你是条铁打的汉子。正为这个我们要你——给大伙儿添点真实生活的气息。」

===Original===

The remarks were made half in jest, half in earnest, but they seldom failed of their object. At the Saturday night rallies it was the same. Stover was everywhere, saying with his good-humored, impudent smile what no one else dared to say, sometimes startling them with his boldness:
“Here now, fellows, no grouping around here. We want to see a sport and a gospel shark sitting arm in arm. Come on, Schley, your social position’s all right﻿—there’s only one crowd here tonight. No one here is going to boost you into a senior society. Percolate, fellows, percolate. We’ve scrapped like Sam Hill, now we’re tired of it. No more biting, scratching, or gouging. Don’t forget this is a love feast, and they’re going to be lovelier. Now let’s try over that song for the Princeton game. Bob Story perpetrated it﻿—pretty rotten, I think, but let’s hit it up all the same.”
The rallies jumped into popularity. The class gasped, then laughed at Stover’s abrupt reference to the late unpleasantness, and with the laugh all constraint went. The class found itself, as a regiment returns to its pride again. It went to the games in a body, it healed its differences, and packed the long room at the Tontine each Saturday night, shouting out the chorus which Buck Waters, McNab, Stone, and the talent led.
Many, undoubtedly, marvelling at the ease with which Stover had inspired the gathering, admired him for what they believed was a clever bid for society honors. But the truth was that he succeeded because he had no underlying motive, because he had achieved in himself absolute independence and fearlessness of any outer criticism, and his strength with the crowd was just the consciousness of his own liberty.
By the fall of junior year, he was the undisputed leader of the class, a force that had brought to it a community of interest and friendly understanding. Unknown to him, his classmates began to regard him, despite his old defiance, as one whom a senior society could not overlook. Stover had no such feeling. He believed that the hatred in what remained of the sophomore society organization was, and would continue, unrelenting, and this conviction had determined him in a course of action to which he was impelled by other reasons.

===Chinese===

这些话半真半假，却很少有落空的。星期六晚上的聚会上也一样。斯托弗无处不在，带着他那又和气又没脸没皮的笑，说着谁也不敢说的话，有时大胆得叫人吃惊：

「行了，弟兄们，这儿不许扎堆。我们要看见一条赌棍和一位福音鲨鱼挽着胳膊坐。来吧，施莱，你的社交地位稳得很——今晚这儿只有一个圈子。这儿没人能把您抬进毕业班会社。渗透起来，弟兄们，渗透起来。我们打得天昏地暗过，如今都打够了。不许再咬、再挠、再抠眼。别忘了这是场相爱宴，往后还要更相爱。现在把那支普林斯顿战的歌过一遍。鲍勃·斯托里炮制的——我看烂得很，可咱们照样唱响。」

聚会一夜爆红。全班先倒吸一口气，随即被斯托弗对「前一阵子那场不愉快」的直呼逗笑，一笑，所有的拘束全散了。班级重新找回了自己，像一团兵马重又找回军威。它成群结队去看比赛，把嫌隙一一抹平，每个星期六晚上挤满唐丁的长厅，吼着巴克·沃特斯、麦克纳布、斯通和那班好嗓子领的头。

无疑有许多人，惊叹斯托弗点起这场聚合竟如此轻松，钦佩他，以为这是为会社荣誉下的一步妙棋。可实情是：他成功，因为他没有藏在底下的动机，因为他已在自身修成了绝对的独立、对任何外界非议的全然无惧——他在人群中的力量，正是这份对自己自由的自觉。

到三年级上学期，他已是无可争辩的全班领袖，一股为班级带来共同利益与友善理解的力量。他不知道，同学们已开始把他——不顾他旧日的公然叫板——看作毕业班会社无法绕过的人。斯托弗自己没这个感觉。他相信，二年级会社残余组织里那份恨，是决绝的，往后也决绝下去；这份确信，连同另几重缘由，把他推上了一条既定的路。

===Original===

He went through the football season as he had gone through the previous season, with a record for distinguished brilliancy, acclaimed by all as the best end in years, the probable captain of the next year. He wanted the position, as he had desired it on his first arrival at Yale, and yet he surrendered it. Hunter had developed into a tackle and made the team. In the class below were two men of the defunct sophomore societies. Stover had vividly before him the record of Dana, his captain of freshman year, and the memory of the ordeal after the game, when he had stood up and acknowledged his lack of leadership.
That this still resentful society element in the eleven would follow him with distaste and reluctance, despite all traditional loyalty, he knew too well. Moreover, sure that he was destined to be passed over on Tap Day, he felt perhaps too keenly the handicap of such a rejection. Then, at the bottom, reluctantly, he knew in his heart that Regan was the born leader of men, and what once he had rebelled against he finally acknowledged.
So when at the end of a victorious season the members of the eleven gathered for the election of the next year’s captain, he stood up immediately and stated his views. It was a difficult announcement to make, both on the score of seeming sentimentality, and from the danger of seeming to refuse what might not be offered him.
But during the tests of the last year the self-consciousness which would have prevented Brockhurst’s expressing himself had completely gone. Determined on one course of action, to be his own master, to do what he wanted to do, and to say what he wanted to say, in absolute fearlessness, he spoke with a frankness that amazed his comrades, still under the fetish of upper-class supremacy.
“Before we begin,” he said, “I’ve a few words I want to say. I suppose I am a candidate here. I don’t say I shouldn’t be crazy to have the captaincy. I would﻿—anyone would. What I say is that I have thought it over and I withdraw my name. Even if you hadn’t in Tom Regan here the best type of leader you could get, it would be very unfortunate for our chances next year if I were chosen. I’m quite aware that in a certain element of the team, due to the open stand I felt forced to take in the question of the sophomore society, there is a great deal of resentment against me. I can understand that; it is natural.

===Chinese===

这个橄榄球赛季，他同上一个赛季一样打完，战绩辉煌，众口一词：多年来最好的边锋，明年的队长多半是他。这个位子他想要——同初到耶鲁时一样想——可他让了。亨特长成了截锋，进了队。低一级的班里，还有两个已散伙的二年级会社的人。斯托弗眼前活生生摆着达纳——他新生年的队长——的那笔账，还有赛后那场过堂的记忆：达纳站起来，认了自己领导无方。

队里那股余怨未消的会社势力，任凭传统如何讲忠义，跟着他也会一肚子嫌恶、一步三回头——这个他太清楚了。再说，他笃定拍肩日自己要被略过，这份落空会成多大的拖累，他也许搋得过重了。还有，在心底，他不情愿地知道：里根才是天生的领袖之才；从前他抗拒承认的，如今终于认了。

于是，凯旋赛季末了，队员们聚在一起选来年队长，他头一个站起来，亮明了自己的看法。这话不好讲——既怕显得矫情，又怕像是在推辞一件人家未必肯给的东西。

可经过去年的种种试炼，那层曾让布罗克赫斯特开不了口的自我意识，已从他身上褪得干干净净。认准了一条路——当自己的主，想做的事就做，想说的话就说，全无惧色——他一开口，坦率得叫队友们咋舌，他们还在高年级至上的偶像底下跪着呢。

「开选之前，」他说，「我有几句话要说。我算是一名候选人吧。我不否认，当上队长我会疯掉一样高兴。会的——谁不会。我要说的是：我反复想过了，我退出。就算你们手里没有汤姆·里根这个能找到的最好的领袖坯子，选了我也将大不利于我们明年的胜算。我很清楚，队里有一路人，因为我在二年级会社问题上被迫摆开的公开立场，对我积着一大股怨。这我明白；人之常情。

===Original===

But there should be no such division in a Yale team. We’ve got a tough fight next year, and we need a captain about whom are no enmities, who’ll command every bit of the loyalty of the team”﻿—he paused a moment﻿—“and every bit of help he can get from the college. I move that Tom Regan be unanimously elected captain.”
There was quite an outcry at the end of his declaration, especially from Regan, who was utterly surprised. But Stover held firm, and perceived, not without a little secret resentment, that the outcome came with relief not only to the team but to the coaches.
When they returned, and Regan was still protesting, Stover said frankly:
“Look here, Tom, we don’t split hairs with one another. If I had thought it was right for me to stand for it I would have. I wanted it﻿—like hell. You remember Dana? I do. It’s an awful thing to lead a team into defeat, and say I was responsible. I don’t care to do it. Besides, you are the better man﻿—and I’m of such a low, skulking nature I hate to admit it. So shut up and buy me a rabbit at Mory’s. I’m hungry as a pirate.”
He had said nothing of his determination to anyone. He had been tempted to talk it over with Jean Story, but he had refrained, feeling instinctively that in her ambition for him, and in her inability to judge the depth of certain antagonisms towards him, she would oppose his determination.
---
The four friends had gone to Lyceum together﻿—Swazey and Pike were in the same building. There was a certain flavor of the simplicity and ruggedness of old Yale in the building that gave to the meetings in their rooms a character of old-time spontaneity.
By the opening of the winter term, Stover, the enthusiast, had begun to see the weakness of movements that must depend on organization. The debating club, which had started with a zest, soon showed its limitations. Once the edge of novelty had worn off, there were too many diverting interests to throng in and deplete the ranks.
When, following Regan’s suggestion, they had attempted a new division on the lines of the political parties, the result was decidedly disappointing. There was no natural interest to draw upon, and the political discussions, instead of fanning the club into a storm of partisanship, lapsed into the hands of perfunctory debaters.
Regan himself took his disillusionment much to heart.

===Chinese===

可耶鲁的球队里不该有这样的裂痕。明年是场硬仗，我们需要一位身上不结仇怨的队长，他能调得动全队每一分忠诚」——他顿了一顿——「也调得动全校能给他的每一分助力。我提议，一致选举汤姆·里根为队长。」

他一说完，四下一阵喆然，里根尤其炸——他全然没有防备。可斯托弗咬定不放，并且看出来——不是没有一点暗暗的怨——这个结果不仅让全队松了口气，也让教练们松了口气。

回来后里根还在推辞，斯托弗坦率地说：

「听着，汤姆，咱们俩之间不掰扯字眼。我要是觉得自己该站出来，我就站了。我想过——想得要命。你还记得达纳吧？我记得。把一支队伍带进败局，再站起来说责任在我——那滋味太苦了。我不想再尝。再说了，你就是更强的人——我天生一副卑鄙窝囊的性子，最恨承认这个。所以闭嘴吧，到莫里氏请我吃份兔肉。我饿得像个海盗。」

这个决定他对谁都没说过。他一度想同琼·斯托里商量，终究忍住了——本能地觉得，凭她对他的期望，凭她搋不出某些敌意有多深，她会反对这个决定。

---

四个朋友一道搬进了吕克昂楼——斯韦齐和派克也在同一栋。那楼里有一种老耶鲁的质朴粗粝的味道，让他们屋里的聚谈带上了一层旧日天然去雕饰的性情。

到冬季学期开学，热忱的斯托弗已开始看出凡靠组织撑着的运动的软处。辩论俱乐部起头热热闹闹，很快就露出了它的限度。新鲜的刃口一钝，太多岔人的兴致便一拥而入，把队伍抽薄了。

按里根的主意，他们照政党的路子重新分了两派，结果败兴得很。没有天生的兴致可供抽取，那些政治辩题，非但没能把俱乐部扇成一场党争的风暴，反倒落进了几个敷衍辩客的手里。

里根自己把这份幻灭很放在心上。

===Original===

They discussed the reasons of the failure one stormy afternoon at one of their informal discussions, to which they had returned with longing.
“What the devil is the matter?” said the big fellow savagely. “Why, where I come from, the people I see, every mother’s son of them, feed on politics, talk nothing else﻿—they love it! And here if you ask a man if he’s a Republican or a Democrat, he writes home and asks his father. A condition like this doesn’t exist anywhere else on the face of the globe. And this is America. Why?”
When he had propounded the question, there was a busy, unresponsive puffing of pipes, and then Pike added:
“That’s what hits me, too. Just look at the questions that are coming up; popular election of senators, income tax, direct primaries; it’s like building over the government again, and no one here cares or knows what’s doing. I say, why?”
“There may be fifty-two reasons for it,” said Brockhurst, in his staccato, biting way. “One is, our colleges are all turning into social clearing houses, and everyone is too absorbed in that engrossing process to know what happens outside; second is the fact that our universities are admirably organized instruments for the prevention of learning!”
“Good old Brocky,” said Swazey with a chuckle. “Just what I like; stormy outside, warm inside, and Brocky at the bat. Serve ’em up.”
Brockhurst, who was used to this reception of his pointed generalizations, paid no heed. He, too, had grown in mental stature and in control. A certain diffidence was over him, and always would be; but when a subject came up that interested him, he forgot himself, and rushed into the argument with a zeal that never failed to arouse his listeners.
Brockhurst turned on Swazey with the license that was always permissible.
“Well, what do you know? You’ve been here going on three years. You are supposed to be more than half educated. And you’re not a fair example either, because you really are seeking to know something.”
“Well, go on,” said Swazey, thoroughly aroused.
“What do you know about the Barbizon school, and the logical reasons for the revolt of the impressionists?”
Instantly there was an outcry:
“Not fair.”
“Oh, I say.”
“That’s no test.”
“Finishing your third year, gentlemen,” said Brockhurst triumphantly, “age over twenty; the art of painting is of course known to the aborigines only in its cruder forms.

===Chinese===

一个风雨大作的下午，在他们又盼回来的那种不拘形式的聚谈上，他们讨论起失败的缘由。

「到底见了什么鬼？」大个子恶狠狠地说。「嗐，我来的地方，我见的人，一个不落，全拿政治当饭吃，张口闭口没别的——他们爱这个！在这儿，你问一个人是共和党还是民主党，他写信回家问他爹。这样的光景，普天之下再没第二处。这儿可是美国。为什么？」

问题一抛出，一时只有烟斗忙碌的吧嗒，谁也不接，随后派克补上：

「这也是扎我的地方。瞧瞧正在冒头的那一堆问题：参议员直选、所得税、直接初选；等于把政府重造一遍，这儿却没人上心、没人知道在干什么。我问的是，为什么？」

「缘由嘛，怕有五十二条，」布罗克赫斯特用他那顿挫的、带刺的调子说。「其一：我们的大学全在变成社交清算所，人人埋头这场热闹，顾不上知道外头出了什么事；其二：我们的大学，是一台台组织精良、专防人学习的器械！」

「好样的老布，」斯韦齐咯咯笑。「正合我意：外头风雨，屋里暖和，老布执棒开球。尽管发球。」

布罗克赫斯特对这份迎候他锋利高论的待遇早已习惯，并不理会。他也在心智的个头和克制上长了。一层羞怯还罩着他，永远会罩着；可一旦撞上他感兴趣的题目，他就忘了自己，一头扎进论战，那股热忱没有一回点不着听众。

布罗克赫斯特拿斯韦齐开刀——这样的放肆向来是被准许的。

「那么，你知道些什么？你来这儿快三年了。按说已受了过半的教育。可你还算不得公道的样本，因为你当真是在求知的。」

「行，接着来。」斯韦齐说，兴致彻底上来了。

「巴比松画派你知道多少？印象派造反的逻辑缘由呢？」

立时一片抗议：

「不公平。」

「喂，不至于。」

「这不算考。」

「三年级都快念完了，诸位先生，」布罗克赫斯特得意洋洋地说，「年过二十；绘画这门艺术，土著自然只认得它较粗糙的形态。

===Original===

Well, does anyone know at least who Manet is, or what he’s painted?”
There was an accusing silence.
“Of course you’ve an idea of the Barbizon school﻿—one or two of you. You remember something about a *Man with a Hoe* or the *Angelus*﻿—that’s Sunday supplement education. Now let me try you. Please raise hands, little boys, when you know the answer to these questions, but don’t bluff teacher. I’m not contending you should have a detailed knowledge of the world in your eager, studious minds. I am saying that you haven’t the slightest general information. I’ll make my questions fair.
“First, music: I won’t ask you the tendencies and theories of the modern schools﻿—you won’t know that such a thing as a theory in music exists. You know the opera of *Carmen*﻿—good old Toreadore song. Do you know the name of the composer? One hand﻿—Bob Story. Do you know the history of its reception? Do you know the sources of it? Do you know what Bach’s influence was in the development of music? Did you ever hear of Leoncavallo, Verdi, or that there is such a thing as a Russian composer? Absolute silence. You have a hazy knowledge of Wagner, and you know that Chopin wrote a funeral march. That is your foothold in music; there you balance, surrounded by howling waters of ignorance.
“Take up architecture. Do you know who built the Vatican? Do you know the great buildings of the world﻿—or a single thing about Greek, Roman and Renaissance architecture? Do you know what the modern French movement is based upon? Nothing.
“Take up religion. Do you know anything about Confucius, Shintoism, or Swedenborg, beyond the names? Of course you would not know that under Louis XVI a determined movement was made to reunite the Catholic and Protestant branches, which almost succeeded. That’s unfair, because of course it is the forerunner of the great religious movement today. Do you know the history of the external symbols of the Christian religion, and what is historically new? Darkness denser and denser.
“Take literature. You have excavated a certain amount of Shakespeare, and grubbed among Elizabethans, and cursed Spenser. Who has read Taine’s *History of English Literature*, or known in fact who Taine is? Only Bob Story. And yet there is the greatest book on the whole subject; you could abolish the English department and substitute it.

===Chinese===

那么，至少有谁知道马奈（Manet）是谁、画过什么吗？」

一阵控诉般的沉默。

「巴比松画派诸位当然有个概念——你们里头的一两位。还记得一点《扶锄的人》或者《晚祷》——那是周日增刊教育。现在我来考考你们。知道答案的小男孩们请举手，不过别蒙老师。我不是说你们这些勤勉好学的头脑里就该装着世界的详细知识。我说的是，你们连最起码的通识都没有。我出题公道点。

「第一门，音乐：现代乐派的趋势和理论我不问——你们根本不知道音乐里还有『理论』这种东西。歌剧《卡门》（*Carmen*）你们知道——好听的老斗牛士之歌。作曲家叫什么，知道吗？一只手——鲍勃·斯托里。它当年问世的风波知道吗？它的来路知道吗？巴赫对音乐发展的影响是什么，知道吗？莱翁卡瓦洛、威尔第听说过吗？俄国也有作曲家这回事听说过吗？绝对的沉默。瓦格纳你们有个影影绰绰的印象，肖邦写了一支葬礼进行曲，这个你们知道。这就是你们在音乐里的立足点；你们就踩在那儿晃，四外是无知的一片喆哮的汪洋。

「再考建筑。梵蒂冈是谁主持建的，知道吗？世界的伟大建筑知道几座吗——希腊、罗马、文艺复兴的建筑，知道一星半点吗？现代法国那场运动基于什么，知道吗？零。

「再考宗教。孔子、神道教、斯维登堡——名字之外还知道什么吗？你们当然不会知道，路易十六治下曾有一场铁了心的运动，要让天主教与新教两派复合，差一点成了。这题出得不公道，因为它本来就是今日那场大宗教运动的前奏。基督教外在象征的历史、以及其中历史上新出的东西，知道吗？黑暗，一层比一层深。

「再考文学。你们发掘了一定分量的莎士比亚，在伊丽莎白时代作家里刨过土，咒骂过斯宾塞。谁读过泰纳的《英国文学史》（*History of English Literature*）——甚至谁知道泰纳是谁？只有鲍勃·斯托里。而那正是这个题目下最伟大的一部书；把英文系裁了、拿它顶上，都尽够。

===Original===

Beside Story, who else has had even a fair reading knowledge of any other literature﻿—Russian, Norwegian, German, French, Italian? Who knows enough about any one of these writers to look wise and nod; Renan, Turgeniev, Daudet, Björnson, Hauptman, Suderman, Strindberg? Do you know anything about Goethe as a critic, or the influence of Poe upon French literature? What do you know? I’ll tell you. You know *Les Misérables* and *The Three Musketeers* in French literature. You know Goethe wrote *Faust*. You’re beginning to know Ibsen as a name, and one may have read Tolstoy, and all know that he’s a very old man with a long white beard, who lives among his peasants, has some queer ideas, and has started to die three or four times. The papers have told you that.
“Take another field, of simple curiosity on what is doing in a world in which by opportunity you are supposed to be of the leading class. What do you know about the strength and spread of socialism in Germany, France and England? In the first place no one of you here probably has any idea of what socialism is; you’ve been told it’s anarchy, and, as that only means dynamite to you, you are against socialism, and will never take the trouble to investigate it. What do you know about the new political experiments in New Zealand?﻿—nothing. What do you know about the labor pension system in Germany, or the separation of the church and state in France?﻿—all subjects dealing with the vital development of the race of bipeds on this earth of which you happen to be members.
“Now here is a catch question﻿—all candidates for the dunce-cap will take a guess. The Botticelli story is such a chestnut now that you all know that it isn’t a cheese or a wine﻿—credit that to ridicule. I’m going to give you a few names from all the professions, and let’s see who can tag them. What was Spinoza, Holman Hunt, Dostoiefski, Ambrose Thomas, Savonarola (if you’ve read the novel you’d know that), Bastien Le Page, Zorn, Bizet, Bossuet! Unfair?﻿—not at all. These things are just as necessary to know to a man of education and culture as it is to a man of good manners to realize that peas are not introduced into the mouth by being balanced on a knife.”
“Help!” cried Hungerford, as Brockhurst went rushing on. “Great Scott, what *do* we know?”
“You know absolutely nothing,” said Brockhurst savagely.

===Chinese===

「斯托里之外，谁还能像样地读任何别国文学——俄国的、挪威的、德国的、法国的、意大利的？下列作家，谁认得到能点头装懂的份上：勒南、屠格涅夫、都德、比昂松、霍普特曼、苏德尔曼、斯特林堡？歌德作为批评家，知道一点吗？爱伦·坡对法国文学的影响，知道吗？你们知道什么？我来说。法国文学，你们知道《悲惨世界》（*Les Misérables*）和《三个火枪手》（*The Three Musketeers*）。你们知道歌德写了《浮士德》（*Faust*）。易卜生，你们刚开始知道这个名字；托尔斯泰，兴许有人读过，而人人都知道他是个留着长白胡子的很老的老头，住在他的农民中间，有些怪主意，已经死过三四回了。报纸告诉你们的。

「再换一片地——单凭好奇，看看这个世界在干什么；凭你们的机遇，你们本该是这世界的头等阶层。社会主义在德国、法国、英国的势力和铺展，你们知道多少？头一条，你们在座的恐怕没一个知道社会主义是什么；人家告诉你它是无政府主义，而既然那对你们只等于炸药，你们就反社会主义，永远不肯费神查一查。新西兰的新政治试验，你们知道什么？——零。德国的劳工养老金制度、法国的政教分离，你们知道什么？——全是关系到你们恰好身为成员的这个地球上两足种族生死攸关的发展的题目。

「现在来一道陷阱题——候补笨蛋帽的都来猜猜。波提切利那桩公案如今已成陈糠，你们总算知道它不是一种奶酪也不是一种酒——这得归功于嘲笑。我从各行当里报几个名字，看看谁能对上号。斯宾诺莎、霍尔曼·亨特、陀思妥耶夫斯基、昂布鲁瓦·托马、萨伏那洛拉（读过那本小说的就知道）、巴斯蒂安-勒帕热、佐恩、比才、博叙埃，各是干什么的？不公道？——一点也不。对一个受过教育、有教养的人，知道这些跟一个体面人明白豌豆不能架在刀尖上送进嘴里，一样是必需的。」

「救命啊！」布罗克赫斯特一路轰下去，亨格福德喊了起来。「老天爷，我们到底*知道*什么？」

「你们绝对地一无所知。」布罗克赫斯特凶狠地说。

===Original===

“Here you are; look at yourselves﻿—four years when you ought to learn something, some informing knowledge of all that has developed during the four thousand years the human race has fought its way toward the light, four years to be filled with the marvel and splendor of it all, and you don’t know a thing.
“You don’t know the big men in music; you don’t know the pioneers and the leaders in any art; you don’t know the great literatures of the world, and what they represent; you don’t know how other races are working out their social destinies; you’ve never even stopped to examine yourselves, to analyze your own society, to see the difference between a civilization founded on the unit of the individual, and a civilization, like the Latin, on the indestructible advance of the family. You have no general knowledge, no intellectual interests, you haven’t even opinions, and at the end of four years of *education* you will march up and be handed a degree﻿—Bachelor of Arts! Magnificent! And we Americans have a sense of humor! Do you wonder why I repeat that our colleges are splendidly organized institutions for the prevention of learning? No, sir, we are business colleges, and the business of our machines is to stamp out so many business men a year, running at full speed and in competition with the latest devices in Cambridge and Princeton!”
“Brocky, you are terrific,” said Swazey in admiration. There was too much truth in the attack, violent as it was, not to have called forth serious attention.
“I feel a good deal the way you do,” said Bob Story, and Stover nodded, “only it seems to me, Brocky, a good deal of what you’re arguing for must come from outside﻿—in just such informal talks as this.”
“That’s true,” said Brockhurst. “If the stimulus in the college life itself were toward education all our meetings would be educational. It’s true abroad, it isn’t here. You know my views. You think I’m extreme. I’m getting an education because I didn’t accept any such flap-doodle as, ‘What am I going to do for Yale?’ but instead asked, ‘What has Yale got to offer me?’ I’m getting it, too.”
Stover suddenly remembered the conversation they had together the year before, and looking now at Brockhurst, revealed in a new strength, he began to understand what had then so repelled him.
“The great fault,” continued Brockhurst, “lies, however, with the colleges.

===Chinese===

「你们就这样；看看自己——四年光阴，本该学点东西，学一点对人类四千年一路向光明厮杀出来的所有造化的通识，四年本该盛满这一切的奇迹与辉煌——而你们一无所知。

「音乐里的大人物，你们不知道；任何一门艺术的先驱与领袖，你们不知道；世界的伟大文学以及它们代表什么，你们不知道；别的族群怎样解开他们的社会命运，你们不知道；你们甚至从没停下来照一照自己，解剖解剖自己的社会，看一看奠基于个人这个单元的文明，同奠基于家族之不可摧的推进的文明——比如拉丁文明——差在哪里。你们没有通识，没有智识上的兴致，连见解都没有；四年*教育*念完，你们会上台领一个学位——文学士！辉煌吧！还亏我们美国人有幽默感！你们还奇怪我为什么反复说，我们的大学是组织得漂漂亮亮、专防学习的机构？不，先生，我们是商学院，我们这些机器的营生，就是全速开动、同坎布里奇和普林斯顿的最新设备竞赛，一年压出这么一批生意人！」

「老布，你真够呛的。」斯韦钦佩地说。这场轰炸再猛烈，其中的实情也太多，不由人不正色相听。

「我的感受同你差不多。」鲍勃·斯托里说，斯托弗也点头。「只是我觉得，老布，你主张的东西，相当一部分得从外头来——就在这样不拘形式的谈话里。」

「这话不假。」布罗克赫斯特说。「要是大学生活自身的刺激朝向教育，我们所有的聚会都会是教育性的。外国是这样，这儿不是。你们知道我的看法。你们当我极端。我能受上教育，是因为我没吞下『我能为耶鲁做什么』那路屁话，反倒问：『耶鲁有什么给我？』我也真受上了。」

斯托弗忽然记起他们前年的那场谈话，此刻看着布罗克赫斯特——在一种新的力量里显形的布罗克赫斯特——他开始懂得当初那么拒斥他的是什么了。

「不过，大错，」布罗克赫斯特接着说，「还在大学身上。

===Original===

The whole theory is wrong, archaic and ridiculous﻿—the theory of education by schedule. All education can do is to instil the love of knowledge. You get that, you catch the fire of it﻿—you educate yourself. All education does today is to develop the memory at the expense of the imagination. It says: ‘Here are so many pounds of Greek, Latin, mathematics, history, literature. In four years our problem is to pass them through the heads of these hundreds of young barbarians so that they will come out with a lip knowledge.’ ”
“But come, we do learn something,” said Hungerford.
“No, you don’t, Joe,” said Brockhurst. “You’ve translated the *Iliad*﻿—you’ve never known it. You’ve recited in Horace﻿—you have no love for him. You’ve excavated the plays of Shakespeare, a couple of acts at a time; you don’t know what *Hamlet* means or *Lear*, the beauty of it all has escaped you. You’ve *recited* in Logic and Philosophy, but you don’t understand what you’re repeating. You’re only *repeating* all the time. Your memory is trained to hold a little knowledge a little time﻿—that’s all. You don’t enjoy it, you’re rather apologetic﻿—or others are.”
“Well, what other system is there?” said Regan.
“There is the preceptorial system of England,” said Brockhurst, “where a small group of men are in personal contact with the instructor. In French universities, education is a serious thing because failure to pass an examination for a profession means two extra years of army service. Men don’t risk over there, or divide up their time heeling the *News* or making a team. In Germany a man is given a certain number of years to get a degree, and I believe has to do a certain amount of original work.
“But of course the main trouble here is, and there is no blinking the fact, that the colleges have surrendered unconsciously a great deal of their power to the growing influence of the social organization. In a period when we have no society in America, families are sending their sons to colleges to place themselves socially. Some of them carry it to an extreme, even directly avow their hope that they will make certain clubs at Princeton or Harvard, or a senior society here. It probably is very hard to control, but it’s going to turn our colleges more and more, as I say, into social clearing houses.

===Chinese===

「整套理论都错了，陈腐而且可笑——按课表办教育的理论。教育能做的全部，就是灌进对知识的热爱。你得了这个，接住了它的火——你就自己教育了自己。如今的教育干的全部，是开发记忆力、牺牲想象力。它说：『这儿是几磅希腊文、几磅拉丁文、数学、历史、文学。四年间，我们的课题是把它们灌过这几百颗小野蛮人的脑袋，让他们出来时嘴皮子上有一层知识。』」

「得了，我们总学到点什么吧。」亨格福德说。

「不，乔，你们没有。」布罗克赫斯特说。《伊利亚特》（*Iliad*）你们翻译过——从没认识过它。贺拉斯你们背诵过——对他没有一点爱。莎士比亚的戏你们发掘过，一回两幕；《哈姆雷特》（*Hamlet*）讲什么、《李尔王》（*Lear*）讲什么，你们不知道，那全部的美从你们身边溜了。逻辑和哲学你们*背诵*过，可你们不懂自己在复述什么。你们从头到尾只在*复述*。你们的记忆力被训练成装一点点知识、装一小会儿——就这么些。你们不以此为乐，你们倒有点道歉的意思——或者是旁人替你们道歉。」

「那么，还有什么别的制度？」里根说。

「有英国的导师制，」布罗克赫斯特说，「一小群人同导师当面相处。在法国的大学，教育是桩严肃的事，因为一门行业的考试不过关，就意味着多服两年兵役。那边的人不冒这个险，也不会把时间切碎了去给《新闻》跑腿或者进什么队。在德国，一个人拿学位有年限，而且我记得还非做一份原创的工作不可。

「当然，这儿的大病——这一点没法眨眼睛躲开——在于大学已经不自觉地把许多权柄，交了出去，交给日益坐大的社交组织的势力。在一个美国根本没有上流社会的时代，家家把儿子送进大学，去谋一个社交的位置。有的把这做到了头，甚至直说指望孩子进普林斯顿或哈佛的某某俱乐部，或者这儿的某个毕业班会社。这大概很难管；可它会像我说的一样，把我们的大学越来越多地变成社交清算所。

===Original===

At present here at Yale we keep down the question of wealth pretty well; fellows like Joe Hungerford here come in and live on our basis. That’s the best feature about Yale today﻿—how it will be in the future I don’t know, for it depends on the wisdom of the parents.”
“Social clearing house is well coined,” said Hungerford. “I think it’s truer though of Harvard.”
“That’s perhaps because you see the mote in your neighbors’ eyes,” said Brocky rising. “Well, discussion isn’t going to change it. Who’s always talking about school for character﻿—Pike or Brown? We might as well stand for that﻿—but it would not be very wise to announce it to the American nation, would it?﻿—we might be dubbed a reformatory. Fathers, send your sons to college﻿—reform their characters, straighten out the crooks. At the end give ’em a degree of﻿—of, say﻿—G.B.”
“What’s that, Brocky?” said Swazey, grinning with the rest.
“Good Boy,” said Brockhurst, who departed, as he liked, on the echoes of the laugh which he had inspired.
“Whew!” said Hungerford, with a comical rubbing of his head. “What struck me?”
“And I expect to make Phi Beta Kappa,” said Swazey, with an apologetic laugh.
“What a dreadfully disconcerting person,” said Bob Story.
“By George, it takes the conceit out of you,” said Stover ruthfully. “Shall we all start in and learn something? What’s the answer?”
At this moment a familiar slogan was heard below, increasing in riotous, pagan violence with the approach of boisterous feet.

> “Oh, father and mother

Pay all the bills,

And we have all the fun.

Hooray!

That’s the way we do in college life﻿—

In college life.”

The room burst into a roar of laughter.
“There’s one answer,” said Regan rising.
The door slammed open, and McNab and Buck Waters reeled in arm in arm.
“I say, fellows, we’ve cornered the sleigh market,” said Dopey uproariously. “We’re all going to beat it to the Cheshire Inn, a bottle of champagne to the first to arrive. Are you on?”
Half an hour later, Stover at the reins was whirling madly along the crusty roads, in imminent danger of collision with three other rollicking parties, who packed the sleighs and cheered on the galloping horses, singing joyfully the battle hymn of the pagans:

> “Oh, father and mother

Pay all the bills,

And we have all the fun.

Hooray!

That’s the way we do

In college life.”

===Chinese===

「眼下在耶鲁，财富这道题压得还算好；像这儿的乔·亨格福德这样的子弟进来，照我们的水准过日子。这是今日耶鲁最好的一面——将来如何，我不知道，那要看做父母的明智不明智。」

「社交清算所，这个词铸得好。」亨格福德说。「不过我看用在哈佛身上更贴。」

「那兴许是因为你只看得见邻人眼里的刺。」老布起身说。「好了，讨论改变不了它。『品格学校』这套话是谁成天挂嘴边的——派克还是布朗？我们干脆认下这个——可向美国全国宣布就不太明智了，是不是？——说不定要被叫成感化院。父亲们，把儿子送来大学吧——改造品格，把歪的掰直。毕业时发他们一个——比方说——G.B.学位。」

「G.B.是什么，老布？」斯韦齐同大伙儿一起咧嘴。

「乖孩子（Good Boy）。」布罗克赫斯特说。他就喜欢这样，踩着自己逗出的那阵笑声的余音，扬长而去。

「嚯！」亨格福德滑稽地揉着脑袋。「刚才是什么东西砸中我了？」

「我还指望评上斐贝卡学会（Phi Beta Kappa）呢。」斯韦齐抱歉地笑了笑。

「一个多么叫人下不来台的人。」鲍勃·斯托里说。

「天哪，这一套把人的自满全卸了。」斯托弗怅然说。「咱们是不是都从头学起点什么？答案在哪里？」

正这时，楼下一句熟悉的口号响起来，随着一阵喧腾的脚步逼近，越闹越凶，异教得没法办：

> 「哦，爹地和妈咪

帐单统统他们付，

快活全归我们自己。

好哇！

大学日子就这么过——

大学日子。」

满屋轰然大笑。

「这就是一个答案。」里根起身说。

门砰地撞开，麦克纳布和巴克·沃特斯互相架着，摇摇晃晃进来。

「我说弟兄们，咱们把雪橇市场包圆了。」多皮大吵大嚷。「大伙儿全速杀去柴郡客栈，头一个到的赏一瓶香槟。来不来？」

半小时后，斯托弗执缰，顺着冻硬的道路狂转，同另外三拨闹哄哄的人马险险相撞——雪橇塞满了人，朝飞奔的马吆喝，欢天喜地唱着异教徒的战歌：

> 「哦，爹地和妈咪

帐单统统他们付，

快活全归我们自己。

好哇！

大学日子就这么过——

大学日子。」

## XXIV / 二十四
===Original===

Once Stover had reconciled himself to the loss of a senior society election, he found ample compensation in the absolute liberty of action that came to him. It was not that he condemned this parent system; he believed in it as an honest attempt to reward the best in the college life, a sort of academic legion of honor, formed not on social cleavage, but given as a reward of merit. In his own case, he believed his own personal offending in the matter of Le Baron and Reynolds had been so extreme that nothing could counteract it.
So he gave himself up to the free and untrammelled delights of living his own life. His fierce stand for absolute democracy made of his rooms the anteroom of the class, through which all crowds seemed to pass, men of his own kind, socially calculating, glad to be known as the friends of Regan, Hungerford and Story, all rated sure men, and Stover, about whom they began to wonder more and more, as a unique and rebellious personality, which, contrary to precedent, had come to bear down all opposition. Gimbel and Hicks, elected managers for the coming year, came often, willing to conciliate the element they had fought, in the hopes of a favorable outcome on Tap Day. Men who worked their way dropped in often on Regan; Ricketts, with his drawling Yankee astuteness, always laughing up his sleeve; twenty odd, lonely characters, glad to sink into a quiet corner and listen to the furious discussions that raged about Brockhurst, Story and Regan.
It was seldom that Stover talked. He learned more by listening, by careful weighing of others’ opinions, than in the attempt to classify his own thoughts through the medium of debate. At times when the discussion wandered from vital sources, he would ask a question, and these sharp, direct remarks had a pertinency and a searching trenchancy that sometimes upset an elaborate argument.
Regan brought him to the romance of commonplace things, to a genuine interest and study of political conditions; Brockhurst irritated and dissatisfied him, and so stimulated him to reading and self-analysis; Story, with his seriousness and fairness, recalled him always to a judicial point of view and an understanding of others; Hungerford, with his big, effusive nature, always dissatisfied and eager for realities, was akin to his own nature, and they grew into a confidential intimacy.

===Chinese===

斯托弗既已同四年级会社选举的落选讲和，便在随之而来的、行动上的绝对自由里找到了十足的补偿。倒不是他谴责这套母制度；他信它——信它是奖励大学生活中最优者的一次诚实的尝试，一种学院的荣誉军团，不按社交的断层结成，而是作为功绩的犒赏颁下。至于他自己，他相信，勒巴龙和雷诺兹那一节上他冒犯得太狠，什么都扳不回来了。
于是他放开了手脚，过自己的日子，享那份无拘无束的快活。他那副为绝对民主死挺的立场，把他的屋子变成了全班的前厅：各路人马仿佛都从这里过——他自己那一类的，工于社交盘算的，乐得被认作里甘、亨格福德和斯托里的朋友——三位都是铁定的入选者——还有斯托弗：大家对他越来越好奇，一个独一份的、造反的性子，破了一切先例，硬是把所有反对都压了下去。金贝尔和希克斯（Hicks），当选了来年的经理，也常来——他们愿意同斗过的那一一路和解，指望叩选日有个好结果。半工半读的人常来找里甘；里基茨带着他那副拖腔的北方佬精明，永远在袖子里笑；还有二十来个离群独居的角色，乐得沉进一个安静的角落，听布罗克赫斯特、斯托里和里甘周围烧得旺的那些激烈的讨论。
斯托弗难得开口。他靠听、靠细掂别人的意见学来的，比靠辩论来归置自己的思想多。有时讨论漂离了要害，他会问一句；这些尖而直的话，又对题又刨根，有时把一套精工的论证掀翻。
里甘把他领进平凡事物的传奇，领进对政治状况的真兴趣和真研究；布罗克赫斯特刺他、叫他不满，也就逼他读书、自己解剖自己；斯托里凭他的严肃与公道，总把他唤回一个法官的眼光和对他人的体谅；亨格福德，那副大而热烈的天性、永远不满足、永远要真的，同他自己的天性相通——两人处成了交心的密友。

===Original===

In a community of splendid barbarians, their circle was exceptional, due to the pronounced individuality of their several rebellious minds.
Despite the abolition of the sophomore societies, other groups still maintained their exclusiveness, and kept alive the old antagonism, as the approach of Tap Day intensified the struggle for election and the natural campaigning of friend for friend.
As Brockhurst had prophesied, the chairmanship of the *Lit* Board went to Wiggin, a conscientious, thorough little plodder, who had never failed to hand in to each number his numerically correct quota of essays, two stories, a hammered-out poem and two painful portfolios.
On the night of the election, Stover heard from his room in Lyceum the familiar:
“Oh, you Dink Stover﻿—stick out your head.”
“Hello there, Brocky; come up,” he said anxiously. “Who got it?”
“Wiggin, of course. Come on down, I want a ramble.”
It was the first time that Brockhurst had shown a longing for companionship. Stover returned into the room, announcing:
“Poor old chap. Wiggin got it. Isn’t it the devil?”
“Wiggin﻿—oh, Lord!” said Regan.
“Why, he’s not fit to tie Brocky’s shoestrings,” said Hungerford, who fired a volley of soul-relieving oaths.
“I’m going down to bum around a bit with him,” said Stover, slipping on his coat, “cheer the old boy up.”
“Well, he knew it.”
“Lots of difference that makes!”
Below Brocky, muffled to the ears, brim down, was whistling in unmusical enthusiasm.

> “ ’Tis a jolly life we lead,

Care and sorrow we defy﻿—”

===Chinese===

在这一族堂堂的蛮人中间，他们这个小圈子是例外，因为几个造反的头脑各自分明的个性。
二年级会社虽废了，别的团伙照旧守着排他，旧日的敌意照旧活着——叩选日一近，选举的争斗和朋友替朋友的造势，都随着涨起来。
正如布罗克赫斯特预言的，《文学》董事会主席归了威金（Wiggin）——一个尽职尽责、埋头苦干的小爬虫，每一期从不缺席地交上他数目分毫不差的份额：一篇论文、两个短篇、一首敲打出来的诗、两册蹙着眉的画夹。
选举那夜，斯托弗在吕克昂的屋里听见那声熟惯的：
「喂，丁克·斯托弗——伸出头来。」
「喂，布罗基；上来，」他急急地说。「谁得了？」
「当然是威金。下来，我要走走。」
布罗克赫斯特头一回露出了要人陪的意思。斯托弗回屋宣布：
「可怜的老兄。威金得了。岂有此理吧？」
「威金——哦，老天！」里甘说。
「嗐，他给布罗基系鞋带都不配，」亨格福德说，放出一串宽胸解气的咒骂。
「我下去陪他荡荡，」斯托弗边套外衣边说，「给老家伙顺顺气。」
「嗐，他早知道。」
「知道顶什么用！」
楼下，布罗基裹到耳朵根，帽檐压下，正用不成调的热乎劲吹着口哨：

> 「我们过的日子多快活，

> 忧愁烦恼全不怕——」

===Original===

“Hello, that you, Dink?” he said, breaking off. “Come on for a tramp.”
At that age, being inexperienced, the undergraduate in questions of sympathy wisely returns to the instincts of the canine. Stover, without speaking, fell into his stride, and they swung off towards West Rock.
“Wiggin is the type of man,” said Brockhurst, meditatively puffing his pipe, “that is the glorification of the commonplace. He is a sort of sublime earthworm, plodding along and claiming acquaintance with the rose because he travels around the roots. He is really by instinct a bricklayer, and the danger is that he may continue either in literature or some profession where the cry is for imagination.”
“You could have beaten him out,” said Stover, as a solace.
“And become an earthworm?” said Brockhurst. “The luck of it is, he made up his mind to heel the *Lit*. With his ideas he would have made leader of the glee club, president of the Phi Beta Kappa, chairman of the *News*, or whatnot.”
“Still, give him credit,” said Stover, smiling to himself, for he felt that he saw for the first time the human side of Brockhurst.
“I did; it was quite an amusing time.”
“What happened?”
“Why, the little grubber came up to me and said, ‘Brocky, old man, you ought to have had it.’ ”
“Why, that was rather decent,” said Stover.
“Rubbish. All form,” said Brockhurst impatiently. “Showed the calibre of his mind﻿—the obvious; nothing but the obvious. He thought it the thing to say, that’s all.”
“Well, what did you answer?” said Stover wondering.
“I said, ‘Well, why didn’t you vote for me then?’ ”
Stover burst out laughing, and Brockhurst, who had lost a coveted honor, was a little mollified by the tribute.
“Of course he stammered and looked annoyed﻿—naturally; situation his imagination couldn’t meet, so I said:
“ ‘Come, Wiggin, no stuff and nonsense. You didn’t think I ought to have it, and I know damn well, now that you’ve won out, you’ll get a Skull and Bones to wear, pose in the middle of the photograph for the Banner, and be thoroughly satisfied at our board meeting to sit back and listen while I do the talking.’ ”
Stover broke into a laugh.
“Brocky, you scandalized him.”
“Not at all. He thought I was joking﻿—the last thing that occurs to the grubber is that wit is only a polite way of calling a man an ass.”
“Brocky, you’re at your best, don’t stop.”
Brockhurst smiled.

===Chinese===

「喂，是你吗，丁克？」他停了哨说。「走，蹓跶去。」
在那个年纪，本科生还没有经验，在同情这门功课上，明智的做法是回到犬类的本能。斯托弗不说话，合上他的步子，两人朝西岩荡去。
「威金这一型的人，」布罗克赫斯特若有所思地喷着烟斗说，「是平庸的加冕礼。他是那种崇高的蚯蚓，一路爬，因为围着玫瑰的根转，就认作同玫瑰相识。他骨子里本是个砌砖匠；危险在于，他会一路干下去——干文学，或干随便哪一门喊要想象力的行当。」
「你本可以赢他，」斯托弗说，算一句安慰。
「然后也变一条蚯蚓？」布罗克赫斯特说。「运气在于：他打定了主意给《文学》跑腿。凭他那套想法，他本可以当合唱团领唱、φBK 会长、《新闻》主席，随便什么。」
「不过，得给他一句公道，」斯托弗说，自己暗笑——他觉得自己头一回看见了布罗克赫斯特的人的那一面。
「我给了；那场面相当好玩。」
「怎么了？」
「嗐，那小爬虫走到我跟前说：『布罗基，老兄，这本该是你的。』」
「嗐，这还算像话，」斯托弗说。
「废话。全是套话，」布罗克赫斯特不耐烦地说。「显出他脑子的斤两——只会说现成的；除了现成的什么都没有。他觉得这句话该说，如此而已。」
「那你怎么回的？」斯托弗纳帘道。
「我说：『哦，那你当时怎么不投我？』」
斯托弗放声大笑，布罗克赫斯特——丢了一项眼热的荣誉——叫这一份敬意稍稍顺了气。
「他自然结巴了、挂相了——当然；这个场面他的想象力应付不了。于是我说：
「『得了，威金，别装模作样。你并不觉得该我得；我也清清楚楚知道，如今你赢了，你会戴上骷髅会的徽章，在《班史》的合影里端坐正中，在我们的编务会上心满意足地往后一靠，听我一个人说话。』」
斯托弗又笑出声。
「布罗基，你把他羞了个透。」
「哪里。他当我说笑呢——爬虫们最想不到的一件事就是：妙语不过是一种客气的骂人蠢。」
「布罗基，你今晚状态最好，别停。」
布罗克赫斯特笑了。

===Original===

It was turning a defeat into a victory. He continued:
“After all, Wiggy is interesting. I’ll be revenged. I’ll put him in a book some day. He represents a type﻿—the mathematical mind, quantity not quality. He set out for the chairmanship as a man trains for a long-distance run. Do you know the truth? He rose every morning and took a cold shower, fifty swings to the left with the dumbbell, fifty to the right, ate nothing heavy or starchy for his meals, walked the same distance each afternoon, and worked his two hours each night, hammering out divine literature.”
“Oh, I say!” said Dink, a little in doubt.
Brockhurst began to laugh.
“He may have for all I know. Now I’ll bury him. He will be eminently successful﻿—I like that word eminently. You see he has no sense of humor, and especially no imagination to hinder him.” Brockhurst, in one of his quixotic moods, began to gesture to the stars as he abandoned himself to the delights of his conceit. “Oh, that’s a wonderful thing, to have no imagination﻿—the saving of commonplace minds. If Wiggin had an imagination he would never have written a line, he would have perceived the immense distances that separated him from the Olympians. Instead he read Stevenson, Dumas, Kipling, and, unafraid, wrote little Stevenson echoes of Dumas, capsule Kiplings. He’ll go out in the world, nothing will frighten him. He will rebel against nothing, for he hasn’t an idea. He will choose the woman he needs for his needs, persuade himself that he’s in love, and then persuade her. And he’ll believe that’s a virtuous marriage. He’ll belong to the conservative party, the conservative church, and will be a distinguished subordinate, who will stand for tradition, institutions, and will be said to resemble some great man. Then he’ll die, and will be pointed to as a great example. Requiescat in pace.”
“Off with his head,” said Stover appreciatively. “Now he’s finished, own up, Brocky, that you are furious that you did not buckle down and beat him out.”
“Of course I am﻿—damn it,” said Brockhurst. “I know I did right, but no one else will ever know it. And the strange thing is, Dink, the best thing for me is to have missed out.”
“Why, in Heaven’s name?”
“If I had made the chairmanship, I should probably be tapped for Bones﻿—one of the successful. I might have become satisfied.

===Chinese===

这是把一场败仗翻成胜仗。他接着说：
「说到底，威基这人挺有意思。我要报仇。哪天我把他写进一本书。他代表一个型——数学的头脑，有数量没有质量。他奔主席之位，像一个人练长跑。你知道实情吗？他每天早起冲一个冷水澡，哑铃左边五十下、右边五十下，饭桌上不沾厚重不沾淀粉，每天下午走同一段路，每晚干满两个钟头，敲出神圣的文学。」
「哦，我说！」丁克说，有点吃不准。
布罗克赫斯特笑起来。
「我知道的那点全说了。现在埋了他。他将出人头地地成功——我喜欢『出人头地』这个词。你看，他没有幽默感，尤其没有想象力碍事。」布罗克赫斯特一阵堂吉诃德的兴头，放开他那一套妙语的快乐，朝星星打起手势。「哦，没有想象力是件妙极的东西——平庸头脑的救星。威金要有想象力，一个字也写不出来：他就会看见他自己同奥林匹斯诸神之间隔着的天大的距离。可他呢，读史蒂文森、大仲马、吉卜林，毫不害怕地写他的小史蒂文森——大仲马味的，胶囊装的吉卜林。他将来走进世界，什么也吓不着他。他什么也不会反抗，因为他一个主意也没有。他会挑一个合他需要的女人，先说服自己恋爱了，再说服她。他还会信这是一桩有德的婚姻。他会属于保守党、保守的教会，做一个出色的下属，站在传统和建制那一边，人家还会说他像某位大人物。然后他会死掉，被人指着说：伟大的榜样。愿他安息（Requiescat in pace）。」
「砍掉他的头，」斯托弗激赏道。「现在他完了。老实说，布罗基，你没憋下劲赢了他，气不气？」
「当然气——气死了，」布罗克赫斯特说。「我知道我做对了，可再没有别人会知道。而且怪就怪在，丁克，错过了这一票，对我反而是最好的事。」
「天哪，为什么？」
「我要是当上了主席，多半就叩进骷髅会了——成功者的一员。我可能就满足了。

===Original===

Do you know that that is the great danger of this whole senior business?”
“What?”
“The fellow who wears his honors like a halo. He’s made Bones or Keys, he’s a success in life. Nothing more awaits him. ‘I was it.’ ”
“Still, you would have liked it.”
“Sure; I’m inconsistent,” said Brocky, with a laugh. “It’s only when I don’t get what I want that my beautiful reason shows me I shouldn’t have had it.”
“Well, there’s no danger of either of us disappearing under the halos,” said Stover shortly.
“I’m not so sure about you,” said Brockhurst.
The casual doubt aroused strange emotions in Stover.
“I thought you didn’t believe in them,” he said slowly.
“I don’t. I don’t believe in organizations, institutions, traditions﻿—that’s my point of view,” said Brockhurst. “But then I’m in the world to be in revolt.”
“You once spoke of the society system﻿—the whole thing as it exists in America﻿—” said Stover, “as a sort of idol worship. I never quite understood your meaning.”
“Why, I think it’s quite obvious,” said Brockhurst surprised. “What was idol worship? A large body of privileged charlatans, calling themselves priests, impressed the masses with all the flummery of mysterious ceremonies, convenient voices issuing from caves or stone idols. What was an idol? An ordinary chunk of marble, let us say, issuing from the sculptor’s chisel. When did it become sacred and awe-inspiring? When it had been placed in an inner shrine of shrines, removed from the public, veiled in shadows, obscured by incense, guarded by solicitous guards; the stone is still a stone but the populace is convinced. Look into a well in daylight﻿—commonplace; look into it at night﻿—a great mystery; black is never empty, the imagination fills it.”
“How does this apply?” said Stover, impatiently.
“Cases are parallel. A group of us come together for the purpose of debate and discussion; no one notices it beyond a casual thought. Suddenly we surround ourselves with mystery, appear on the campus with a sensational pin stuck in our cravats, a bat’s head or a gallows, and when, marvellously enough, someone asks us what the dickens we are wearing, we turn away; instantly it becomes known that something so deadly secret has begun that we have sworn to shed our heart’s blood before we allow the holy, sacred name of Bat’s Head or Gallow’s Bird to pass our lips!”
“It’s a little foolish, but what’s the harm?”

===Chinese===

你知道不知道，这正是这整套四年级把戏的大危险？」
「什么？」
「那种把荣誉当光环戴的家伙。他进了骷髅会或钥匙会，他一生就成功了。再没有别的等他。『我就是那个。』」
「可你还是会喜欢它的。」
「当然；我言行不一，」布罗基笑着说。只有得不到想要的东西的时候，我那套漂亮的道理才向我证明我本不该要它。」
「好，反正咱们俩谁也没有被光环吞掉的危险，」斯托弗简短地说。
「你嘛，我拿不准，」布罗克赫斯特说。
这随口的一疑，在斯托弗心里激起奇异的波动。
「我还当你不信它们，」他慢慢地说。
「我不信。我不信组织、建制、传统——这是我的立场，」布罗克赫斯特说。「可我活在世上，就是为了造反。」
「你从前说过，会社体系——美国现存的这一整套——」斯托弗说，「是一种偶像崇拜。你的意思我一直没大懂。」
「嗐，我以为明摆着呢，」布罗克赫斯特说，奇怪了。「偶像崇拜是什么？一大帮有特权的骗子，自称祭司，拿神秘仪式的种种排场、从山洞或石头偶像里发出的便利的声音，镇住大众。偶像是什么？一块普通的石头蛋子，出在雕塑家的凿子底下。它什么时候变得神圣、可畏了？在它被请进至圣的圣所、同公众隔开、裹进阴影、遮进香烟、加上殷勤的卫士之后——石头还是那块石头，可大众信了。白天往井里看——平平常常；夜里往井里看——天大的神秘；黑色从来不是空的，想象力把它填满。」
「这怎么对上号？」斯托弗不耐烦地说。
「案情平行。咱们一伙人为辩论和讨论聚到一起；谁也不多想一眼。忽然，咱们给自己围上神秘，胸前别一枚耸动的徽章出现在校园里——一颗蝙蝠头或一副绞架——等哪个人偏偏问了句『你们戴的是什么鬼东西』，咱们把脸一转；立刻人人皆知：一件要命的秘密大事开始了，我们起过誓——宁可抛头颅洒热血，也不让『蝙蝠头』或『绞架鸟』这神圣的名字过我们的嘴唇！」
「是有点傻，可有什么害处？」

===Original===

“The harm is that this mumbo-jumbo, fee-fi-fo-fum, high cockalorum business is taken seriously. It’s the effect on the young imagination that comes here that is harmful. Dink, I tell you, and I mean it solemnly, that when a boy comes here to Yale, or any other American college, and gets the flummery in his system, believes in it﻿—surrenders to it﻿—so that he trembles in the shadow of a tomblike building, doesn’t dare to look at a pin that stares him in the face, is afraid to pronounce the holy, sacred names; when he’s got to that point he has ceased to *think*, and no amount of college life is going to revive him. That’s the worst thing about it all, this mental subjection which the average man undergoes here when he comes up against all this rigmarole of Tap Day, gloomy society halls, marching home at night, et cetera﻿—et ceteray. By George, it *is* a return of the old idol-worship idea﻿—thinking men in this twentieth century being impressed by the same methods that kept nations in servitude to charlatans three thousand years before. It’s wrong, fundamentally wrong﻿—it’s a crime against the whole moving spirit of university history﻿—the history of a struggle for the liberation of the human mind.”
“But, Brocky, what would you have them do﻿—run as open clubs?”
“Not at all,” said Brockhurst. “I would strip them of all nonsense; in fact that is their weakness, not their strength, and it is all unnecessary. This is what I’d do: drop the secrecy﻿—this extraordinary muffled breathless guarding of an empty can﻿—retain the privilege any club has of excluding outsiders, stop this childishness of getting up and leaving the room if some old lady happens to ask are you a Bones man or a Keys man. Instead, when a Bones man goes to see a freshman whom he wants to befriend, have him say openly as he passes the chapter house:
“ ‘That’s my society﻿—Skull and Bones. It stands as a reward of merit here. Hope you’ll do something to deserve it.’
“Which is the better of the two ideas, the saner, the manlier and the more natural? What would they lose by eliminating the objectionable, unnecessary features﻿—all of which you may be sure were started as horse play, and have curiously enough come to be taken in deadly earnestness?”
“I think you exaggerate a little,” said Stover, unwilling to accept this arraignment.
“No, I don’t,” said Brockhurst stubbornly.

===Chinese===

「害处是：这套巫术神坛、噼里嘣隆、高跷大老爷的把戏，被人当了真。受害的是来到这里的年轻想象力。丁克，我告诉你，我说得郑重：一个少年上耶鲁来，或上随便哪所美国大学来，把这套排场吸进血里，信了它——降了它——在一座坟似的楼影里发抖，不敢看一枚蹬着他脸的徽章，不敢念那些神圣的名字；到了这一步，他就不再*思想*了，多少大学生活也救不活他。这整套东西里最坏的就是这个：普通人在这儿撞上叩选日这一套缛节、阴沉沉的会社堂、夜里列队归舍、等等——等等——所受的精神降服。天哪，这*就是*古老的偶像崇拜的还魂——二十世纪的思想者，被三千年前把万国锁在骗子脚下的那同一套手法镇住。错了，从根上错了——这是对大学历史的全部运动精神的犯罪——那历史是一场为解放人心而斗的历史。」
「可是布罗基，你要他们怎么办——办成公开的俱乐部？」
「根本不必，」布罗克赫斯特说。「我要剥掉它一切的胡闹；其实胡闹正是它们的软处，不是强处，而且全无必要。我这么办：把秘密丢掉——这种守着一只空罐头的、鬼鬼祟祟屏住气的非凡戒备——保留任何俱乐部都有的拒外特权，戒掉这种小孩子脾气：哪位老太太碰巧问一句你是骷髅会的还是钥匙会的，就起身离屋。反过来，一位骷髅会员去看他想照拂的一个新生，让他路过会社堂时公开地说：
「『那是我的会社——骷髅会。在这儿它是功绩的犒赏。希望你做点配得上它的事。』
「两个主意哪一个好？哪个健全、哪个有男子气、哪个自然？去掉这些招人嫌又多余的东西——你尽可相信，这些东西起初全是胡闹起的头，怪就怪在后来被当成了天大的正经——他们损失什么？」
「我看你夸张了一点，」斯托弗说，不肯接这份诉状。
「我没有，」布罗克赫斯特执拗地说。

===Original===

“The thing is a fetish; it gets you; it’s meant to get you. It gets me, and if you’re honest you’ll admit it gets you. Now own up.”
“Yes, I suppose it does.”
“Now, Dink, you’re fighting for one thing up here, the freedom of your mind and your will.”
“Why, yes,” Stover said, surprised at Brockhurst’s knowledge of his inner conflicts. “Yes, that’s exactly what I’m fighting out.”
“Well, my boy, you’ll never get what you’re after until you see this thing as it is﻿—the unreasoning harm done, the poppycock that has been thrown around a good central idea﻿—if you admit such things are necessary, which of course I don’t.”
“You see,” said Stover stubbornly, “you’re against all organization.”
“I certainly am﻿—inherited organizations,” said Brockhurst immediately, “organizations that are imposed on you. The only organization necessary is the natural, spontaneous coming together of congenial elements.”
They had returned to the campus, and Brockhurst, by intent leading the way, stopped before the lugubrious bulk of Skull and Bones.
“There you are,” he said, with a laugh. “Look at it. It’s built of the same stone as other buildings, it has in it what secret? Go up, young Egyptian, to its mystery in awe and reverence, young idol worshiper of thirty centuries ago.”
“Damn it, Brocky, it does get me,” said Stover with a short laugh.
“Curious,” said Brockhurst, turning away. “The architecture of these sacred tombs is almost invariably the suggestion of the dungeon﻿—the prison of the human mind.”
---
Stover’s conversation with Brockhurst did not at first trouble him much. Curiously enough the one idea he retained was that Brockhurst had spoken of him as a possibility for Tap Day.
“What nonsense,” he said to himself angrily. “Here, I know better!”
But the next afternoon, the thought returning to him with pleasure, all at once, following a boyish whim, he passed into his old entry at Lawrence, and, going down a little guiltily into the region of the bathtubs, came to the wall on which was inscribed the lists of his class.
On the Bones list, third from the top, the name Stover had been replaced and heavily underlined.
It gave him quite a thrill; something seemed to leap up inside of him, and he went out hastily. Then all at once he became angry. It was like opening up again a fight that had been fought and lost.
“What an ass I am,” he said furiously.

===Chinese===

「这东西是个物神；它抓你；它存心抓你。它抓我；你要是诚实，就承认它也抓你。老实说。」
「是，我认。」
「那么，丁克，你在这儿斗的是一样东西：你头脑和意志的自由。」
「嗐，是，」斯托弗说，惊异于布罗克赫斯特对他内心的战事知道得这样清。「是，我斗的正是这个。」
「好，我的孩子，你把这件事看成它的本来面目之前——看清它那不由分说的害处、泼在一个不坏的主意四周的胡话——你得不到你追的东西；如果你承认这类东西必要的话；当然我不承认。」
「你看，」斯托弗执拗地说，「你反对一切组织。」
「我当然反对——反对世袭的组织，」布罗克赫斯特立刻说。「强加给你的组织。唯一必要的组织，是气味相投的成分自然而然的聚拢。」
两人已走回校园，布罗克赫斯特有心领路，在骷髅会那团阴郁的庞然大物前站住。
「你瞧，」他笑着说。「看它。同别的楼一块石头砌的，里头藏着什么秘密？上去吧，年轻的埃及人，怀着敬畏去朝它的神秘，三十个世纪前的年轻偶像崇拜者。」
「见鬼，布罗基，它真抓我，」斯托弗短短一笑说。
「有意思，」布罗克赫斯特转身说。「这些圣陵的建筑，几乎永远带着地牢的意味——人心的监狱。」
---
同布罗克赫斯特的这场谈话，起初没怎么搅扰他。说来奇怪，他留下来的唯一一个念头，是布罗克赫斯特把他当作叩选日的一个可能。
「胡扯，」他愤愤地自语。「得了，我有数！」
可第二天下午，这个念头带着愉悦回来，他忽然童心一动，走进劳伦斯楼自己旧日的门洞，带着一点负疚下到澡堂那片地界，来到刻着他那一届名单的墙前。
骷髅会的名单上，从顶上数第三行，斯托弗（Stover）这个名字换了上去，还重重画了线。
他心里着实一颤；有什么东西似乎在里头一跃而起，他匆匆走了出去。随即他忽然恼了。这像把一场打完、输掉的仗重新撕开。
「我多蠢哪，」他狂怒地说。

===Original===

“The deuce of a chance I have to go Bones﻿—with Reynolds and Le Baron. Can the leopard change his spots? About as much chance as a ki-yi has to go through a sausage machine and come out with a bark.”
But, as he went towards Jean Story’s home, thinking of her and what she would want, the force of what Brockhurst had said began to weaken.
“Brocky is impractical,” he said artfully. “We must deal with things as they are, make the best of them. He exaggerates the effect on the imagination. At any rate, no one can accuse me of not taking a stand.”
He saw the old colonial home, white and distinguished under the elms, and he said to himself, hoping against hope:
“If I were tapped﻿—it would mean a good deal to her. I’ll be darned if I’ll let Brocky work me up. I’m not going up against anything more! I’ve done enough here.”
He said it defiantly, for the courage of a man has two factors, his courage and the courage of the woman he loves.

===Chinese===

「我进骷髅会的机会——同雷诺兹同勒巴龙在一个屋里。花豹改得了斑吗？同把一条杂毛狗塞进灌肠机、指望它出来还会叫，差不多的机会。」
可是，往琼·斯托里家走的路上，想着她、想着她会要什么，布罗克赫斯特那番话的力道开始退了。
「布罗基不切实际，」他乖巧地自辩。「咱们得照事物的原样对付，尽力往好里办。他把对想象力的作用夸大了。无论如何，谁也不能说我没站过队。」
他望见那座旧殖民时代的家宅，榆树下白得堂堂正正，怀着绝望中的指望自语：
「我要是被叩选了——对她就有分量。休想让布罗基把我撩起来。我再也不去撞什么了！我在这儿的仗打够了。」
他说得逶强——因为一个男人的勇气有两样来路：他自己的勇气，和他爱的那个女人的勇气。

## XXV / 二十五
===Original===

When he had returned to the college after the summer, he came to his first call on Jean Story with a confident enthusiasm, eager for the first look in her eyes. He had not corresponded with her during the summer. He had not even asked for permission to write, confident though he was that her consent would now be given. He was resolved, as a penance for his first blunder, to hold himself in reserve on every occasion. Bob had written the news, always pressing him to take two weeks off for a visit to the camp, but Dink, despite the tugging at his heart, had stuck to Regan, perhaps a little secretly pleased to show his earnestness.
Now, as he came swinging impatiently toward the glowing white columns under the elms, he realized all at once what was the moving influence in his struggle for growth and independence.
“Here is the horny-handed son of toil,” he said, holding out his hand with a laugh.
She took it, turning over the firm palm with a little curiosity, and looked at him sharply, aware of a great change﻿—they were no longer boy and girl. The vacation had made of the impetuous Dink Stover she had known a new personality that was strange and a little intimidating.
He did not understand at all the sudden dropping of her look, nor the uneasy turning away, nor the quick constraint that came. He was hurt with a sudden sharp sting that he had never known before and the ache of unreasoning jealousy at the bare thought of what might have happened during the summer.
“I’m awfully glad to see you,” she said, but the words sounded formal.
He followed her into the parlor puzzled, irritated by something he did not understand, something that lay underneath everything she said, and seemed to interpose itself as a barrier between them and the old open feeling of camaraderie.
“Mother will be so glad to see you,” she said, after a little moment of awkwardness. “I must call her.”
This maneuver completed his bewilderment, which increased when, Mrs. Story joining them, suddenly the Jean Story of old returned with the same cordiality and the same enthusiasm. She asked a hundred questions, leading him on until he was launched into an account of his summer experiences, the little bits of real life that had brought home to him the seriousness of the world that waited outside.

===Chinese===

夏天过后回到学校，他头一回去拜访琼·斯托里，带着自信的热情，急等她眼里投来的第一眼。整个暑假他没有同她通过信。他连写信的许可都没讨——尽管他笃定，如今她会答应。他拿定主意，算是对头一回失言的补赎，每逢场合都收敛着三分。鲍勃来信报消息，总催他抽两个星期去营地看看，可丁克任凭心口发紧，还是钉在里根身边——或许还暗暗乐意显一显自己的认真。

此刻，他大步流星、按捺不住地朝榆树下那几根亮堂堂的白柱子走去，忽然明白了他这一场场争取成长与独立的仗，背后推动的到底是什么。

「瞧，劳动人家那双老茧手来了。」他伸出手，笑着说。

她握住，带着一点好奇把那结实的巴掌翻过来看，又利利地打量他，察觉出一场大变——他们不再是男孩和女孩了。这个假期，把她认得的那个横冲直接的丁克·斯托弗，造就成了一个陌生而略带威势的新人。

她目光忽然垂下，不安地别开脸，一阵拘谨转瞬即至——这些他全然不懂。他挨了一记从没挨过的尖刺，生疼；单是想到暑假里可能发生过什么，一股不讲理的醋意便煞煞地疼。

「见到你我真高兴。」她说，可这话听着是客套。

他跟着她进客厅，一头雾水，为一桩他不懂的事着恼——那东西垫在她每一句话底下，仿佛横出来一道墙，隔开了他们同旧日那份敞亮的伙伴情谊。

「母亲见到你会很高兴的。」别扭了一小会儿，她说。「我去叫她。」

这一招把他彻底搞糊涂了；而斯托里太太一到，从前的那个琼·斯托里忽然又回来了，还是那份热络、那股兴致——他更糊涂了。她问了一百个问题，引着他讲，直引得他把一暑假的经历摆开来讲——那些真实生活的小碎块，让他搋明白了外头那个等着的世界的分量。

===Original===

He spoke not as the Stover of sophomore year, filled with the enthusiasm of discovery, but with a maturer mind, which had begun to reflect and to reason upon what had come into his knowledge.
Mrs. Story, sunk in the old high-backed armchair near the fire, followed him, too, aware also of the change in the boy, wondering what lay in the mind of her daughter, camped at her knee on the hearth rug, listening so intently and yet clinging to her as though for instinctive protection.
Stover spoke only of outward things; the thoughts that lay beneath, that would have come out so eagerly before the girl, did not appear in the presence of another. As he understood nothing of this sudden introduction of a third into the old confidential relationship, he decided to be more formal than the girl, and rose while still his audience’s attention was held by his account.
“It’s been awfully jolly to see you again,” he said with a perfect manner to Mrs. Stover.
“But you’re going to stay to dinner,” she said, with a little smile.
“Awfully sorry, but I’ve got a dozen things to do,” he said, in the same careful, matter-of-fact tone. “Bob sent word he’d come later.”
Jean Story had not urged him. He went to her with mechanical cheeriness, saying:
“Goodbye. You’re looking splendidly.”
She did not answer, being in one of her silent moods. Mrs. Story went with him towards the door, with a few practical housekeeping questions on the ménage that had just begun. As they were in the anteroom, Jim Hunter entered and, greeting them, passed into the salon.
Stover, deaf to anything else, heard her greeting:
“Why, Jim, I *am* glad to see you.”
Mrs. Story was asking him a question, but he did not hear it. He heard only the echoes of what seemed to him the joy in her laugh.
“If you need any rugs let me know,” said Mrs. Story in patient repetition.
“I beg your pardon,” he stammered. “Yes﻿—yes, of course.”
She looked at him with a little maternal pity, knowing the pang that had gone through him, and for a moment a word was on her lips to enlighten him. But she judged it wiser to be silent, and said:
“Come in for dinner tomorrow night, surely.”
This invitation fitted at once into Stover’s scheme of mislogic. He saw in it a mark of compassion, and of compassion for what reason? Plainly, Jean was interested in someone else, perhaps engaged.

===Chinese===

他讲起来，已不是二年级那个满心发现之狂喜的斯托弗，而是一副更成熟的心智，开始对他所知的东西反劰、推究。

斯托里太太陷在炉边那把旧高背扶手椅里，也一路听着，同样察觉出这孩子的变化，也纳闷女儿心里装着什么——她跪坐在炉边地毯上，偎在母亲膝旁，听得那样专注，却又贴着母亲，像在寻求一种本能的庇护。

斯托弗只谈外头的事；压在底下的那些念头——那些换了从前会急急吐给姑娘听的——当着第三个人，一句也不冒头。这份旧日知心关系里突兀插进来的第三人，他一点不懂，便打定主意要比姑娘更拘礼，趁着听众还被他讲的事吊着，起身告辞。

「再见到你们，真是快活极了。」他对斯托里太太说，礼数周全得无可挑剔。

「可你要留下吃饭的呀。」她带着一点笑说。

「万分抱歉，我手头还有一打事。」他用同样斟酌、就事论事的调子说。「鲍勃捎话他晚些到。」

琼·斯托里没有留他。他挂上一副机械的欢快，走到她跟前，说：

「再见。你气色好极了。」

她没答话——正赶上她那阵沉默的性子。斯托里太太送他往门口走，顺口问了几个实在的持家问题——关于他才开张的那一小摊家务。两人在前厅时，吉姆·亨特进来了，同他们打过招呼，径直进了客厅。

斯托弗别的什么都聋了，只听见她的招呼：

「哎呀吉姆，见到你我*真*高兴。」

斯托里太太正在问他一句什么，他没听见。他只听见回声——在他听来，那是她笑声里的欢喜。

「要添什么地毯，告诉我。」斯托里太太耐着性子又说了一遍。

「您说什么？」他结巴起来。「是——是，当然。」

她带着一点母亲的怜惜看他，知道方才扎过他的是什么，有一瞬，一句点破的话已到唇边。可她搋量还是不说为好，便道：

「明晚一定来吃饭。」

这份邀请立刻嵌进了斯托弗那套错乱的逻辑。他读出了其中的怜悯——可怜悯他什么呢？明摆着：琼心里有了别人，兴许订了约。

===Original===

In ten minutes, to his own lugubrious satisfaction, he had convinced himself it was no other than Jim Hunter. But a short, inquisitive talk with Joe Hungerford, who magnanimously appeared stupidly unconscious of the real motives, reassured him on this point. So, after the hot tempest of jealousy, he began to feel a little resentment at her new, illogical attitude of defensive formality.
Gradually, as he gave no sign of unbending from his own assumption of strict politeness, she began to change, but so gradually that it was not for weeks that he perceived that the old intimate relations had returned. This little interval, however, had brought to him a new understanding. With her he had lost the old impulsiveness. He began to reason and analyze, to think of cause and effect in their relationship. As a consequence the initiative and the authority that had formerly been with her came to him. All at once he perceived, to his utter surprise, what she had felt immediately on his return: that he was the stronger, and that the old, blind, boyish adoration for the girl, who was companion to the stars, had steadied into the responsible and guiding love of a man.
This new supremacy brought with it several differences of opinion. When the question of the football captaincy had come up he did not tell her of his decision, afraid of the ambition he knew was strong in her for his career.
When he saw her the next night, Bob had already brought the news and the reason. She received him with great distance, and for the first time showed a little cruelty in her complete ignoring of his presence.
“You are angry at me,” he said, when finally he had succeeded in finding her alone.
“Yes, I am,” she said point blank. “Why didn’t you tell me what you were planning?”
“I didn’t dare,” he said frankly. “You wouldn’t have approved.”
“Of course I wouldn’t. It was ridiculous. Why shouldn’t you be the captain?”
“There were reasons,” he said seriously. “I should not have had a united team back of me﻿—oh, I know it.”
“Absurd,” she said with some heat. “You should have gone out and made them follow you. Really, it’s too absurd, renouncing everything. Here’s the Junior Prom; everyone says you would have led the class if you’d have stood for it.”
“Yes, and it’s just because a lot of fellows thought they knew my whole game of democracy that I wouldn’t stand for it.”
She grew quite angry.

===Chinese===

十分钟工夫，他愁云惨雾地自我满足了：那别人不是别人，正是吉姆·亨特。可同乔·亨格福德短短一番打探——对方宽宏大量，装出一副蠢然不觉的样子——这一点上他放了心。于是，醋意的热风暴过去之后，他开始对她那套新的、不讲理的防卫式客套生出一点怨气。

渐渐地——见他咬着自己那套严正客套死不松口——她开始变了，变得那么缓，过了好几个星期他才觉出，旧日那份亲密回来了。不过这段小别扭给他带来一层新的懂得。同她在一起，他丢了旧日的冲动。他开始推究、拆解，想他们关系里的因与果。结果，从前握在她手里的主动和权威，转到了他手里。募地，他大吃一惊地看明白了他返校那一刻她便感到的东西：他是更强的那个；而旧日那股对姑娘的、盲目的少年崇拜——对那个与星辰为伴的姑娘——已稳稳落定成一个男人有担当、能引路的爱情。

这份新的上风，随之带来好几场意见相左。橄榄球队长一事摆上台面时，他没把决定告诉他——他知道她对他的前程抱着多强的期望，他怕的就是这个。

第二天晚上见她时，鲍勃已把消息和缘由带到了。她极冷淡地待他，还头一回露出一点狠：全然当他不在场。

「你生我气了。」他终于寻到机会单独见她时说。

「是，生气。」她直截了当地说。「你打算的事，为什么不同我说？」

「不敢。」他坦白说。「你不会赞成的。」

「当然不赞成。荒唐。你凭什么不该当队长？」

「有原因。」他正色说。「我身后不会有上下一心的队伍——哦，这我知道。」

「荒唐。」她带着火气说。「你就该出去，让他们跟着你走。真的，什么都推掉，太荒唐了。就说三年级舞会；人人都说，你要肯出头，带队的就是你。」

「是啊，可正因为一帮家伙自以为摸透了我那套民主把戏，我才不出这个头。」

她真动了气。

===Original===

He had never seen her so stirred.
“Stuff and nonsense. What do you care for their opinion? You should be captain and chairman of the Prom, but you renounce everything﻿—you seem to delight in it. It’s too absurd; it’s ridiculous. It’s like Don Quixote riding around.”
He was hurt at this, and his face showed it.
“It’s something to be able to refuse what others are grabbing for,” he said shortly. “But all you seem to care for is the name.”
The flash that was in his eyes surprised her, and the sudden stern note in his voice that she had never heard before brought her to a quick realization of how she must have wounded him. Her manner changed. She became very gentle, and before he went she said hurriedly:
“Forgive me. You were right, and I was very petty.”
But though he had shown his independence of her ambitions for him, and gained thereby, at heart he had a foolish longing, a senseless dream of winning out on Tap Day﻿—just for the estimation he knew she held of that honor. And, wishing this ardently, he was influenced by it. There were questions about the senior societies that he had not put to himself honestly, as he had in the case of the sophomore. He knew they were way back in his mind, claiming to be met, but, thinking of Jean, he said to himself evasively again and again:
“Suppose there are bad features. I’ve done enough to show my nerve. No one can question that!”
---
With the passing of the winter, and the return to college in the pleasant month of April, the final, all-absorbing Tap Day loomed over them only six weeks away. It was not a particularly agreeable period. The contending ambitions were too keen, too conflicting, for the maintenance of the old spirit of comradeship. The groups again defined themselves, and the “lame ducks,” in the hopes of being noticed, assiduously cultivated the society of what are called “the big men.”
One afternoon in the first week in April, as Dink was returning from the gymnasium, he was suddenly called to from the street. Chris Schley and Troutman, in a two-seated rig, were hallooing:
“Hello there, Dink.”
“Come for a ride.”
“Jump in﻿—join us.”
The two had never been of his intimates, belonging to a New York crowd, who were spoken of for Keys. He hesitated, but as he was free he considered:
“What’s the game?”
“We’re out for a spin towards the shore. Tommy Bain and Stone were going but had to drop out. Come along.

===Chinese===

他从没见她这样激慯过。

这话伤了他，脸上挂了出来。

「别人抢破头的东西能一口回掉，这也是一种本事。」他没好气地说。「可你在乎的，好像只剩名头。」

他眼里那道光惊了她；他嗓音里那声突然的严厉——她从没听过——让她猛然明白自己方才伤他多深。她的态度变了。她变得十分温柔，他走前她急急地说：

「原谅我。你是对的，我太小心眼了。」

可他虽然亮明了自己不受她那份期望的摆布、还因此得了分，心底里却存着一股傻傻的渴望、一场没道理的梦：拍肩日赢一回——就为她对那份荣誉的看重，他清楚她看重。而这一愿既然热切，他便受了它的左右。关于毕业班会社，有几道题他没有像当年对二年级会社那样，诚实地问过自己。他知道它们沉在脑子后头，喊着要他面对；可一想到琼，他就一遍遍躲闪地对自己说：

「就算有坏处吧。我的胆气亮得够多了。谁也挑不出这个！」

---

冬天过去，四月这个可人的月份里回到学校，那最后的、吞没一切的拍肩日已压在头顶，只剩六个星期。这段日子并不怎么好过。相争的野心太利、太撞，旧日那份袍泽之情撑不住了。各圈子重新划清了界限，「瘸腿鸭」们巴望着被看见，勤勤恳恳地巴结起所谓「大人物」的交际来。

四月头一个星期的一个下午，丁克从健身房回来，街上忽然有人喊他。克里斯·施莱和特劳特曼坐在一辆双座马车上，正扬声招呼：

「喂，丁克。」

「兜风去。」

「跳上来——一起。」

这两位从不在他知交之列，属于纽约一帮，据说内定钥匙会。他犹豫了一下，反正闲着，便想：

「去干什么？」

「去海边方向遛一圈。汤米·贝恩和斯通本要去，临时泡了汤。来吧。

===Original===

We might get a shore supper, and toddle back by moonlight.”
“I’ve got to be here by seven,” said Dink doubtfully.
“Oh, well, come on; we’ll make it just a drive.”
“Fine.”
He sprang into the front seat, and they started off in the young, tingling air. Troutman, at the reins, was decidedly unfamiliar with their uses, and, at a fervent plea from Schley, Stover assumed control. Since freshman year the three had been seldom thrown together. He remembered Troutman then as a rather overgrown puppy type, and Schley as a nuisance and a hanger-on. He scanned them now, pleasantly surprised at their transformation. They had come into a clean-cut type, affable, alert, and if there was small mark of character, there was an abundance of good-humor, liveliness, and sociability.
“Well, Dink, old chap,” said Troutman, as he passed along quieter ways, “the fatal day approaches.”
“It does.”
“A lot of seniors are out buying nice brand-new derbies to wear for our benefit.”
“I’ll bet they’re scrapping like cats and dogs,” said Schley.
“They say last year the Bones list wasn’t agreed upon until five minutes before five.”
“The Bones crowd always fight,” said Schley, from the point of view of the opposite camp. “I say, Dink, did you ever think of heeling Keys?”
“No, I’m not a good enough jollier up for that crowd.”
“They say this year Keys is going to shut down on the sporting life and swipe some of the Bones type.”
“Really?” said Stover, in disbelief.
“Sure thing; Tommy Bain has switched.”
“I heard he was packer,” said Stover, not particularly depressed. In the college the rumor had always been that the Keys crowd had what was termed a packer in the junior class, who helped them to pledge some of their selections before Tap Day.
“Sure he is,” said Troutman, with conviction.
“Wish he’d stuck to Bones,” said Schley. “Yours truly would feel more hopeful.”
“Why, you fellows are sure,” said Stover to be polite.
“The deuce we are!”
Schley, tiring of the conversation, was amusing himself from the back seat by well-simulated starts of surprise and a sudden snatching off of his hat to different passersby, exclaiming:
“Why, how *do* you do. I remember meeting you before.”
He did it well, communicated his good spirits to the pedestrians, who took his banter good-naturedly.
All at once his mischievous eye perceived two girls of a rather noticeable type.

We’ll send them to you. Of course we’ve met before. In New York, wasn’t it, at the junior cotillion?”

Stover laughed and drove on. The party had now passed the point of interest. He had no desire for a chance meeting that would require explanations, but he volunteered no advice, not caring to appear prudish in the company of such men of the world.
They were in the open country, the outskirts of New Haven just left behind. For some time Fanny Le Roy had been silent, pressing her hand against her side, frowning. All at once a cry was wrung from her. The carriage stopped. All turned in alarm to where the girl, her teeth compressed, clutching at her side, was lying back against the seat, writhing in agony.
Troutman swore under his breath.
“A devil of a mess!”
They descended hurriedly and laid the girl on the grass, where her agony continued increasingly. Schley and Troutman were whispering apart. The other girl, hysterically bending over her companion, mopped her face with a useless handkerchief, crying:
“She’s got a fit; she’s got a fit!”
“I say it’s appendicitis or gripes,” said Troutman, coming over to Stover. His face was colorless, and he spoke the words nervously. “The deuce of a fix Chris has got us into!”
“Come, we’ve got to get her back,” said Stover, realizing the gravity of the situation. He went abruptly to the girl and spoke with quick authority. “Now stop crying; I want you to get hold of yourself. Here Schley, lend a hand; you and Troutman get her back into the carriage. Do it quickly.”
“What are you going to do?” said Troutman, under his breath.
“Drive her to a doctor, of course.”
“Couldn’t we go and fetch a doctor here?”
“No, we couldn’t!”
With some difficulty they got the suffering girl into the carriage and started back.

No one spoke; the banter had given place to a few muttered words that broke the moaning, delirious tones of the stricken girl.

“Hell!”
They came to the city streets, and Stover drove on hastily, seeking from right to left for a doctor. All at once he drew up at the curb, flung the reins to Troutman, and rushed into a house where he had seen a sign displayed﻿—“Dr. Burke.” He was back almost immediately with the doctor at his heels.
“I say, Dink, look here,” said Schley, plucking him aside, as the doctor hurriedly examined the girl. “This is a deuce of a mess.”
“You bet it is,” said Stover, thinking of the sufferer.
“I say, if this gets out it’ll be a nasty business.”
“What do you mean?”
“If we’re seen driving back with﻿—well, with this bunch!”
“What do you propose?” said Stover sharply.
Troutman joined them.
“See here, leave her with the doctor, I’ll put up all the money that’s necessary, the doctor’ll keep a close mouth! Man alive, you can’t go back this way!”
“Why not?”
“Good Lord, it’ll queer us﻿—we’ll never get over it.”
“Think of the papers,” said Schley, plucking at his glove.
“We can fix it up with the doctor.”
At this moment Dr. Burke joined them, quiet, businesslike, anxious.
“She has all the symptoms of a bad attack of appendicitis. There’s only one thing to do; get her to the hospital at once. I’ll get my hat and join you.”
“Drive to﻿—drive to the hospital?” said Troutman, with a gasp, “right through the whole city, right in the face of everyone?”
“Don’t be a fool, Dink,” said Schley nervously. “We’ll fix up Burke; we’ll give him a hundred to take her and shut up.”
Stover, too, saw the danger and the inevitable scandal. He saw, also, that they were no longer men as he had thought. The thin veneer had disappeared﻿—they were boys, terrified, aghast at a crisis beyond their strength.
“You’re right, it would queer *you*,” he said abruptly. “Clear out﻿—both of you.”
“And you?”
“You’re going to stay?” said Schley. Neither could face his eyes.
“Clear out, I tell you!”

“Hello, where are your friends?”
“They had other engagements,” said Dink grimly. “All ready.”
“I’ve seen your face before,” said Dr. Burke, climbing in.
“I’m Stover.”

「咱们跟医生私了就行。」

正这时伯克医生（Dr. Burke）过来了，沉静，干练，一脸着急。

「症状全齐了，是一场重阑尾炎。只有一个办法：立刻送医院。我拿顶帽子随你们去。」

「送到——送到医院？」特劳特曼倒抽一口气，「横穿全城，当着所有人的面？」

「别犯傻，丁克。」施莱神经质地说。「咱们把伯克摆平；给他一百块，收下人、闭上嘴。」

斯托弗也看见了凶险、看见了躲不掉的丑闻。他也看见了：他们不再是他在心里以为的男子汉。那层薄薄的漆褪尽了——他们是两个吓坏了的孩子，被一场超出他们力气的变故吓得目瞪口呆。

「你们说得对，这事会坏*你们*的。」他猛地说。「滚吧——你们俩。」

「那你呢？」

「你要留下？」施莱说。谁也不敢看他的眼睛。

「滚，听见没有！」

伯克跑下台阶时，诧异地看着斯托弗。

「咦，你那几位朋友呢？」

「另有约会去了。」丁克冷冷地说。「走吧。」

「这张脸我见过。」伯克医生边上车边说。

「我是斯托弗。」

「球队的丁克·斯托弗？」

===Original===

“Yes, Dink Stover of the eleven,” said Stover, his face hardening. “Where do I drive?”
“Do you want to go quietly?” said Dr. Burke, with a look of sympathetic understanding.
From behind the girl, writhing, began to moan:
“Oh, Doctor﻿—Doctor﻿—I can’t stand it﻿—I can’t stand it.”
“What’s the quickest way?” said Stover.
“Chapel Street,” said the doctor.
Stover turned the horses’ heads into the thoroughfare, looking straight ahead, aware soon of the men who saw him in the full light of the day, driving through the streets of New Haven in such inexplicable company. And suddenly at the first turn he came face to face with another carriage in which were Jean Story and her mother.

===Chinese===

「正是，球队的丁克·斯托弗。」斯托弗说，脸沉了下来。「往哪儿赶？」

「想走僻静路吗？」伯克医生说，眼神里是一层体谅。

身后，扭动着的姑娘开始呻吟：

「哦，医生——医生——我受不了了——受不了了。」

「哪条路最快？」斯托弗说。

「礼拜堂街。」医生说。

斯托弗拨转马头驶上大街，目视前方，很快便觉出那一道道目光——大天白日里，他们看见他赶着车穿过纽黑文的街道，身边是这样一车没法解释的同伴。而在第一个路口，他迎面撞上另一辆马车——车上坐着琼·斯托里，和她的母亲。

## XXVI / 二十六
===Original===

When Stover returned to his rooms, it was long after supper.
“Where the deuce have you been?” said Hungerford, looking up from his books.
“Went for a drive, got home late,” said Stover shortly. He filled the companionable pipe, and sank into the low armchair, which Regan had broken for comfort. Something in his abrupt procedure caused Bob Story to look over at Regan with an inquiring raise of his eyebrows.
“Got this psychology yet?” said Hungerford, to try him out.
“No,” said Stover.
“Going to get it?”
“No.”
“The thinghood of a thing is its indefinable somewhatness,” said Hungerford, with another slashing attack on the common enemy, to divert Stover’s attention. “What in the name of peanuts does that stuff mean?”
Dink, refusing to be drawn into conversation, sat enveloped in smoke clouds, his eyes on the clock.
“Hello, I forgot,” said Story presently. “I say, Dink, Troutman and Schley were around here hallooing for you.”
“They were, eh?”
“About an hour ago. Wanted to see you particularly. Said they’d be around again.”
“I see.”
At this moment from below came a bellow:
“Oh, Dink Stover﻿—hello above there!”
“That’s Troutman now,” said Joe Hungerford.
Stover went to the window, flinging it up.
“Well, who’s there?”
“Troutman and Chris Schley. I say, Dink, we’ve got to see you. Come on down.”
“Thanks, I haven’t the slightest desire to see you now or at any other time,” said Stover, who closed the window and resumed his seat, eyeing the clock.
His three friends exchanged troubled glances, and Regan began to whistle to himself, but no questions were asked. At nine o’clock Stover rose and took his hat.
“I’m going out. I may be back late,” he said, and went down the stairs.
“What the devil?” said Hungerford, closing his book.
“He’s in some scrape,” said Regan ruthfully.
“Oh, Lord, and just at this time, too,” said Story.
Stover went rapidly towards the hospital. The girl had been operated on immediately, and the situation was of the utmost seriousness. He had been told to come back at nine. When he arrived he found Muriel Stacey already in the waiting-room, her eyes heavy with frightened weeping. He looked at her curiously. All suggestion of the provoking impertinence and the surface allurement was gone. Under his eyes was nothing but an ignorant boor, stupid and hysterical before the awful fact of death.
“What’s the news?” he asked.
“Oh, Mr. Stover, I don’t know.

「不。」
「一物的物性，即其不可名状的某某性，」亨格福德说，又朝共同的敌人砍了一刀，想岔开斯托弗的注意。「这堆东西到底讲的是什么屁话？」

三位朋友交换着不安的眼色；里甘吹起口哨，可谁也没问。九点整，斯托弗起身拿帽。

斯托弗快步朝医院去。姑娘立刻做了手术，局势凶险到极点。人家叫九点再来。他到时，穆里尔·斯塔西（Muriel Stacey）已坐在候诊室，一双哭肿的眼睛沉甸甸的。他好奇地看她。那副招人恼的无礼、表面的风情，全没了。他眼下只剩一个无知的粗人，在死亡这件可怕的事实面前，又蠢又歇斯底里。
「有消息吗？」他问。
「哦，斯托弗先生，我不知道。

===Original===

I can’t get anything out of them,” the woman said wildly. “Oh, do you think she’s going to die?”
“Of course not,” he said gruffly. “See here, where’s her family?”
“I don’t know.”
“Don’t they live here?”
“They’re in Ohio somewhere, I think. I don’t know. Ask the doctor, won’t you, Mr. Stover? He’ll tell you something.”
He left her, and, making inquiries, was met by a young intern, immaculate and alert, who was quite communicative to Dink Stover of the Yale eleven.
“She’s had a bad case of it; appendix had already burst. You got her here just in time.”
“What’s the outlook?”
“Can’t tell. She came out of the anaesthetic all right.” He went into a technical discussion of the dangers of blood poisoning, concluding: “Still, I should say her chances were good. It depends a good deal on the resistance. However, I think your friend’s family ought to be notified.”
Stover did not notice the “your friend,” nor the look which the doctor gave him.
“She’s here alone as far as I can find out,” he said. “Poor little devil. I’ll call round about midnight.”
“No need,” said the doctor briskly, “nothing’ll develop before tomorrow.”
Stover sent the waiting girl home somewhat tranquilized, and, finding a florist’s shop open, left an order to be sent in to the patient the first thing in the morning. Then, thoroughly exhausted by his sudden contact with all the nervous fates of the hospital, he walked home and heavily to bed.
The next morning as he went to his eating-joint with Regan and Hungerford, the newsboy, who had his papers ready, gave them to him with a hesitating look. All at once Joe Hungerford swore mightily.
“Now what’s wrong, Joe?” said Regan in surprise.
“Nothing,” said Hungerford hastily, but almost immediately he stopped, and said in a jerky, worried way: “Say, here’s the devil to pay, Dink. I suppose you ought to know about it. Damn the papers.”
With his finger he indicated a space on the front page of the New York newspaper he was reading. Stover took it, reading it seriously. It was only a paragraph, but it rose from the page as though it were stamped in scarlet.

> Dink Stover’s Lark

Ends Seriously.

===Chinese===

「他们什么都不肯说，」女人发疯似的说。「哦，您说，她会死吗？」

「不知道。」
「不住在这儿？」
「我记得是在俄亥俄什么地方。我不知道。去问问大夫好不好，斯托弗先生？他会说个信儿的。」
他撂下她，一路打听，迎上一位年轻的住院医——衣冠楚楚、手脚麻利——对耶鲁十一人队的丁克·斯托弗相当健谈。
「她的病症不轻；阑尾已经穿了。你送来得正是时候。」
「前景怎样？」
「说不好。麻醉醒得顺利。」他进而把血毒的危险讲了些门道，末了说：「不过，我看她的机会不小。多半看抵抗力。另外，我看你朋友家里该通知一声。」
斯托弗没留意「你朋友」三个字，也没留意医生看他的那一眼。
「据我所知，她在这儿孤身一人，」他说。「可怜的小东西。我半夜再过来。」
「不必，」医生干脆地说，「明天之前出不了事。」
斯托弗把候着的姑娘劝回家——她安了些神——又寻到一家还开着的花店，留了订单，明早第一件事送进病房。随后，被医院里种种紧绷的神经历数了一遍，他精疲力竭，走回家，沉沉睡去。
第二天早晨，他同里甘和亨格福德往饭庄走，报童把备好的报纸递给他，眼神迟疑。忽然乔·亨格福德大骂一声。
「怎么了，乔？」里甘吃惊地说。
「没什么，」亨格福德急忙说；可几乎立刻又站住，结结巴巴、心事重重地说：「喂，出大乱子了，丁克。我看你该知道。该死的报纸。」
他用手指点着他正读着的那份纽约报纸头版的一块。斯托弗接过来，正色读。不过一小段，却像朱红烙上去的一样从版面上立起来。

> 丁克·斯托弗的艳游

> 闹出大事
